classical-mechanics.bigb
= Classical mechanics
{wiki}

<Classical mechanics> describes the motion of bodies by assigning <positions> and <velocities> and specifying how their interactions change those quantities. A <point mass> is the simplest model: its size and internal structure are ignored, leaving a <mass> and a moving <position>. A <rigid body> retains orientation and <rotational kinetic energy>. Choosing a model means deciding which features affect the question being asked; a sliding block may be treated as a <point mass>, whereas predicting its <rolling without slipping> requires its <moment of inertia>.

Three complementary questions organize the subject. <Newton's second law> asks how the net <force> determines the instantaneous <acceleration>. The <work-energy theorem> asks how that <force>, acting through a <displacement>, changes <kinetic energy>. <Conservation of mechanical energy> asks which combinations of <position> and <velocity> are accessible when the interactions admit a time-independent <potential energy> and no other <force> supplies net <power>. These descriptions are compatible, but answer different questions: an <energy> calculation may find a maximum <speed> without first solving for <position> at every <time>.

The <harmonic oscillator> below makes this unity visible. A restoring <force> produces a repeating <position> trace; the corresponding <phase plane> has a closed <ellipse>; <kinetic energy> and <potential energy> exchange while their sum stays fixed. <Lagrangian mechanics> and <Hamiltonian mechanics> provide further ways to organize the same dynamics, especially when coordinates or constraints make individual <forces> awkward to track.

The scope of <classical mechanics> also matters. <Newtonian mechanics> approximates motion at <speeds> small compared with the <speed of light>; <special relativity> changes that description when this approximation fails. <Quantum mechanics> is needed for phenomena for which a classical assignment of simultaneous <position> and <momentum> is inadequate. These limitations do not prevent <classical mechanics> from being a useful predictive model within its regime. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-01sc-classical-mechanics-fall-2016/mit8_01scs22_chapter1.pdf[MIT mechanics, Chapter 1: scope and limitations]{ref}

= Variable-mass system
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{title2=$m\dot{\mathbf v}=\mathbf F+(\mathbf u-\mathbf v)\dot m$}
{wiki}

A body exchanging <mass> with material of inertial <velocity> $\mathbf u$ satisfies the displayed <momentum conservation> law for one stream, with signed $\dot m$ positive for entry and negative for exit. The <force> $\mathbf F$ excludes the incoming or outgoing momentum flux. A short time interval gives $d(m\mathbf v)/dt=\mathbf F+\mathbf u\dot m$, proving the formula. For stationary incoming matter, $d(m\mathbf v)/dt=\mathbf F$; for matter leaving at the body's own <velocity>, $m\dot{\mathbf v}=\mathbf F$. The <rocket equation> and accreting-drop motion are applications.

= Accreting raindrop with quadratic drag
{parent=Variable-mass system}
{title2=$\dot a=cv/4,\quad\dot v=g-3(c+k)v^2/(4a)$}

For a spherical drop of constant <mass density> $\rho$, radius $a>0$ and downward <velocity> $v>0$, suppose its volume grows at rate $c\pi a^2v$ by collecting stationary mist and its upward <quadratic drag> is $k\rho\pi a^2v^2$, with $c,k>0$. The drop <mass> is $4\pi\rho a^3/3$. Its incoming <mass accretion rate> and the <variable-mass system> momentum law give the displayed equations. The accreted matter must be accelerated from rest, producing the additional $c$ term alongside drag.

= Speed-radius attractor of an accreting raindrop
{parent=Accreting raindrop with quadratic drag}
{title2=$v^2/a\longrightarrow8g/(7c+6k),\quad\dot v\longrightarrow cg/(7c+6k)$}

For the <accreting raindrop with quadratic drag>, set $q=v^2/a$ and $B=7c/8+3k/4$. Its exact equation is $\dot q=2(v/a)(g-Bq)$. Dividing by $\dot a=cv/4$ gives $q(a)=g/B+(q(a_0)-g/B)(a_0/a)^{7+6k/c}$. Thus $q$ stays between its positive initial value and positive equilibrium. Since $d\sqrt a/dt=c\sqrt q/8$, the radius grows without finite-time divergence and tends to infinity. The power correction then vanishes, proving both limits. Positive drag makes the limiting <acceleration> strictly less than $g/7$.

= Inertia
{parent=Classical mechanics}

<Inertia> is the resistance of a body's motion to change under applied force. For a particle, <Newton's second law> relates force to the rate of change of momentum; at constant mass it is $F=ma$. In an overdamped approximation the inertial acceleration term is small compared with drag over the time scales considered.

= Mechanical configuration space
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{title2=$Q$}

= Configuration space
{synonym}

The <configuration space> of a mechanical system is the space of its allowed positions or generalized coordinates. Its tangent bundle describes configurations and velocities, and its cotangent bundle describes configurations and conjugate momenta. For smooth holonomic constraints it is a manifold on which geometric Lagrangian and Hamiltonian descriptions can be built. Singular constraints or symmetry quotients can require strata rather than one global smooth manifold.

= Relational shape space
{parent=Classical mechanics}

The <relational shape space> of a particle configuration is obtained by removing collective translations and rotations; here scale is retained. For generic collision-free, noncollinear configurations of $N\ge3$ labelled particles in three dimensions, the resulting configuration dimension is $3N-6$. Collinear configurations have nontrivial rotational stabilizers and require singular strata. Removing position and orientation does not automatically remove conserved angular momentum or its centrifugal effect; a dynamically faithful reduction must retain the appropriate momentum-level data.

= Simple pendulum
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{title2=$\ddot\theta+(g/\ell)\sin\theta=0$}

A point mass constrained to a fixed-length massless arm under uniform gravitational <acceleration> is a simple pendulum. With no drag its angular equation is $\ddot\theta+(g/\ell)\sin\theta=0$. The <small-angle approximation> yields <simple harmonic motion>, whereas the full restoring force is nonlinear. Adding drag gives a <damped pendulum>.

For an ideal point mass $m$ swinging in a vertical plane, the <torque> about the pivot is $-mg\ell\sin\theta$. Dividing by its <moment of inertia> $m\ell^2$ gives the displayed equation. Both the inertia and the gravitational <torque> scale with $m$, explaining why the point mass cancels. This derivation and the small-amplitude period are given in https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/15-4-pendulums[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics, pendulums].

Released from rest at a small angular <amplitude> $\theta_0$, the linearized solution is $\theta(t)\simeq\theta_0\cos(\sqrt{g/\ell}\,t)$, with <oscillation period> $T_0=2\pi\sqrt{\ell/g}$. For $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, lengths one and four metres give $T_0\approx2.0061\,\mathrm s$ and $4.0121\,\mathrm s$. This same scaling follows from <dimensional analysis>; the dynamical calculation supplies the coefficient $2\pi$.

Finite angular <amplitude> changes the exact ideal period. <Conservation of energy> gives $\dot\theta^2=(2g/\ell)(\cos\theta-\cos\theta_0)$ for release from rest. Integrating a quarter oscillation and substituting $\sin(\theta/2)=\sin(\theta_0/2)\sin\phi$ gives
$$
T(\theta_0)=4\sqrt{\frac\ell g}\,K\!\left(\sin\frac{\theta_0}2\right),\qquad
\frac{T(\theta_0)}{T_0}=\frac2\pi\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{d\phi}{\sqrt{1-\sin^2(\theta_0/2)\sin^2\phi}},\quad 0<\theta_0<\pi.
$$
Here $K$ is the <complete elliptic integral of the first kind>. The <small-angle approximation> gives a ratio tending to one; at $10^\circ,60^\circ,90^\circ$, the ratios are approximately $1.00191,1.07318,1.18034$. Thus almost constant period at small <amplitude> has a quantitative limit. As $\theta_0\to\pi$, the period diverges, connecting to the <pendulum separatrix approach takes infinite time>.

\Image[pendulum-period-scaling.png]
{title=Length scaling and finite-amplitude period corrections for an ideal simple pendulum}

These predictions assume the specified ideal system. Drag changes the <mechanical energy>; a massive support changes the <moment of inertia>. The distinction is useful in experiments: a change in period can test an approximation rather than merely indicate an arithmetic mistake.

= Conical pendulum
{parent=Simple pendulum}
{wiki}

A <conical pendulum> is a pendulum whose bob executes uniform horizontal <circular motion> beneath a fixed pivot, with its string at a constant <angle> $\theta$ to the downward vertical. The string sweeps a <right circular conical surface>; the bob follows its circular rim, without any physical cone supporting it. The ideal model has a <point mass> $m>0$, a taut, massless, inextensible string of length $\ell>0$, uniform <gravitational acceleration> of magnitude $g>0$, and negligible <drag force> and pivot <friction>. A nonzero circular motion requires $0<\theta<\pi/2$. This geometry and the corresponding <forces> are described in https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/301/lectures/node88.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, The conical pendulum, equations 262–267].

\Image[conical-pendulum-geometry.png]
{title=Conical pendulum geometry, horizontal orbit, and the two forces on the bob}

The <right triangle> formed by the pivot, the orbit centre, and the bob gives $r=\ell\sin\theta$ and $h=\ell\cos\theta$. Here $r$ is the orbit radius and $h$ is the vertical drop from the pivot to the bob's horizontal plane, not the bob's height above a table. Write $T$ for <tension>, $P$ for the <oscillation period> of one revolution, and $\omega=2\pi/P$ for the <angular speed>; then the bob's <speed> is $v=\omega r$. A side view compresses the horizontal circle to a line segment; the top view reveals the circle and its tangent <velocity>.

The two actual <forces> on the bob are <tension> toward the pivot and <gravitational weight> vertically downward. The vertical <acceleration> is zero, while the horizontal <centripetal acceleration> is $v^2/r=\omega^2r$. Thus <Newton's second law> gives
$$
T\cos\theta=mg,\qquad T\sin\theta=m\omega^2r=\frac{mv^2}{r}.
$$
The inward net <force> is the horizontal component of <tension>, rather than an extra interaction to add to these two <forces>. Constant <speed> still requires <acceleration> because the <velocity> changes direction. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/6-3-centripetal-force[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics 1, §6.3: centripetal acceleration and net force]{ref}

Dividing the two component equations and using $r=\ell\sin\theta$ gives the useful collection of predictions
$$
\omega^2\cos\theta=\frac g\ell,\qquad T=\frac{mg}{\cos\theta},\qquad v^2=g\ell\frac{\sin^2\theta}{\cos\theta},\qquad P=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{\ell\cos\theta}{g}}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac hg}.
$$
If $\phi$ is the azimuthal <angle>, then $\dot\phi=\pm\omega$; either direction of rotation satisfies $\dot\phi^2\cos\theta=g/\ell$. The <mass> cancels from the <angular speed> and <oscillation period>, but not from the <tension>. The motion is a <relative equilibrium>: in a <rotating frame> following the bob at <angular speed> $\omega$, its <position> is fixed. This is a concrete way to see how rotational <symmetry> can turn an apparently moving solution into a stationary one.

= Rotating bob experiment for a conical pendulum
{parent=Conical pendulum}

The motivating observation is a bob going around at almost constant height while its string traces a cone. The University of Iowa's demonstration places a camera above a short pendulum to reveal this motion. https://instructional-resources.physics.uiowa.edu/1d5025-conical-pendulum[University of Iowa, demonstration 1D50.25: short pendulum and overhead camera]{ref} The following proposed measurement procedure develops that observation into a test of the <conical pendulum> model; it is not a report of collected data.

Suspend a compact bob from a rigid, fixed support using a light string, and measure $\ell$ from the pivot to the bob's centre. Put a stationary marker beside the orbit as a timing reference. Displace the bob sideways and give it a horizontal push tangent to the intended circle, then let go. For a chosen starting <angle> $\theta$, the predicted initial <speed> is $v=\sqrt{g\ell\sin^2\theta/\cos\theta}$. Adjust the launch between trials until a short observation interval has nearly constant orbit radius and height. Keep the pivot fixed while recording: circling the support by hand changes the problem, and a maintained drive would introduce another <force>.

Record from above with the camera looking approximately along the vertical axis. Check that the bob's centre follows a circle and that successive returns to the marker take nearly equal times. A separate side recording checks whether the bob's height stays constant. A markedly elliptical orbit or visibly rising and falling bob is not the uniform <circular motion> assumed by the <conical pendulum> equations. Measure the orbit diameter with a scale at the orbit's height; in a level side view, use the two extreme horizontal positions, not one arbitrary frame. Halve the diameter to obtain $r$, or measure the vertical drop $h$ directly in a calibrated side view. An oblique camera view and a scale at a different depth distort apparent lengths, so arrange the geometry before interpreting the images.

Time $N$ complete returns to the same marker in the same direction and estimate $P=\Delta t/N$. Counting ten revolutions, when the orbit stays sufficiently steady, spreads an endpoint timing error over ten periods. Repeat the timing and compare the first and last few revolutions to detect drift; using a longer interval helps only if the motion remains close to uniform. From independently measured geometry, compute $\sin\theta=r/\ell$ or $\cos\theta=h/\ell$, and check $r^2+h^2\simeq\ell^2$ if both were measured. Then compare the measured <oscillation period> with $P^2=4\pi^2h/g$. MIT's 8.01X experimental assignment explicitly asks students to compare the <conical pendulum>'s period, length, and orbit radius with theory. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-01x-physics-i-classical-mechanics-with-an-experimental-focus-fall-2002/a056f6a3e071767d4f7620bca6a59d0c_expt_vs_problem.pdf[MIT 8.01X, Fall 2002, Vibrating Systems experimental problem, question 8]{ref}

Repeat at several launch radii with the same $\ell$. Larger $r$ means smaller $h=\sqrt{\ell^2-r^2}$, so the ideal <oscillation period> decreases. A plot of measured $P^2$ against independently measured $h$ should follow a straight line through the origin with slope $4\pi^2/g$; conversely, $g=4\pi^2h/P^2$ estimates <gravitational acceleration>. Do not infer $h$ from the timing equation and then use that same equation as a test. Compare discrepancies with the spread of repeated timings and the resolution of the length measurements. String stretch, a moving pivot, and appreciable bob size challenge the stated geometry; <drag force> and pivot <friction> challenge the assumption of undriven uniform motion. Observe a short interval and record any shrinking or drifting orbit rather than silently treating it as an exact <conical pendulum>.

For an illustrative calculation, assume $\ell=1.00\,\mathrm m$, $m=0.050\,\mathrm{kg}$, $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, and $\theta=40^\circ$. The model gives $r\approx0.643\,\mathrm m$, $h\approx0.766\,\mathrm m$, $P\approx1.756\,\mathrm s$, $v\approx2.300\,\mathrm{m/s}$, and $T\approx0.640\,\mathrm N$. Its vertical <tension> component is $0.4905\,\mathrm N$, equal to the <gravitational weight>; its inward component is approximately $0.412\,\mathrm N$. These are predictions from chosen parameters, not measured data or measurements of local $g$. The next figure uses the same stipulated $\ell$ and $g$, including illustrative <angles> $15^\circ$, $40^\circ$, and $60^\circ$.

= Period and small-angle limit of a conical pendulum
{parent=Conical pendulum}
{title2=$P/P_0=\sqrt{\cos\theta},\quad P_0=2\pi\sqrt{\ell/g}$}

\Image[conical-pendulum-period.png]
{title=Ideal conical pendulum period decreases with cone angle and its square is proportional to the vertical drop}

For fixed $\ell$ and $g$, the exact <conical pendulum> <oscillation period> satisfies $P/P_0=\sqrt{\cos\theta}$. In the <small-angle approximation>, with $\theta$ in <radians>,
$$
\frac{P}{P_0}=1-\frac{\theta^2}{4}+O(\theta^4),\qquad P_0=2\pi\sqrt{\frac\ell g}.
$$
Thus the limiting revolution period equals the small-amplitude <oscillation period> of a planar <simple pendulum>. This equality has a geometric explanation: to first order, $x(t)=r\cos(\omega t)$ and $y(t)=r\sin(\omega t)$ are perpendicular <simple harmonic motions> of equal <amplitude>, a quarter period apart, with $\omega\to\sqrt{g/\ell}$. At finite <angle>, however, the <conical pendulum>'s <oscillation period> decreases with $\theta$, whereas a planar <simple pendulum>'s <oscillation period> increases with its angular <amplitude>.

The endpoint $\theta=0$ itself is a stationary bob with $r=0$ and no observable revolution; $P_0$ is a limit of nonzero circular motions, not a measured revolution period at rest. For fixed $\ell$, nonzero <conical pendulum> motion also requires $\omega>\sqrt{g/\ell}$, because $\cos\theta=g/(\ell\omega^2)<1$. At the other endpoint, $\theta\to\pi/2$ gives $h\to0$, $P\to0$, and unbounded <tension> and <speed>. These mathematical limits explain why the ideal string cannot sustain a horizontal orbit at finite <tension>; a real experiment ceases to satisfy the ideal assumptions before that limit.

= Damped pendulum
{parent=Simple pendulum}
{title2=$\ddot\theta+c\dot\theta+\sin\theta=0$}

A <damped pendulum> is a <simple pendulum> whose <mechanical energy> is removed by a resisting <torque>. Here the model is undriven, with a fixed pivot, a <point mass> $m>0$ at distance $\ell>0$, uniform <gravitational acceleration> $g>0$, and resisting <torque> $-b\,d\theta/dt$ with constant $b>0$. The <angle> $\theta$ is measured from the downward vertical in <radians>. An ideal viscous rotary <dashpot> realizes this torque law; tangential <linear drag> $-\alpha v$ on the bob gives the same equation with $b=\alpha\ell^2$. The <moment of inertia> is $m\ell^2$, so
$$
m\ell^2\frac{d^2\theta}{dt^2}+b\frac{d\theta}{dt}+mg\ell\sin\theta=0,\qquad
\omega_0=\sqrt{\frac g\ell},\quad \gamma=\frac{b}{m\ell^2},\quad \tau=\omega_0t,\quad c=\frac\gamma{\omega_0}>0.
$$
Here $t$ is physical <time>, $\omega_0$ is the undamped small-amplitude <angular frequency>, and $\gamma$ has units of inverse <time>. In the normalized equation $\ddot\theta+c\dot\theta+\sin\theta=0$, dots mean differentiation with respect to $\tau$, not $t$. The parameter $c$ is dimensionless. This separates the chosen damping law from the <small-angle approximation>: retaining $\sin\theta$ allows finite <angles> even when the resisting <torque> is linear in <angular velocity>.

\Image[damped-pendulum-decay-observation.png]
{title=Proposed viscous-damper experiment and illustrative small-angle decay: angle versus time and successive peak ratios, not measured data}

To motivate the model, release a pendulum without a push and record how its swing shrinks. A published example by Gintautas and Hubler uses a rotary sensor built from a computer mouse to measure the frequency and damping coefficient of a <physical pendulum>. https://arxiv.org/abs/0901.4319[Gintautas and Hubler, A simple, low-cost, data-logging pendulum built from a computer mouse, 2009]{ref} The following is a proposed experiment with a chosen viscous rotary <dashpot>; the plotted observations are predictions, not a reproduction of their measurements.

Mount a compact bob on a light rigid rod with a fixed pivot, couple the pivot to an adjustable viscous rotary <dashpot>, and measure $\ell$ from the pivot to the bob's centre. First record decay with the <dashpot> disconnected, then repeat with it connected at several settings; this establishes how much decay is already due to other losses. Keep the bob, rod, camera, and release <angle> unchanged between these trials. The simple model requires rod and damper-rotor <moments of inertia> to be negligible compared with $m\ell^2$. Otherwise measure the total <moment of inertia> $I$ and the distance $d$ to the assembly's centre of mass: the <physical pendulum> equation becomes $I\,d^2\theta/dt^2+b\,d\theta/dt+Mgd\sin\theta=0$, where $M$ is the total moving <mass>. A damper that also exerts an elastic restoring <torque> would require another term.

Place a camera perpendicular to the swing plane, include the pivot and bob centre in every frame, and determine their coordinates relative to the resting vertical. For horizontal and downward separations $x,y$, use $\theta=\operatorname{atan2}(x,y)$; record the frame timestamps rather than assuming a frame rate. Release from rest at about $10^\circ$, record through several complete swings, and repeat the release. For every same-side turning point, tabulate its <time> $t_n$ and angular <amplitude> $A_n=|\theta(t_n)|$. Measure the <oscillation period> from differences $t_{n+1}-t_n$; comparing several periods exposes drift. Interpolating around each observed extremum reduces frame discretization error. Check that late peaks still exceed the angular resolution; taking the <natural logarithm> of tiny, uncertain peaks amplifies their relative errors.

In the <small-angle approximation>, the <damped pendulum> becomes a <damped harmonic oscillator>. For $0<\gamma<2\omega_0$ and release from rest at $\theta_0$, its exact linear-model solution is
$$
\theta_{\mathrm{lin}}(t)=\theta_0e^{-\gamma t/2}\left(\cos\omega_dt+\frac{\gamma}{2\omega_d}\sin\omega_dt\right),\qquad
\omega_d=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\frac{\gamma^2}{4}},\qquad T_d=\frac{2\pi}{\omega_d}.
$$
The decaying sinusoidal envelope has coefficient $|\theta_0|\omega_0/\omega_d$; because release is from rest, its successive same-side extrema occur at $t_n=nT_d$ and have $A_n=|\theta_0|e^{-\gamma nT_d/2}$. Thus the envelope and the sequence of actual peak heights are different descriptions of the same trace. The linear damping regimes and this solution are derived in https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/336k/Newton/node19.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Damped Oscillatory Motion, equations 82–93].

The experiment tests two predictions independently: nearly equal same-side peak spacing and a straight line when plotting $\ln(A_n/A_0)$ against $t_n-t_0$. Its predicted slope is $-\gamma/2$, giving
$$
\gamma=\frac{2}{t_{n+1}-t_n}\ln\frac{A_n}{A_{n+1}},\qquad b=m\ell^2\gamma,\qquad
\omega_d^2+\frac{\gamma^2}{4}=\frac g\ell.
$$
Use several peaks and repeated trials rather than a single ratio, and compare the frequency identity with independently measured $\ell$ and $g$. For the illustrative choices $\ell=1.00\,\mathrm m$, $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, $c=0.20$, and $\theta_0=10^\circ$, the linear model gives $\gamma\approx0.6264\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, $T_d\approx2.0162\,\mathrm s$, and $A_{n+1}/A_n\approx0.5318$. The first two positive peaks after release are therefore approximately $5.318^\circ$ and $2.828^\circ$. The undamped small-amplitude <oscillation period> is about $2.0061\,\mathrm s$: substantial loss per swing need not imply a large change in period.

Repeat at a larger release <angle> to distinguish two limitations. The relative error in replacing $\sin\theta$ by $\theta$ is about $\theta^2/6$ for small <angles>: about $0.51\%$ at $10^\circ$, but about $17.3\%$ at $60^\circ$ when compared with $\theta$. Finite-amplitude swing spacing can therefore change as the swing shrinks even if $b$ is constant. Separately, <friction> at the pivot or <quadratic drag> can invalidate the assumed linear resisting <torque>; exponential peak decay should be checked, not imposed on all recorded motion. Peters discusses why real pendulum damping can require nonlinear terms. https://arxiv.org/abs/physics/0306081[Randall D. Peters, Nonlinear Damping of the 'Linear' Pendulum, 2003]{ref} Repeatability, residual pivot motion, changes in damper <temperature>, and finite camera resolution are useful checks on this proposed measurement.

The normalized <equilibrium points> are $(\theta,\dot\theta)=(k\pi,0)$. Even multiples of $\pi$ are attracting: their <linearization> has <eigenvalues> $(-c\pm\sqrt{c^2-4})/2$, giving a <stable focus> for $0<c<2$, repeated negative <eigenvalues> at $c=2$, and a <stable node> for $c>2$. These are the underdamped, critically damped, and overdamped small-displacement regimes. Odd multiples of $\pi$ have <eigenvalues> $(-c\pm\sqrt{c^2+4})/2$ of opposite signs and are <saddle equilibria>. The <energy dissipation of a damped pendulum> explains why trajectories in the <phase portrait> spiral inward near a <stable focus> instead of closing into the recurring orbit of an undamped <simple pendulum>.

= Energy dissipation of a damped pendulum
{parent=Damped pendulum}
{title2=$\dot E=-c\dot\theta^2$}

For the normalized <damped pendulum>, the sum of <kinetic energy> and gravitational <potential energy>, divided by $mg\ell$, is
$$
E(\tau)=\frac12\dot\theta^2+1-\cos\theta,\qquad
\dot E=\dot\theta(\ddot\theta+\sin\theta)=-c\dot\theta^2\leq0.
$$
The <chain rule> and the full nonlinear equation give this identity exactly, without a <small-angle approximation>. Here $\dot\theta=d\theta/d\tau$ as in <damped pendulum>. In physical units the <mechanical energy> and the <power> of the resisting <torque> are
$$
\mathcal E(t)=\frac12m\ell^2\left(\frac{d\theta}{dt}\right)^2+mg\ell(1-\cos\theta),\qquad
\frac{d\mathcal E}{dt}=-b\left(\frac{d\theta}{dt}\right)^2.
$$
The pendulum's lost <mechanical energy> is transferred to the damper and surroundings; it does not violate <conservation of energy>. In an ideal viscous <dashpot> this loss is <frictional heating>. The exact cumulative balance is particularly useful for checking a numerical solution:
$$
Q(\tau)=c\int_0^\tau\dot\theta(s)^2\,ds,\qquad E(\tau)+Q(\tau)=E(0).
$$

\Image[damped-pendulum-nonlinear-energy.png]
{title=Illustrative nonlinear and linear damped pendulum solutions at 10 and 60 degrees, with exact mechanical energy transfer and loss; no measured data}

The figure uses $c=0.20$ and release from rest. Over $0\leq\tau\leq25$, the largest angular discrepancy between the nonlinear solution and the <damped harmonic oscillator> solution is approximately $0.000702\,\mathrm{rad}$ ($0.0402^\circ$) for a $10^\circ$ release, but $0.14995\,\mathrm{rad}$ ($8.592^\circ$) for a $60^\circ$ release. Their nonlinear initial energies are $1-\cos10^\circ\approx0.015192$ and $1-\cos60^\circ=0.5$, respectively. At $\tau=25$ the remaining fractions are approximately $0.0064080$ and $0.0063757$. These are illustrative calculations from specified initial conditions, not observations. The generator uses a <Runge-Kutta method> and integrates $Q$ alongside the motion; halving its step and independently integrating $c\dot\theta^2$ checks the result.

For $\ell=1\,\mathrm m$ and $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, the first three nonlinear same-side swing intervals after a $60^\circ$ release are approximately $2.1008$, $2.0378$, and $2.0221\,\mathrm s$. They approach the linear-model <oscillation period> $2.0162\,\mathrm s$ as the <amplitude> shrinks. After a $10^\circ$ release the corresponding intervals are already about $2.0185$, $2.0168$, and $2.0164\,\mathrm s$. This makes the experiment's period drift a quantitative test of the <small-angle approximation>, rather than an automatic reason to change the damping coefficient.

At a turning point, $\dot\theta=0$ and $\dot E=0$ even though the swing is losing <mechanical energy> over the cycle. Its <potential energy> there is $E_n=1-\cos A_n$, so measured turning <angles> give successive energies without estimating <angular velocity>. In the proposed decay experiment, multiply these values by $mg\ell$ to obtain energies in <joules>, then subtract adjacent same-side energies to estimate the loss over each full swing. Only for small <amplitudes> is $E_n\simeq A_n^2/2$. In the linear underdamped model, same-side peak energies consequently satisfy $E_{n+1}/E_n=e^{-\gamma T_d}$, the square of the angular <amplitude> ratio. For the illustrative parameters above this energy ratio is approximately $0.2828$. Between turning points, <kinetic energy> and <potential energy> continue to exchange while their sum decreases; the energy slope vanishes briefly at each extremum and is negative whenever the bob moves.

It is therefore inaccurate to replace the exact identity by $\dot E=-cE$. Even for the linear model, $E_{\mathrm{lin}}=(\dot\theta^2+\theta^2)/2$ has $\dot E_{\mathrm{lin}}=-c\dot\theta^2$. For weak damping and small <angles>, cycle averaging gives $\langle\dot\theta^2\rangle\simeq\langle E_{\mathrm{lin}}\rangle$, so the averaged energy approximately follows $e^{-c\tau}$ while angular <amplitude> follows $e^{-c\tau/2}$. This <exponential decay> is an approximation to the energy trace; the same-side peak ratio is exact within the linear model. The figure distinguishes the actual energy curves from this averaging approximation.

The energy identity also gives a global conclusion without solving the nonlinear equation. If $E$ were constant on a positive-length interval, then $\dot\theta$ would vanish throughout it; the equation would force $\sin\theta=0$, making the entire trajectory an <equilibrium>. Every nonstationary trajectory therefore loses <mechanical energy> over every positive-length interval, even though its instantaneous <derivative> can vanish at a turning point. A nonconstant periodic motion would have to return to its initial <mechanical energy>, so it is impossible for this undriven <damped pendulum>. Oscillations near a <stable focus> are successive, shrinking swings, not a repeating cycle. Moreover, if $E(0)<2$, the <angle> cannot reach an upright <equilibrium>, whose <potential energy> is $2$; such motion stays within its original potential well. This is the usefulness of the energy description: one scalar balance controls both the observed decay and which nonlinear motions remain accessible.

= Eisenhart-Duval lift
{c}
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{title2=$g=d\mathbf x^2+2dt(du-2\mathbf A\cdot d\mathbf x-Udt)$}

= Eisenhart lift
{c}
{synonym}

= Eisenhart-Duval metric lift
{c}
{synonym}

A mechanical Lagrangian quadratic in velocities can be represented by <geodesics> of a <Lorentzian metric> with an extra null coordinate. For $L=\tfrac12|\dot{\mathbf x}|^2-2\mathbf A\cdot\dot{\mathbf x}-U$, use $g=d\mathbf x^2+2dt(du-2\mathbf A\cdot d\mathbf x-Udt)$. The null <Killing vector field> $\partial_u$ gives the conserved momentum $p_u=t\prime$. Fixing $p_u=1$ projects the <geodesic equation> to the mechanical <Euler-Lagrange equations>. This is useful for relating mechanical forces to spacetime geometry.

= Gauge transformations of an Eisenhart-Duval lift
{parent=Eisenhart-Duval lift}
{title2=$\mathbf A\mapsto\mathbf A+\nabla\chi,\quad u\mapsto u+2\chi$}

A time-independent <gauge transformation> $\mathbf A\mapsto\mathbf A+\nabla\chi$ in an <Eisenhart-Duval lift> is the coordinate change $u\mapsto u+2\chi$. It preserves the metric one-form $du-2\mathbf A\cdot d\mathbf x-Udt$ and adds only $-2d\chi/dt$ to the reduced Lagrangian.

= Null Kaluza-Klein reduction of a stationary force
{parent=Eisenhart-Duval lift}
{title2=$p_u=t\prime\ne0$}

For an <Eisenhart-Duval lift>, reduction along the null isometry fixes a nonzero conserved $p_u=t\prime$ and projects <geodesics> to $(t,\mathbf x)$. One never divides by $g_{uu}=0$. When $\mathbf E=-\nabla U$ and $\nabla\times\mathbf A=\mathbf B$, the reduced equation is $\ddot{\mathbf x}=\mathbf E+2\mathbf B\times\dot{\mathbf x}$. Every trajectory admits a null lift by choosing $\dot u=U+2\mathbf A\cdot\dot{\mathbf x}-\tfrac12|\dot{\mathbf x}|^2$.

= Newtonian mechanics
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{c}
{title2=$\mathbf F=m\mathbf a$}

Newtonian mechanics describes motion using forces, masses and acceleration in <inertial frames> with absolute time. It is the low-speed limit of <special relativity>; for constant <proper acceleration> with $|at|\ll c$, relativistic <hyperbolic motion> reduces to $x\simeq at^2/2$.

= Small-slope particle motion on a height graph
{parent=Newtonian mechanics}
{title2=$\ddot{\mathbf r}\simeq-g\nabla h$}

A particle constrained to the graph $z=h(x,y)$ has <kinetic energy> $m[|\dot{\mathbf r}|^2+(\nabla h\cdot\dot{\mathbf r})^2]/2$ and <potential energy> $mgh$. For a gently varying graph and velocities for which its geometric kinetic correction is higher order, the leading <Euler-Lagrange equations> are the displayed planar gradient-force equations. Small slope alone at arbitrarily large speed or curvature does not justify discarding the normal inertial acceleration. For a radial height, the leading planar <angular momentum> is conserved, allowing an <effective potential> reduction.

= Radial turning points for an inverse-square height well
{parent=Small-slope particle motion on a height graph}
{title2=$\dot r^2=2\mathcal E+2g/r-(L^2+2g)/r^2$}

For the height profile $h(r)=r^{-2}-r^{-1}$, set $A=L^2+2g$ and use the planar small-slope model. Its <effective potential> per unit mass is $V_{\rm eff}=A/(2r^2)-g/r$. Release with negligible radial speed at $r_0$ gives $\mathcal E=A/(2r_0^2)-g/r_0$ and the displayed radial-speed law. A bounded trajectory requires $\mathcal E<0$, equivalently $r_0>A/(2g)$. The two positive turning radii are $r_0$ and $Ar_0/(2gr_0-A)$; they coincide at the circular solution $r_0=A/g$. Zero energy is marginal escape, not bounded motion.

= Tangentially driven motion on an axisymmetric surface
{parent=Newtonian mechanics}
{title2=$\dot\phi^2=\frac{g h'(r)}{r+2r_\phi^2/r-r_{\phi\phi}}$}

Neglecting vertical acceleration and the vertical driving-force component, the radial normal reaction is $-Mg h'(r)$. A drive parallel to the horizontal velocity cancels when the radial acceleration equation is combined with $r_\phi/r$ times the azimuthal equation. For a prescribed path $r(\phi)$ this gives the displayed speed constraint. It describes the projected approximation, not arbitrary trajectories with substantial vertical motion.

// Target: classical-mechanics.bigb

= Friction
{parent=Newtonian mechanics}

<Friction> is a contact <force> tangent to the surfaces. <Kinetic friction> opposes <contact slip>; <static friction> maintains contact without <contact slip>, including <rolling without slipping>. The perpendicular contact component is the <normal force>. These two components answer different questions: how strongly the surfaces press together, and how strongly they resist sliding past one another.

The ideal Coulomb dry-contact model combines a bound on <static friction> with a sliding law for <kinetic friction>. Its parameters are the <coefficient of static friction> and the <coefficient of kinetic friction>; constant coefficients are an empirical approximation, rather than a consequence of <Newton's laws of motion>. Surface condition matters, and the static coefficient need not universally exceed the kinetic coefficient. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_12.html#Ch12-S2[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics I, §12-2: friction and the limits of the empirical law]{ref}

The model's historical setting was practical machinery: Coulomb's \i[Théorie des machines simples] investigated <friction> and the stiffness of ropes. The Académie's bibliography records its 1781 prize and its appearance in volume 10, pages 161–332, of the \i[Recueil des savants étrangers]. https://www.academie-sciences.fr/pdf/dossiers/Coulomb/Coulomb_pubd1.htm[Académie des sciences: Coulomb's published works]{ref}

= Kinetic friction
{parent=Friction}
{title2=$|F_{\mathrm{fr}}|=\mu N$}

<Kinetic friction> is <friction> during nonzero <contact slip>. For one-dimensional motion along a surface, choose right as positive and write $v$ for the body's contact-point <velocity> and $U$ for the surface's contact-point <velocity>, both measured in the same <inertial frame>. The signed <relative velocity> is $w=v-U$. The ideal Coulomb law for the tangential <force> on the body is
$$
f_k=-\mu_kN\operatorname{sgn}(v-U),\qquad v-U\ne0.
$$
Here $N\ge0$ is the magnitude of the <normal force>, $\mu_k\ge0$ is the <coefficient of kinetic friction>, and $\operatorname{sgn}$ is the <sign function>. Thus <kinetic friction> opposes <contact slip>, which can differ from the body's direction of motion. In several tangential directions the same law reads $\mathbf f_k=-\mu_kN\mathbf w/|\mathbf w|$ for a nonzero tangential <relative velocity> $\mathbf w$. The magnitude law is documented in https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/6-2-friction[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, OpenStax University Physics 1, §6.2: static and kinetic friction].

For a stipulated toy model, take a nonrotating box with <mass> $m=1\,\mathrm{kg}$, <normal force> $N=10\,\mathrm N$, <coefficient of static friction> $\mu_s=0.5$, and <coefficient of kinetic friction> $\mu_k=0.3$. These are assumed model parameters, rather than measured properties of any particular pair of materials; the prescribed <normal force> is consistent with horizontal support under $g=10\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$ and no other vertical <forces>. On stationary ground, a box already sliding right has $f_k=-3\,\mathrm N$. With an applied horizontal <force> $F=6\,\mathrm N$ to the right, <Newton's second law> gives $a=(F+f_k)/m=3\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, for as long as rightward <contact slip> persists.

Direction is clearer by comparing two instantaneous states of this same model. If the box has $v=1\,\mathrm{m/s}$ on stationary ground, $U=0$ and $w=+1\,\mathrm{m/s}$, so <kinetic friction> acts left with magnitude $3\,\mathrm N$. If the box still has $v=1\,\mathrm{m/s}$ but a belt beneath it has $U=2\,\mathrm{m/s}$, then $w=-1\,\mathrm{m/s}$: <kinetic friction> acts right with magnitude $3\,\mathrm N$, even while the box moves right.

\Image[kinetic-friction-relative-motion.png]
{title=The same rightward box velocity gives opposite friction directions on stationary ground and a faster moving belt}

In the belt case, the <power> delivered to the box is $P_{\mathrm{box}}=f_kv=+3\,\mathrm W$. With no other horizontal <force>, its <kinetic energy> therefore increases by the <work-energy theorem>. By <Newton's third law>, the box exerts $-f_k$ on the belt, so the contact <power> delivered to the belt is $P_{\mathrm{belt}}=-f_kU=-6\,\mathrm W$. Their sum is $-3\,\mathrm W$: <frictional heating> produces thermal <internal energy> at $3\,\mathrm W$. An ideal motor maintaining the prescribed belt <velocity> supplies $+6\,\mathrm W$ against this contact load, neglecting other losses, so its input divides into $3\,\mathrm W$ of box <kinetic energy> and $3\,\mathrm W$ of <frictional heating>. These are instantaneous rates, since the box's <velocity> subsequently changes. This balance follows directly from <power> as <force> times <velocity> and <conservation of energy>; the power law is given in https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/7-4-power[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, OpenStax University Physics 1, §7.4: power].

When <contact slip> vanishes, including a transition to <rolling without slipping>, the nonzero-slip law no longer applies. Determine the required <static friction> from the no-slip condition and the <equations of motion>, then test its bound. Substituting $\operatorname{sgn}(0)=0$ into the sliding law would incorrectly exclude a nonzero <static friction>.

= Coefficient of kinetic friction
{parent=Kinetic friction}
{title2=$\mu_k$}

The <coefficient of kinetic friction> is the <dimensionless quantity> $\mu_k=|f_k|/N$ for sliding contact with positive <normal force> $N$ in the ideal <kinetic friction> model. It characterizes the pair of contacting surfaces and their condition. A constant $\mu_k$ means the model neglects dependence on <contact slip> speed; unlike the <coefficient of static friction>, it specifies a sliding <force> magnitude rather than a no-slip upper bound.

// Target: classical-mechanics.bigb

= Two-stage stopping under linear drag and dry friction
{parent=Kinetic friction}
{title2=$t=\frac M\lambda\log\frac U{u_*}+\frac{u_*}{\mu g},\quad d=\frac M\lambda(U-u_*)+\frac{u_*^2}{2\mu g}$}

For a body of mass $M$ initially at speed $U$, suppose the total resistance is <linear drag> $\lambda u$ until speed $u_*>0$, followed by <kinetic friction> $\mu Mg$ until rest. The first stage solves $M\dot u=-\lambda u$, giving duration $(M/\lambda)\log(U/u_*)$ and distance $(M/\lambda)(U-u_*)$. Constant deceleration in the second stage gives duration $u_*/(\mu g)$ and distance $u_*^2/(2\mu g)$. The stopping distance is finite because the switch removes the infinite exponential stopping time of pure linear drag.

= Contact slip
{parent=Friction}
{title2=$\mathbf w$}

<Contact slip> is the tangential <relative velocity> between the two material points instantaneously in contact. For a nonrotating box on a belt it is $w=v-U$; for a <rigid body>, use the <velocity> of its contact point, including the contribution from <angular velocity>. Zero <contact slip> defines <rolling without slipping> at a rolling contact, even when the <center of mass> moves.

= Frictional heating
{parent=Friction}
{title2=$\dot E_{\mathrm{thermal}}=\mu_kN|w|$}

In the ideal <kinetic friction> model, the loss of combined <mechanical energy> of the two contacting bodies becomes thermal <internal energy> at rate
$$
\dot E_{\mathrm{thermal}}=-f_k(v-U)=\mu_kN|v-U|\ge0.
$$
This follows by adding the contact <powers> $f_kv$ and $-f_kU$ from <Newton's third law>. It depends on <contact slip>, whereas the contact <power> received by either body separately depends on its <velocity> in the chosen <inertial frame>. Thus positive <frictional heating> is compatible with increasing <kinetic energy> of one body, as the moving-belt example in <kinetic friction> shows. Heating need not cause a <temperature> rise if thermal <energy> escapes by <heat transfer>.

= Newton–Cartan theory
{parent=Newtonian mechanics}
{c}
{wiki}

Newton–Cartan theory describes <Newtonian mechanics> geometrically using absolute time, a degenerate spatial inverse metric, and a compatible torsion-free <affine connection>. It is not a theory with a nondegenerate Lorentzian metric. Gravitationally accelerated particles are geodesics of that connection, whose curvature measures tidal gravity.

= Newton–Cartan gravitational connection
{parent=Newton–Cartan theory}
{c}
{title2=$\Gamma^i{}_{00}=\partial_i\Phi$}

In Cartesian spatial coordinates and absolute time $t$, the <gravitational potential> $\Phi(t,x)$ determines an <affine connection> by the displayed coefficients, all others zero. The <geodesic equation> with affine time is $d^2x^i/dt^2=-\partial_i\Phi$. It preserves $dt$ and the spatial inverse metric $h^{ij}=\delta^{ij}$, reproducing freely falling <Newtonian mechanics> in a curved spacetime connection.

= Newtonian tidal curvature
{parent=Newton–Cartan gravitational connection}
{title2=$R^i{}_{0j0}=\partial_i\partial_j\Phi,\quad R_{00}=\nabla^2\Phi$}

Using $[\nabla_c,\nabla_d]V^a=R^a{}_{bcd}V^b$, the <Newton–Cartan gravitational connection> has only the displayed Hessian components and their antisymmetric partners in the last two indices. The <geodesic deviation> equation gives relative acceleration $-\partial_i\partial_j\Phi\,\delta x^j$. The <Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity> is therefore $R_{ab}=4\pi G\rho\,(dt)_a(dt)_b$. A spatially uniform gravitational acceleration has zero tidal curvature even if the connection coefficients do not vanish in the original coordinates.

= Equation of motion
{parent=Classical mechanics}

= Equations of motion
{synonym}

An equation of motion determines how a physical state evolves. A variational action gives <Euler-Lagrange equations>; a <phase-space action> instead gives canonical <Hamiltonian mechanics> equations together with any imposed <constraints>.

= Time reversal in classical mechanics
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{title2=$x(t)\mapsto x(-t)$}

<Time reversal in classical mechanics> reverses the order of a trajectory's states and the sign of its velocities. A coordinate is time-even, while its velocity is time-odd. <Microscopic reversibility> compares a path with this physical reversal, including the correct equilibrium initial weights.

= Newton's law of universal gravitation
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{c}
{title2=$\mathbf F=-GMm\mathbf r/|\mathbf r|^3$}
{wiki}

<Newton's law of universal gravitation> gives the attractive <force> between two <point masses>. With source <mass> $M$ at the origin and relative <position> $\mathbf r$ of <mass> $m$, write $r=|\mathbf r|>0$:
$$
\mathbf F_{M\to m}=-\frac{GMm}{r^3}\mathbf r,\qquad |\mathbf F_{M\to m}|=\frac{GMm}{r^2}.
$$
Here $G$ is the <gravitational constant>. The minus sign points toward $M$; the magnitude follows an <inverse-square law>, so doubling the separation quarters the <force>. The opposite <force> on $M$ satisfies <Newton's third law>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/13-1-newtons-law-of-universal-gravitation[OpenStax: mutual gravitational forces]{ref}

Both <masses> accelerate. Applying <Newton's second law> to each in the isolated <two-body problem> gives
$$
\ddot{\mathbf r}=-\frac{\mu}{r^3}\mathbf r,\qquad \mu=G(M+m).
$$
The relative <standard gravitational parameter> includes both <masses>; replacing it by $GM$ requires the <test particle> approximation $m\ll M$.

Historically, Newton's Book III, Proposition VII argues that gravitational attraction applies to all bodies and scales with their <masses>; its second corollary states the <inverse-square law> for individual particles. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Mathematical_Principles_of_Natural_Philosophy_(1729)/Proposition_7:_Gravity_tending_to_all_Bodies[Newton, Principia, Book III, Proposition VII, Motte's 1729 translation]{ref}

= Free fall
{parent=Newton's law of universal gravitation}
{wiki}

Free fall is motion under <gravity> alone: there is no supporting <normal force>, engine thrust or appreciable <drag force>. In the <Newtonian mechanics> model, a <test particle> of <mass> $m$ obeys $m\ddot{\mathbf r}=m\mathbf g(\mathbf r)$, hence $\ddot{\mathbf r}=\mathbf g(\mathbf r)$. The <mass> cancels. Bodies released at the same <position> with the same <velocity> follow the same path in this model, even if their <gravitational weights> differ. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/free-falling-objects/[NASA Glenn: free-falling objects and the cancellation of mass]{ref}

A dropped stone is one example; a coasting satellite is another. Free fall does not require motion straight down or decreasing distance to the attracting body. An ideal <circular orbit> has constant radius while its <velocity> continually turns under <centripetal acceleration>. The <circular orbit as continuous free fall> connects the familiar drop with orbital motion.

Near a surface, treating the <gravitational acceleration> as constant gives a useful first prediction. Over larger heights, the <inverse-square law> changes the acceleration during the fall. <Radial free fall from rest> uses <conservation of mechanical energy> to account for that change exactly, within the specified <Newtonian gravitational field>. The contrast matters: the simplest formula is an approximation to a physical model, rather than a law with the same numerical $g$ everywhere.

History also makes the idealization tangible. Galileo's *Two New Sciences* (1638), Third Day, Theorem II, derives distance proportional to the square of time for uniformly accelerated motion from rest; its first corollary gives successive distances in the ratios $1,3,5,7,\ldots$ over equal time intervals. This is a kinematic statement, predating the gravitational <inverse-square law>. https://galileoandeinstein.phys.virginia.edu/tns_draft/tns_160to243.html[Galileo, Two New Sciences, Third Day, Theorem II and Corollary I; Crew and de Salvio's 1914 translation, pp. 174–176]{ref}

On Apollo 15's final lunar surface excursion, David Scott released a hammer and a feather together. The near-<vacuum> removed the large difference in <drag force> familiar on Earth. NASA's report describes their arrival together within the accuracy of the release; this demonstration illustrates the <free fall> prediction without establishing infinite experimental precision. https://science.nasa.gov/resource/the-apollo-15-hammer-feather-drop/[NASA's original footage and Joe Allen's account in the Apollo 15 Preliminary Science Report, p. 2-11]{ref}

\Video[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oYEgdZ3iEKA]
{title=NASA footage of David Scott's Apollo 15 hammer-and-feather free-fall demonstration}

= Free fall in a constant gravitational field
{parent=Free fall}
{title2=$s(t)=\tfrac12gt^2,\quad v(t)=gt$}

Take downward <displacement> $s$ as positive, assume a constant <gravitational acceleration> $g>0$, and release a <point mass> from rest at $s=0$. Integrating $\ddot s=g$ twice gives
$$
v(t)=gt,\qquad s(t)=\frac12gt^2.
$$
For a downward distance $h>0$, the arrival time and <speed> are
$$
t_{\mathrm{fall}}=\sqrt{\frac{2h}{g}},\qquad v_{\mathrm{arrival}}=\sqrt{2gh}.
$$
For the model values $h=20\,\mathrm m$ and $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, these are approximately $2.0193\,\mathrm s$ and $19.8091\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. Doubling the height multiplies both quantities by $\sqrt2$; it does not double them. The same <speed> follows from the <work-energy theorem>, $mgh=mv^2/2$.

For intervals of length $\Delta t$, the successive downward <displacements> are
$$
s((j+1)\Delta t)-s(j\Delta t)=\frac12g(\Delta t)^2(2j+1),\qquad j=0,1,2,\ldots.
$$
Thus equal spacing in time produces increasingly separated positions. This is the odd-number pattern in Galileo's result. It assumes constant <acceleration> and release from rest; a nonzero initial <velocity> adds a linear term to $s(t)$.

The approximation can be estimated for a spherical source of radius $R$ and <standard gravitational parameter> $\mu$. Writing $g_0=\mu/R^2$, the gravitational magnitude at height $h$ is
$$
\frac{g(R+h)}{g_0}=\left(1+\frac hR\right)^{-2}.
$$
For $h/R\ll1$, a <Taylor expansion> gives $g(R+h)/g_0=1-2h/R+O((h/R)^2)$. Small height relative to the source's radius makes nearly constant gravity plausible; negligible <drag force> remains a separate requirement.

= Radial free fall from rest
{parent=Free fall}
{title2=$v(r)=\sqrt{2\mu(1/r-1/r_0)}$}

Let a <test particle> start at rest at radius $r_0$ outside a fixed spherically symmetric source. The <spherical shell theorem> gives the exterior <Newtonian gravitational potential> $\Phi(r)=-\mu/r$, where $\mu=GM$ is the source's <standard gravitational parameter>. Zero initial transverse <velocity> makes the motion radial. <Conservation of mechanical energy> per unit <mass> gives
$$
\frac{\dot r^2}{2}-\frac{\mu}{r}=-\frac{\mu}{r_0},\qquad
\dot r=-\sqrt{2\mu\left(\frac1r-\frac1{r_0}\right)}.
$$
The minus sign selects inward motion; the positive square root is its <speed>. For a fall from $r_0=2R$ to the surface $r=R$, that <speed> is $\sqrt{\mu/R}$. In the limit of release from rest infinitely far away, the familiar <free-fall speed> is $\sqrt{2\mu/r}$, equal in magnitude to the local <escape velocity>. A finite starting radius gives a smaller speed.

The arrival time follows from the same first integral. Set $r=r_0\cos^2\eta$. Then
$$
t(\eta)=\sqrt{\frac{r_0^3}{2\mu}}\left(\eta+\sin\eta\cos\eta\right),\qquad
\eta=\arccos\sqrt{\frac r{r_0}},\qquad 0\leq\eta<\frac\pi2.
$$
For arrival at $R>0$ this is finite, and the field formula is used only outside the source. For $r_0=2R$, $\eta=\pi/4$ gives
$$
t_{\mathrm{arrival}}=\left(1+\frac\pi2\right)\sqrt{\frac{R^3}{\mu}}\approx2.570796\sqrt{\frac{R^3}{\mu}}.
$$
Keeping the smaller initial gravitational magnitude $g(r_0)=\mu/(4R^2)$ constant would predict $\sqrt8\,\sqrt{R^3/\mu}$, about $10.0\%$ longer. The actual <acceleration> increases during the fall.

\Image[radial-free-fall-gravity-variation.png]
{title=Radial free-fall height and arrival time compared with constant gravity at the release point}

The curves compare a shallow fall, $h/R=0.02$, with a fall from $r_0=2R$, so $h/R=1$. Each comparison holds the release height and initial gravitational magnitude fixed. The constant-gravity model is close for the shallow fall and increasingly inaccurate for the larger one. This is also a practical use of <nondimensionalization>: the normalized trajectories depend on $h/R$, while $\sqrt{R^3/\mu}$ supplies the dimensional time scale.

= Rotational deflection of a falling body
{parent=Free fall}

In an Earth-fixed <rotating reference frame>, the horizontal displacement of a freely falling body receives contributions from <Coriolis acceleration> and <centrifugal acceleration>. A short-fall approximation substitutes the unperturbed downward <velocity> into the Coriolis term and the Earth's surface <position> into the centrifugal term. If <gravitational acceleration> excludes centrifugal acceleration, both contributions must be included explicitly.

= Newtonian gravitational field
{parent=Newton's law of universal gravitation}
{c}
{title2=$\mathbf g$}

The <Newtonian gravitational field> is the gravitational <force> per unit <mass> of a <test particle>. For a <point mass> $M$ at the origin,
$$
\mathbf g(\mathbf r)=\frac{\mathbf F}{m}=-\frac{GM}{r^3}\mathbf r,\qquad g(r)=|\mathbf g|=\frac{GM}{r^2}.
$$
The <test particle>'s <mass> cancels: different small <masses> have the same <gravitational acceleration> at the same <position>. In <SI>, the <Newtonian gravitational field> has units $\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}=\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. The same exterior expression holds for a spherically symmetric source, by the <spherical shell theorem>.

Choose a reference radius $r_0>0$, set $g_0=GM/r_0^2$, and use <nondimensionalization> with $z=r/r_0$. Then $g/g_0=z^{-2}$: at $z=1,2,4$, the magnitudes are $1,1/4,1/16$. This makes the spatial variation visible without specifying a planet. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/13-1-newtons-law-of-universal-gravitation[OpenStax: inverse-square attraction and spherical sources]{ref}

\Image[gravity-force-and-potential.png]
{title=Normalized Newtonian gravitational field and point-mass potential}
{description=Doubling the radius quarters the magnitude of the <Newtonian gravitational field>, but halves the magnitude of the negative <Newtonian gravitational potential>. Both curves approach zero as radius increases; the inward <gravitational acceleration> is minus the <gradient> of the <Newtonian gravitational potential>.}

= Uniform gravitational field in an off-center spherical cavity
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}
{title2=$g=-4\pi G\rho c/3$}

Remove a spherical void centered at $c$ from a uniform sphere centered at the origin. At points inside the void, <superposition> combines the sphere's interior field $-4\pi G\rho x/3$ with the removed sphere's field $+4\pi G\rho(x-c)/3$. Their sum is the displayed constant <Newtonian gravitational field>. A second removed mass outside this void adds a nonuniform contribution.

= Cavity equilibrium destabilized by a second void
{parent=Uniform gravitational field in an off-center spherical cavity}

A uniform <Newtonian gravitational field> inside one spherical void can be cancelled at a point by the repulsive field representing another removed sphere. The latter field has a <Jacobian matrix> proportional to $I-3\hat r\hat r^T$: its axial eigenvalue is negative and both transverse eigenvalues positive. The equilibrium is restoring along the axis but unstable transversely. A zero force is thus not enough for stable rest.

= Newtonian gravitational stress tensor
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}
{c}
{title2=$T_{ij}$}

For a <Newtonian gravitational potential> obeying the <Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity>, define $T_{ij}=[\partial_i\Phi\partial_j\Phi-\delta_{ij}|\nabla\Phi|^2/2]/(4\pi G)$. Commutation of mixed <partial derivatives> gives $\partial_jT_{ij}=\rho\partial_i\Phi$. Thus the gravitational force density can be put in conservative form as $-\partial_jT_{ij}$. The sign is chosen so that $T_{ij}$ is added to the momentum flux; reversing the stress convention reverses the divergence sign.

= Gravitational stress contribution to angular-momentum transport
{parent=Newtonian gravitational stress tensor}
{title2=$rT_{r\phi}$}

In cylindrical coordinates, the <Newtonian gravitational stress tensor> gives radial <angular-momentum flux> $rT_{r\phi}=r(\partial_r\Phi)(r^{-1}\partial_\phi\Phi)/(4\pi G)$. Nonaxisymmetric structure can correlate the field components and transport <angular momentum>. The isotropic negative-pressure part has zero off-diagonal component, so it gives no direct radial transport. This separates a shear flux from a contribution to normal force balance.

= Self-gravity
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}

Self-gravity is the <gravitational acceleration> produced by a system's own <mass density>. It is obtained by solving the <Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity> for that density, rather than treating the <Newtonian gravitational potential> as an externally fixed field. It competes with <pressure> and rotational support in <gravitational instability of an astrophysical disk>.

= Standard gravitational parameter
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}
{title2=$\mu$}
{wiki}

= Gravitational parameter
{synonym}

The product $\mu=GM$ of the <gravitational constant> and a central <mass> sets the strength of the exterior <Newtonian gravitational field>. Its dimensions are length cubed divided by time squared. In the exact relative <two-body problem>, the parameter is $G(M_1+M_2)$; using $GM$ for one source assumes the <test particle> approximation.

The combination is useful because orbital and falling-body observations constrain $\mu$ directly. A gravitational <circular orbit> of radius $a$ has <oscillation period> $T=2\pi\sqrt{a^3/\mu}$, so measuring $a$ and $T$ gives $\mu=4\pi^2a^3/T^2$. That measurement alone does not separately determine $G$ and $M$. Similarly, <radial free fall from rest> has a natural time scale $\sqrt{r_0^3/\mu}$.

Other interactions require a different model. Inverse-square <radiation pressure> can reduce the effective attraction for a dust <Kepler orbit>; calling that effective coefficient a gravitational parameter must not hide the added <force>.

= Tidal tensor
{title2=$T_{jk}$}
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}

The acceleration tidal tensor is $T_{jk}=\partial_k g_j=-\partial_j\partial_k\Phi$ for <gravitational acceleration> $\mathbf g=-\nabla\Phi$. It describes differential acceleration $\Delta\mathbf g\simeq T\Delta\mathbf r$. Some authors instead call the positive <Hessian matrix> $\partial_j\partial_k\Phi$ the tidal tensor, reversing this sign convention. For a point mass and the positive potential convention $\mathbf g=\nabla\psi$, the tensor is $Gm(3\mathbf R\mathbf R^{\mathsf T}/R^5-\mathbf1/R^3)$.

The <tidal tensor> is the <Jacobian matrix> of the <Newtonian gravitational field>, rather than the <gravitational acceleration> itself. Its entries have units $\mathrm{s^{-2}}$: multiplying by a <displacement> produces an <acceleration>. For a smooth <Newtonian gravitational potential>, the <tidal tensor> is a <symmetric matrix>, since mixed <partial derivatives> commute. A spatially uniform <gravitational acceleration> has zero <tidal tensor>, however large that <acceleration> is.

= Point-mass tidal stretching and squeezing
{parent=Tidal tensor}
{title2=$T=\mu(3\mathbf n\mathbf n^{\mathsf T}-I)/R^3$}

For a source <point mass> $M$ at the origin, let $\mathbf R=R\mathbf n$, where $\mathbf n$ is an outward <unit vector>, and let $\mu=GM$ be the source's <standard gravitational parameter>. Differentiating $g_i=-\mu R_i/R^3$ gives the <tidal tensor>
$$
T_{ij}=\frac{\mu}{R^3}(3n_i n_j-\delta_{ij}),\qquad T=\frac{\mu}{R^3}(3\mathbf n\mathbf n^{\mathsf T}-I),
$$
where $\delta_{ij}$ is the <Kronecker delta> and $I$ is the <identity matrix>. In an <orthonormal basis> with first axis radial, the <tidal tensor> is
$$
T=\frac{\mu}{R^3}\begin{pmatrix}2&0&0\\0&-1&0\\0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The radial <eigenvalue> $2\mu/R^3$ separates nearby freely falling <test particles>; the two transverse <eigenvalues> $-\mu/R^3$ bring them together. The farther radial <test particle> falls inward less rapidly, so its <acceleration> relative to the nearer one points outward. Transverse <gravitational accelerations> converge toward the source. Thus an initially resting small cloud stretches radially and squeezes in both transverse directions. These are instantaneous relative <accelerations>; the subsequent shape also depends on initial <velocities> and the changing <tidal tensor> along the cloud's <free fall>.

Outside the source, the <matrix trace> is $2\mu/R^3-\mu/R^3-\mu/R^3=0$. This matches $\operatorname{tr}T=-\nabla^2\Phi=0$ in a region with zero <mass density>, by the <Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity>. Zero <matrix trace> means no leading change of a small initially resting cloud's <volume> at order $t^2$, not that each separation stays fixed. Inside matter, the same sign convention gives $\operatorname{tr}T=-4\pi G\rho$ instead.

= Quadrupolar point-mass tidal potential
{parent=Tidal tensor}
{title2=$\psi_{\mathrm{tid}}=\tfrac12r_iT_{ij}r_j$}

Subtracting the constant and uniform-acceleration terms from the point-mass <relative potential> gives $\psi_{\mathrm{tid}}=Gm_p[3(\mathbf r\cdot\widehat{\mathbf r}_p)^2-r^2]/(2r_p^3)$, with force convention $\nabla\psi$. Its <tidal tensor> is $Gm_p(3\widehat{\mathbf r}_p\widehat{\mathbf r}_p^T-I)/r_p^3$. This is the leading term for target size small compared with separation.

= Tidal disruption
{parent=Tidal tensor}

Disruption of a self-gravitating body when differential gravitational <acceleration> across it exceeds its binding <acceleration>. For a body of radius $R$, <mass> $m$ at distance $r$ from a much more massive body $M$, the competing scales are $GMR/r^3$ and $Gm/R^2$; their equality gives $r\sim R(M/m)^{1/3}$, up to geometry and internal-structure factors. The <tidal tensor> gives the directional differential <acceleration>. A <tidal disruption event> near a black hole is a specific astronomical application.

= Tidal force
{parent=Tidal tensor}
{wiki}

Differential gravitational <acceleration>, multiplied by the affected <mass>, across a spatially extended body. In the linear approximation the <acceleration> difference is $T\Delta\boldsymbol r$, where $T$ is the <tidal tensor>. Stellar <tidal forces> limit the domain of a <circumplanetary orbit>; sufficiently strong ones can produce <tidal disruption>.

To isolate a <tidal force>, subtract the <gravitational acceleration> shared by the body as a whole. Let a reference <test particle> follow $\ddot{\mathbf R}=\mathbf g(\mathbf R)$, and let another have <position> $\mathbf R+\mathbf x$. In a <translating accelerated reference frame> with parallel, nonrotating axes,
$$
\ddot{\mathbf x}=\mathbf g(\mathbf R+\mathbf x)-\mathbf g(\mathbf R)=T(\mathbf R)\mathbf x+O\!\left(\frac{\mu|\mathbf x|^2}{R^4}\right)
$$
for a source <point mass>, when $|\mathbf x|/R\ll1$. Here $\mu=GM$ is its <standard gravitational parameter> and the last term uses <Big O notation>. This <Taylor expansion> removes the common inward pull and keeps the spatial variation. The near side has an excess pull toward the source; the far side has a deficit of inward pull, which is an outward relative <acceleration>. Both sides can stretch apart while both still accelerate toward the source.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 7: The Theory of Gravitation>[§7–4, discussion of stronger near-side and weaker far-side attraction]{ref}

\Image[tidal-field-difference-and-stretching.png]
{title=Subtracting common gravitational acceleration reveals radial stretching and transverse squeezing}
{description=Blue arrows show the exact <Newtonian gravitational field> at several <positions> near a reference point. Orange arrows show its linear residual, calculated with the <tidal tensor>. Arrow lengths use separate scales in the two panels; this is a comparison of direction and spatial variation, not two equal-magnitude fields.}

For a material element of <mass> $m$, the residual external <force> in this <translating accelerated reference frame> is $m[\mathbf g(\mathbf R+\mathbf x)-\mathbf g(\mathbf R)]$. Actual deformation also depends on <elasticity>, <pressure> and <self-gravity>. A <rigid body> approximation suppresses deformation but can still have internal <forces> that resist the <tidal force>. The <point-mass tidal stretching and squeezing> pattern describes freely falling, noninteracting <test particles>, rather than promising the same shape for every solid, star or ocean. A <rotating reference frame> introduces <centrifugal acceleration> and <Coriolis acceleration>, which must be included separately.

= Exact tidal acceleration between separated masses
{parent=Tidal force}
{title2=$\Delta a=2\mu L/[R^3(1-L^2/(4R^2))^2]$}

Stipulate a fixed source <point mass> $M=10^{12}\,\mathrm{kg}$, two equal <test particles> of <mass> $m=1\,\mathrm{kg}$, and $G=6.67430\times10^{-11}\,\mathrm{m^3\,kg^{-1}\,s^{-2}}$. This is a chosen model, not a measurement of a particular object. Place the <test particles> on the outward radial axis at $R-L/2$ and $R+L/2$, with midpoint distance $R=100\,\mathrm m$ and separation $L=2\,\mathrm m$. Neglect their mutual attraction, other sources and all supporting <forces>.

Each signed radial <gravitational acceleration> is inward:
$$
a_-=-\frac{\mu}{(R-L/2)^2}\approx-0.0068098153\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}},\qquad a_+=-\frac{\mu}{(R+L/2)^2}\approx-0.0065427899\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}.
$$
Their separation has outward relative <acceleration>
$$
\ddot L=a_+-a_-=\mu\left[\frac{1}{(R-L/2)^2}-\frac{1}{(R+L/2)^2}\right]=\frac{2\mu L}{R^3}\left(1-\frac{L^2}{4R^2}\right)^{-2}.
$$
The exact value is $2.6702540\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. The midpoint <tidal tensor> predicts $2\mu L/R^3=2.6697200\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, an underestimate of $0.019999\%$ relative to the exact value. For these equal $1\,\mathrm{kg}$ <masses>, the difference of external <forces> has the same numerical value in the <SI> unit of <force>, the <newton (unit)>. It is not an additional mutual <force> between the <test particles>.

The exact formula requires $L<2R$ and describes the instantaneous relative <acceleration>. Because the two sample <positions> are symmetric about the midpoint, the <Taylor expansion> error relative to the linear result starts at order $(L/R)^2$; a one-sided comparison with a reference point generally has relative error of order $L/R$. If either <test particle> enters an extended source, use that source's interior <Newtonian gravitational field> instead of extrapolating the singular <point-mass> model.

= Gravity magnitude versus tidal gradient
{parent=Tidal force}
{title2=$g=\mu/R^2,\quad \lambda_r=2\mu/R^3$}

For a fixed source <point mass>, the magnitude $g=\mu/R^2$ of the <gravitational acceleration> measures the local pull; the radial <eigenvalue> $\lambda_r=2\mu/R^3$ of the <tidal tensor> measures how that pull changes across space. Doubling $R$ divides $g$ by four but $\lambda_r$ by eight. For a fixed small radial separation $L$, the linear relative <acceleration> is $\Delta a\simeq\lambda_r L$, so
$$
\frac{\Delta a}{g}\simeq\frac{2L}{R}.
$$
A small body can therefore experience strong common <gravitational acceleration> with a comparatively small <tidal force>. A measurement of $g$ alone does not determine the <tidal tensor>: sources at different distances can have the same $g$ but different spatial variation.

\Image[tidal-gravity-distance-scaling.png]
{title=Gravity falls as inverse distance squared, while point-mass tidal stretching falls as inverse distance cubed}
{description=The left panel compares independently normalized <gravitational acceleration> and radial <tidal tensor> <eigenvalue> for a fixed source. The right panel compares exact and linear relative <accelerations> for two radially separated <test particles> at a fixed midpoint, showing the finite-separation correction as their separation grows.}

The local stretching time scale is $\lambda_r^{-1/2}=\sqrt{R^3/(2\mu)}$: it marks when an initially resting separation could change by order itself if the <tidal tensor> stayed fixed. During <free fall>, $R$ changes, so this scale is not a complete trajectory or a universal disruption time. <Tidal disruption> additionally compares the <tidal force> with binding by <self-gravity> and material <forces>; $g$ by itself supplies no such comparison.

= Love number
{c}
{title2=$k_2$}
{wiki}
{parent=Tidal force}

A Love number measures a body's tidal response to an applied potential. The degree-two potential response $k_2$ is sensitive to radial density structure and central concentration, and can complement mass and radius constraints on an <exoplanet interior>. Inferring it from orbital precession requires separating other contributions and assumptions about rotation and the tidal response.

= Gravity gradiometry
{parent=Tidal tensor}

<Gravity gradiometry> measures spatial differences of <gravitational acceleration>. Two simultaneous measurements along a <unit vector> $\mathbf e$, separated by $L\mathbf e$, give
$$
\frac{\mathbf e\cdot[\mathbf g(\mathbf R+L\mathbf e)-\mathbf g(\mathbf R)]}{L}\simeq\mathbf e^{\mathsf T}T\mathbf e.
$$
This measures one directional component of the <tidal tensor>, not its whole <matrix>. Any identical platform <acceleration> cancels in the difference. Unequal instrument responses, changing geometry and <rotating reference frames> can spoil that cancellation. For a large baseline, the quotient is an average of the directional <tidal tensor> along the baseline, rather than its exact value at one point.

= Atom-cloud gravity-gradient measurement
{parent=Gravity gradiometry}

A laboratory <gravity gradiometry> experiment used two vertically separated clouds of <chemical atoms> in <vacuum>, launched into <free fall>. Shared <laser> beams applied three pulses ($\pi/2,\pi,\pi/2$), splitting and recombining <matter waves>. Balanced <photodiodes> measured probe-light absorption to infer internal-state populations; scanning the last pulse's <wave phase> produced an <interference> fringe.<Sensitive absolute-gravity gradiometry using atom interferometry by J. M. McGuirk and others>[§I, pp. 033608-1–2; §II E 1, p. 033608-6; §II F 1, p. 033608-7]{ref}

The difference of fringe <wave phases> obeys $\delta\phi=\mathbf k_{\mathrm{eff}}\cdot\Delta\mathbf g\,\tau^2$, with effective <wave vector> $\mathbf k_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and pulse spacing $\tau$: shared reference motion cancels, leaving differential <gravitational acceleration>. Eight lead bricks, each approximately $12.5\,\mathrm{kg}$, were placed symmetrically around the lower chamber, about $0.2\,\mathrm m$ from the fountain apex. The measured signal was $(8.1\pm2.1)\times10^{-9}g$, versus a calculated $8.2\times10^{-9}g$, where $g$ denotes terrestrial <gravitational acceleration>.<Sensitive absolute-gravity gradiometry using atom interferometry by J. M. McGuirk and others>[§I, p. 033608-2; §III A 2, p. 033608-10, mass detection]{ref}

Controls deliberately shook the shared reference reflector and compared actively isolated and unisolated operation. Tilting tests exposed sensitivity to <Coriolis acceleration> and <centrifugal acceleration>. Thus shared translation is rejectable, while rotation and finite measurement uncertainty remain limitations. The brick signal detects spatially varying <gravity>; it does not establish a precision value of the <gravitational constant>.<Sensitive absolute-gravity gradiometry using atom interferometry by J. M. McGuirk and others>[§III A, p. 033608-9; §III B, pp. 033608-10–11]{ref}

= Quadrupolar tidal forcing
{parent=Tidal tensor}

For a point-mass companion at separation $a$, subtract the constant and linear terms of its <Newtonian gravitational potential> in a frame accelerating with the target's center. The leading residual is
$$
\Phi_{\mathrm{tide}}=-\frac{GM_2}{2a^3}\left[3(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf r)^2-r^2\right].
$$
It is degree two in <spherical harmonics>. In a circular orbit with angular frequency $\omega_o$, its time-dependent $m=\pm2$ components force at $2\omega_o$ in a frame with no spin; there is also a static $m=0$ component. This is the leading approximation in $r/a$, and the tidal amplitude may be absorbed into a harmonic-dependent constant.

= Gravitational acceleration
{title2=$g$}
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}
{wiki}

= Newtonian gravity
{synonym}
{c}

Gravitational acceleration is the <acceleration> of a <test particle> in <free fall> in a <Newtonian gravitational field>. It is a vector pointing along that field; $g$ commonly denotes its positive magnitude. Near Earth's surface, the magnitude is approximately constant over shallow vertical scales. For a fixed spherical source it instead varies as $g(r)=\mu/r^2$, where $\mu$ is the <standard gravitational parameter>.

This is distinct from <gravitational weight>, the <force> $m\mathbf g$, and from <apparent weight>, the support force a scale measures. An orbiting body can have substantial <gravitational acceleration> while its <apparent weight> is zero.

= Newtonian central configuration
{c}
{parent=Gravitational acceleration}
{title2=$\nabla_iW=\Lambda m_i\mathbf a_i$}

A collision-free positive-mass configuration satisfies the displayed equation when every gravitational acceleration points proportionally toward a common centre. With $W=-\sum_{i<j}Gm_im_j/|\mathbf a_i-\mathbf a_j|$, homogeneity gives $\Lambda I=-W>0$, $I=\sum_i m_i|\mathbf a_i|^2$. Pairwise force cancellation implies $\sum_i m_i\mathbf a_i=0$. Such a configuration generates a nonrotating <homothetic gravitational motion> by multiplying all position vectors by one positive scale factor.

= Homothetic gravitational motion
{parent=Newtonian central configuration}
{title2=$\ddot a=-\Lambda/a^2$}

For a <Newtonian central configuration>, set $\mathbf x_i(t)=a(t)\mathbf a_i$ with $a>0$. The gravitational force scales as $a^{-2}$, reducing all equations to the displayed scalar <ordinary differential equation>. Its first integral is $\dot a^2/2-\Lambda/a=k/2$. The total <energy> is $kI/2$, so the scalar energy constant exactly tracks the many-body energy for the fixed shape.

= Gravitational constant
{title2=$G$}
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}
{wiki}

The gravitational constant is the proportionality constant in <Newton's law of universal gravitation>.

= Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity
{c}
{title2=$\nabla^2\Phi=4\pi G\rho$}
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}

For <mass density> $\rho$, the <Newtonian gravitational potential> satisfies the <Poisson equation> $\nabla^2\Phi=4\pi G\rho$. The gravitational acceleration is $-\nabla\Phi$. An isolated system also requires the exterior vacuum solution and decay at infinity; solving the interior equation alone does not fix a globally physical field.

= Dyadic spherical shells with inverse-radius density scaling
{parent=Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity}
{title2=$M(2^{-N})=M(1)\,4^{-N}$}

Suppose the shell $2^{-n}<r<2^{1-n}$ has <mass density> $2^{n-1}\rho_0$. Its mass is $(14\pi\rho_0/3)4^{-n}$, by integrating $4\pi r^2\rho\,dr$. Summing the <geometric series> gives total mass $14\pi\rho_0/9$. At $r=2^{-N}$ the enclosed shells have indices $n\geq N+1$, so the enclosed mass is the total mass times $r^2$. The magnitude of the <Newtonian gravitational field> at every such boundary is therefore the same, $14\pi G\rho_0/9$. This does not assert that the field is constant throughout each shell.

= Newtonian gravitational potential
{title2=$\Phi$}
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}
{c}

The <Newtonian gravitational potential> $\Phi$ is <potential energy> per unit <mass> of a <test particle>. Its <gradient> determines the <Newtonian gravitational field>:
$$
U=m\Phi,\qquad \mathbf g=-\nabla\Phi,\qquad \mathbf F=-\nabla U=-m\nabla\Phi.
$$
In <SI>, $U$ has units $\mathrm J$, while $\Phi$ has units $\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}}=\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-2}}$; its <gradient> has units of <acceleration>, not <force>. Adding a constant to $\Phi$ leaves the <Newtonian gravitational field> unchanged. For an isolated <point mass>, the convenient reference is $\Phi(\infty)=0$. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/16-07-dynamics-fall-2009/0bb19205a6316ba88f88284bf7257ec6_MIT16_07F09_Lec16.pdf[Peraire and Widnall, MIT Dynamics, Lecture 16: potential per unit mass]{ref}

Increasing the radius from $r_0$ to $r_1>r_0$ raises the <Newtonian gravitational potential energy>:
$$
\Delta U=GMm\left(\frac1{r_0}-\frac1{r_1}\right)>0.
$$
An external agent supplies this positive <work> if the initial and final <kinetic energies> agree; gravity does <work> $-\Delta U$. For $r_1=2r_0$, the required <work> is $GMm/(2r_0)$. <University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/13.3 Gravitational Potential Energy and Total Energy>{ref}

= Planetary quadrupole coefficient
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential}
{title2=$J_2$}

The dimensionless coefficient $J_2$ describes the leading axisymmetric departure of a planet's external <Newtonian gravitational potential> from a monopole: $\Phi=-GM_p/r+[GM_pJ_2R_p^2/r^3]P_2(\cos\vartheta)$, where $\vartheta$ is colatitude from the spin axis. An oblate mass distribution has positive $J_2$. The <disturbing function> is minus the nonspherical potential term. On a circular inclined <Kepler orbit>, $\cos\vartheta=\sin I\sin M$, so <orbit averaging> gives $\overline{\mathcal R}_{J_2}=GM_pJ_2R_p^2a^{-3}(1/2-3\sin^2I/4)$. The exact circular nodal rate is $-3nJ_2(R_p/a)^2\cos I/2$; its small-tilt limit is $-3nJ_2(R_p/a)^2/2$.

= Oblate-quadrupole epicyclic precession
{parent=Planetary quadrupole coefficient}
{title2=$\kappa^2=n^2(1-3\alpha^2/R^2),\quad\nu^2=n^2(1+9\alpha^2/R^2)$}

For $\Phi=-\mu/r+\alpha^2\mu(3z^2-r^2)/r^5$, a nearly circular equatorial orbit has $n^2=\mu/R^3$ and azimuthal frequency $\Omega_c^2=n^2(1+3\alpha^2/R^2)$. The <radial epicyclic frequency> is $\kappa^2=R\,d\Omega_c^2/dR+4\Omega_c^2$, and the <vertical epicyclic frequency> is $\nu^2=\partial_z^2\Phi|_{z=0}$. Radial stability requires $R^2>3\alpha^2$. The <nodal precession> rate $\Omega_c-\nu$ is negative and the <apsidal precession> rate $\Omega_c-\kappa$ positive. These sign statements concern small eccentricity and inclination. For an arbitrary inclined orbit, the averaged <argument of periapsis> instead evolves as $(3/2)n\alpha^2[5\cos^2i-1]/[a^2(1-e^2)^2]$ and can regress.

= Axisymmetric logarithmic gravitational potential
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential}
{title2=$\Phi=\tfrac12V^2\log(R^2+z^2/q^2)$}

An axisymmetric logarithmic <Newtonian gravitational potential> has spheroidal equipotential surfaces and a <flat galaxy rotation curve>: $R\Phi_R(R,0)=V^2$. The singular model is defined away from the origin; a fixed reference length makes the logarithm dimensionless. Its central $r^{-2}$ <mass density> cusp is locally integrable, but its total <mass> diverges at large radius.

= Density positivity for an axisymmetric logarithmic potential
{parent=Axisymmetric logarithmic gravitational potential}
{title2=$q^2\geq1/2$}

The <Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity> gives
$$
\rho=\frac{V^2}{4\pi Gq^2}\frac{R^2+(2-q^{-2})z^2}{(R^2+q^{-2}z^2)^2}.
$$
