For $V>0$, nonnegative <mass density> away from the origin is equivalent to $q^2\geq1/2$. Strict positivity requires $q^2>1/2$. This condition does not by itself guarantee a nonnegative <galactic distribution function>.

= Power-law gravitational potential
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential}
{title2=$\Phi=-Ar^{-\beta}$}

A radial <Newtonian gravitational potential> $\Phi=-Ar^{-\beta}$ produces inward acceleration $A\beta r^{-\beta-1}$. The point-mass case has $\beta=1$ and $A=GM$. Treating the <power-law gravitational potential> as prescribed does not require it to be sourced by the flowing gas.

= Gravitational quadrupole potential of a point-mass binary
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential}
{title2=$\Phi_2=-G\mu[3(\mathbf x\cdot\mathbf r)^2-x^2r^2]/(2x^5)$}

For a <binary star> viewed far from its <centre of mass>, let $\mathbf r$ be the separation and $\mu$ the <reduced mass>, $M=M_1+M_2$ the total <mass>, $r=|\mathbf r|$ and $x=|\mathbf x|$. The <Taylor expansion> of the point-mass <Newtonian gravitational potential> has zero dipole because $\sum_A M_A\mathbf x_A=0$. Its second moment is $\sum_A M_Ax_{Ai}x_{Aj}=\mu r_i r_j$, so
$$
\Phi=-\frac{GM}{x}-\frac{G\mu}{2x^5}\left[3(\mathbf x\cdot\mathbf r)^2-x^2r^2\right]+O(GMr^3/x^4).
$$
This holds for an instantaneous separation in any orbit, not only a <circular orbit>. The <mass quadrupole moment> convention $Q_{ij}=\mu(r_ir_j-r^2\delta_{ij}/3)$ gives $\Phi_2=-3GQ_{ij}x_ix_j/(2x^5)$.

= Flattened power-law gravitational potential
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential}
{title2=$\Phi=\Phi_0[R_0/(R^2+\lambda^2z^2)^{1/2}]^\beta$}

A flattened power-law <Newtonian gravitational potential> has equipotential spheroids $R^2+\lambda^2z^2=\text{constant}$. For $\Phi_0<0$ and $0<\beta<1$, its midplane <circular orbit> angular frequency satisfies $\Omega^2=-\beta\Phi_0R_0^\beta R^{-\beta-2}$.

= Density positivity for a flattened power-law potential
{parent=Flattened power-law gravitational potential}

Applying the <Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity> to a <flattened power-law gravitational potential> yields
$$
\rho=-\frac{\beta\Phi_0R_0^\beta}{4\pi G}\frac{(\lambda^2-\beta)R^2+\lambda^2[2-(\beta+1)\lambda^2]z^2}{(R^2+\lambda^2z^2)^{(\beta+4)/2}}.
$$
For $0<\beta<1$, nonnegative <mass density> everywhere outside the origin requires and is ensured by $\beta\leq\lambda^2\leq2/(\beta+1)$. The central density singularity is locally integrable and carries no point mass, although the total mass diverges at large radius in this scale-free model.

= Newtonian potential of a point mass
{title2=$\Phi(r)=-GM/r$}
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential}
{c}

= Gravitational potential
{synonym}

The <Newtonian potential of a point mass> $M$ at the origin is
$$
\Phi(r)=-\frac{GM}{r},\qquad r>0,\qquad \Phi(\infty)=0.
$$
It is the free-space <Green function of the Laplacian> with the normalization of the <Poisson equation for Newtonian gravity>. Differentiating gives inward <gravitational acceleration> $-GM/r^2$. With $z=r/r_0$, the normalized <Newtonian gravitational potential> is $\Phi/(GM/r_0)=-1/z$; at $z=1,2,4$ it equals $-1,-1/2,-1/4$.

For a radially outward launch from $r_0$, <conservation of mechanical energy> in the fixed-source <test particle> approximation gives the minimum <escape velocity>:
$$
\frac{E}{m}=\frac{v_0^2}{2}-\frac{GM}{r_0}=0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad v_{\mathrm{esc}}=\sqrt{\frac{2GM}{r_0}}.
$$
At this threshold the <speed> tends to zero only at infinite separation. The model neglects air resistance, external <tidal forces>, and <general relativity>. <University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/13.3 Gravitational Potential Energy and Total Energy>{ref}

A <circular orbit> at $r_0$ instead requires tangential <speed> $v_c=\sqrt{GM/r_0}$. Choosing units with $G=M=r_0=1$ gives $g_0=1$, $v_c=1$, and $v_{\mathrm{esc}}=\sqrt2\simeq1.41421$. A radial launch at <speed> $1$ reaches $r=2$ and returns; the same <speed> directed tangentially gives a <circular orbit>. <University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/13.4 Satellite Orbits and Energy>{ref}

\Image[gravitational-escape-energy.png]
{title=Energy balance and outward radial speeds below, at, and above escape}
{description=Left: at the <escape velocity>, positive <kinetic energy> exactly balances negative <Newtonian gravitational potential energy>. Right: radial launches at normalized <speeds> $1$, $\sqrt2$, and $1.6$ respectively turn at $r/r_0=2$, approach zero <speed> at infinity, and retain a positive limiting <speed>. The plotted trajectories use a fixed source and the <test particle> approximation.}

= Spherically symmetric potential
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential}

A spherically symmetric potential depends only on distance $r$ from one point. Its force is central, so angular momentum is conserved.

= Newtonian gravitational potential energy
{c}
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}

Two point masses $m_1,m_2$ separated by distance $r$ have <potential energy>
$$
V(r)=-\frac{Gm_1m_2}{r}.
$$
The corresponding attractive force is $-\nabla V$.

This is the Newtonian point-mass specialization of <gravitational energy>, with the reference value chosen at infinite separation.

= Spherical binding-energy mass integral
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential energy}
{title2=$W=-\dfrac G2\int_0^\infty M(r)^2r^{-2}\,dr$}

Assembling a spherical mass distribution in shells gives <gravitational potential energy> $W=-G\int M(r)\,dM(r)/r$. Integration by parts gives the displayed identity when $M(r)^2/r$ vanishes at both endpoints. It includes each interacting pair once; no extra factor of one half belongs in the original shell-assembly integral.

= Minimum-separation binding-energy bounds
{parent=Newtonian gravitational potential energy}
{title2=$B_0\leq rU\leq A_0$}

For positive <masses>, let $r$ be the actual minimum pairwise separation and use positive binding <energy> $U=\sum_{j<k}Gm_jm_k/r_{jk}$. If $m_{(1)},m_{(2)}$ are the smallest <masses>, then $Gm_{(1)}m_{(2)}\leq rU\leq G\sum_{j<k}m_jm_k$. Thus $U^{-1}$ is comparable to $r$ with constants depending only on the masses.

= Negative-energy minimum-separation upper bound
{parent=Minimum-separation binding-energy bounds}
{title2=$r\leq A_0/|E|$}

With $E=T-U<0$ and $T\geq0$, the positive binding <energy> satisfies $U\geq|E|$. The <minimum-separation binding-energy bounds> imply $r\leq A_0/|E|$. At least one pair stays close; this does not bound every particle's distance or exclude a <negative-energy gravitational collision>.

= Cavendish experiment
{parent=Newtonian gravitational field}
{c}
{wiki}

The <Cavendish experiment> makes the attraction predicted by <Newton's law of universal gravitation> measurable between laboratory <masses>. Two small test <masses> hang at the ends of a horizontal rod in a <Cavendish torsion balance>; nearby larger source <masses> exert a tiny <torque>. A thin suspension fiber supplies an opposing <torque> as it twists. Measuring its twist and calibrating its <torsion constant> lets an experimenter infer the gravitational <force> and, in a modern analysis, the <gravitational constant>. https://www.phys.uconn.edu/~hamilton/phys258/N/cav.pdf[University of Connecticut, Physics 258/259, The Cavendish Experiment: apparatus and equations of motion, pp. 1–3]{ref}

The beautiful feature is that the two horizontal <forces> can cancel as vectors while their <torques> add. The instrument converts weak attraction into a rotation, with sensitivity set by the fiber's <torsion constant>. Earth's much larger downward <gravitational weight> is supported by the suspension rather than supplying the intended <torque> about its vertical axis. A laboratory measurement can therefore probe the same <Newtonian gravitational field> that governs the <two-body problem>.

Henry Cavendish's original paper was \i[Experiments to determine the Density of the Earth], published in \i[Philosophical Transactions], volume 88 (1798), pp. 469–526, and read on 21 June 1798. On p. 469 he credits John Michell with the apparatus idea and explains that Michell died before using it; the instrument reached Cavendish through Francis John Hyde Wollaston. Cavendish's stated aim was Earth's mean <mass density>. Calling the experiment a determination of the <gravitational constant> is a modern interpretation, not Cavendish's own formulation of his result. https://www.famaf.unc.edu.ar/~wolf/FisicaI/Phil.%20Trans.%20R.%20Soc.%20Lond.-1798-Cavendish-rstl.1798.0022.pdf[Cavendish (1798), original paper, opening p. 469; DOI 10.1098/rstl.1798.0022]{ref}

The connection between Earth's mean <mass density> and the <gravitational constant> follows in a spherical, nonrotating model of <Earth>. With Earth <mass> $M_E$, radius $R_E$, and surface <gravitational acceleration> $g$, the <spherical shell theorem> and <Newton's law of universal gravitation> give
$$
g=\frac{GM_E}{R_E^2},\qquad \overline\rho_E=\frac{3M_E}{4\pi R_E^3},\qquad G=\frac{3g}{4\pi R_E\overline\rho_E}.
$$
Thus a local comparison of gravitational attractions, together with knowledge of Earth's size and surface <gravitational acceleration>, reaches a property of the entire planet. These are modern equations explaining the connection, not equations quoted from Cavendish's paper.

For a worked example, use an explicitly simplified two-near-pair model. Each test <point mass> is $m$, each source <point mass> is $M$, and each <lever arm> is $L$, so the rod's full <length> is $2L$. Neglect the rod's <mass>. At zero twist put the test <masses> at $(-L,0)$ and $(L,0)$, and the sources at $(-L,-r)$ and $(L,r)$. Here $r$ is the near-pair center separation. Ignore each source's attraction on the test <mass> at the opposite end. Evaluate the two near-pair <forces> at zero twist and treat their <torque> as constant for a small twist, requiring $L|\theta|\ll r$. This is a teaching model, not a precision model of Cavendish's actual apparatus.

By <Newton's law of universal gravitation>, each retained <force> has magnitude $F=GmM/r^2$. The left <force> points down and the right <force> points up. Using <torque> about the suspension axis, both contributions are counterclockwise when viewed from above:
$$
\tau_{z,\mathrm{left}}=(-L)(-F)=LF,\qquad \tau_{z,\mathrm{right}}=LF,\qquad |\tau_g|=2L\frac{GmM}{r^2}.
$$
The net retained horizontal <force> is zero, yet the net <torque> is positive. Moving the sources to $(-L,r)$ and $(L,-r)$ reverses both <forces> and hence the <torque>. In a real apparatus, the opposite source also attracts each test <mass>; that correction is explicitly included in the university derivation. https://www.phys.uconn.edu/~hamilton/phys258/N/cav.pdf[University of Connecticut, The Cavendish Experiment, p. 2, equation (1): opposite-source correction]{ref}

\Image[cavendish-torsion-balance-schematic.png]
{title=Two near-pair forces give the same counterclockwise torque about a vertical suspension fiber}

For illustrative parameters, take $m=0.010\,\mathrm{kg}$, $M=1.0\,\mathrm{kg}$, $r=0.050\,\mathrm m$, $L=0.10\,\mathrm m$, and <torsion constant> $\kappa=1.0\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{N\,m/rad}$. Use $G=6.67430\times10^{-11}\,\mathrm{m^3\,kg^{-1}\,s^{-2}}$, the central value in NIST's 2022 CODATA table; that table gives standard uncertainty $0.00015\times10^{-11}\,\mathrm{m^3\,kg^{-1}\,s^{-2}}$. This is an input for the example, not a measurement made with these assumed parameters. <Fundamental Physical Constants — Complete Listing by National Institute of Standards and Technology and others>{ref}
$$
F=\frac{(6.67430\times10^{-11})(0.010)(1.0)}{(0.050)^2}=2.66972\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm N,\qquad |\tau_g|=2(0.10)F=5.33944\times10^{-11}\,\mathrm{N\,m}.
$$
This sub-nanonewton <force> produces a measurable twist in the <Cavendish torsion balance>. The displayed digits make the toy calculation reproducible; they do not describe experimental precision.

= Cavendish torsion balance
{parent=Cavendish experiment}
{c}

A <Cavendish torsion balance> is a <torsion balance> arranged to measure gravitational <torque> from nearby source <masses>. Its suspended horizontal rod rotates about a vertical fiber. In the linear elastic regime, a twist <angle> $\theta$, measured from the fiber's untwisted orientation, gives a restoring <torque> $\tau_{\mathrm{fiber}}=-\kappa\theta$, where $\kappa>0$ is the <torsion constant>. The minus sign is essential: the fiber resists the twist. Under an approximately constant gravitational <torque>, the rotational equation using <moment of inertia> $I$ and <angular acceleration> $\ddot\theta$ is
$$
I\ddot\theta=\tau_g-\kappa\theta,\qquad \theta_{\mathrm{eq}}=\frac{\tau_g}{\kappa}.
$$
At <equilibrium>, the two <torques> balance. The <angle> is a displacement from the fiber's reference orientation, not the largest excursion during an <oscillation>. https://www.phys.uconn.edu/~hamilton/phys258/N/cav.pdf[University of Connecticut, The Cavendish Experiment, pp. 2–3: restoring torque and equilibrium deflection]{ref}

For the toy parameters in the <Cavendish experiment>, the predicted <equilibrium> twist is
$$
\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}=\frac{5.33944\times10^{-11}}{1.0\times10^{-8}}=0.00533944\,\mathrm{rad}\simeq0.3059^\circ.
$$
Here $L|\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}|/r\simeq0.0107$, consistent with using a small geometric displacement in the toy model, but not an estimate of its total <measurement error>. Reversing the source positions ideally takes the <equilibrium> <angle> from $+\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}$ to $-\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}$: the magnitude of the change is $2|\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}|\simeq0.6119^\circ$. A stable zero offset then cancels in the difference. A real reading may track a reflected light spot; the <law of reflection> doubles a mirror's rotation in the outgoing beam, so the spot's angular change must not be confused with the rod's <angle>. https://sciencedemonstrations.fas.harvard.edu/presentations/cavendish-experiment[Harvard Natural Sciences Lecture Demonstrations, Cavendish Experiment: mirror readout and source reversal]{ref}

How can the fiber's <torsion constant> be found without already knowing the gravitational <force>? Measure the <oscillation period> and calculate the <moment of inertia>. For the same toy rod, neglecting its <mass> and treating both test <masses> as <point masses>, $I=mL^2+mL^2=2mL^2$. With the source <torque> absent or treated as constant, let $\phi=\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}$ be a small departure from <equilibrium>. Neglect <damping> and any change of gravitational <torque> with <angle>. The rotational equation becomes <simple harmonic motion>:
$$
I\ddot\phi+\kappa\phi=0,\qquad \phi(t)=A\cos(\omega t+\delta),\qquad \omega=\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{I}},\qquad T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac I\kappa},\qquad \kappa=\frac{4\pi^2I}{T^2}.
$$
Here $A$ is the angular amplitude, $\delta$ specifies the initial phase, and $\omega$ is the <angular frequency>. A softer fiber gives a larger <equilibrium> twist but also a longer <oscillation period>: greater sensitivity costs observation time. An undamped <oscillation> does not settle at <equilibrium>; <damping> is needed for settling. https://www.phys.uconn.edu/~hamilton/phys258/N/cav.pdf[University of Connecticut, The Cavendish Experiment, p. 3, equation (7): period calibration neglecting damping]{ref}

The toy <moment of inertia> and undamped <oscillation period> are
$$
I=2(0.010)(0.10)^2=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2},\qquad T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{2.0\times10^{-4}}{1.0\times10^{-8}}}\simeq888.5766\,\mathrm s\simeq14.81\,\mathrm{min}.
$$
Timing this <oscillation period> would recover the assumed <torsion constant>. The units check explains the result: <torque> divided by <torsion constant> is an <angle> in <radians>, and $I/\kappa$ has units of <time> squared when <radians> are treated as dimensionless in <SI>.

\Image[torsion-balance-period-calibration.png]
{title=Undamped small torsional oscillations calibrate the restoring torque: a softer fiber has a longer period}

Combining the <equilibrium> twist with the period calibration, the two-near-pair model would infer the <gravitational constant> as
$$
G=\frac{\kappa|\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}|r^2}{2LmM}=\frac{4\pi^2Lr^2|\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}|}{MT^2}.
$$
The test <mass> cancels in the second expression because it enters both the gravitational <torque> and the toy <moment of inertia>. If the measured quantity is the magnitude $|\Delta\theta|$ of the change between opposite source positions, use $|\theta_{\mathrm{eq}}|=|\Delta\theta|/2$. Substituting the toy twist and <oscillation period> returns the input $G$; it is a consistency check, not independent experimental evidence.

The sensitivity of a <Cavendish torsion balance> also explains its practical difficulty. Air motion can exert unwanted <torque>; unequal <temperatures> can drive that motion. Cavendish discusses this effect on p. 471 and describes observing from outside a closed room to avoid disturbing the instrument. https://www.infinitoteatrodelcosmo.it/wp-content/uploads/2021/07/cavendish-1997-xxi-experiments-to-determine-the-density-of-the-earth.pdf[Cavendish (1798), original paper, p. 471: temperature-driven air currents and remote observation]{ref} Mechanical vibrations can excite the same slow <oscillations> used to calibrate the <torsion constant>, while changes in the fiber or source separations alter the inferred <force>. Precision therefore depends on resolving the <equilibrium> <angle>, calibrating the full suspended <moment of inertia> and <torsion constant>, and modeling the actual source geometry. The two-near-pair model omits opposite-source attraction, rod contributions, finite-size effects, <damping>, and the variation of gravitational <torque> as the rod turns. https://sciencedemonstrations.fas.harvard.edu/presentations/cavendish-experiment[Harvard Cavendish demonstration: sensitivity, settling, and separation-dependent measurement]{ref}

= Torsion balance
{parent=Classical mechanics}

A <torsion balance> measures a weak <torque> by suspending a body from a thin fiber and reading its twist <angle>. The fiber supports the body's <gravitational weight> while permitting rotation about the suspension axis. In the range of <linear elasticity>, a twist $\theta$ in <radians>, measured from the untwisted reference orientation, produces a restoring <torque>
$$
\tau_{\mathrm{fiber}}=-\kappa\theta,\qquad \kappa>0.
$$
The <torsion constant> $\kappa$ sets the sensitivity. At static <equilibrium> with an applied <torque> $\tau_{\mathrm{ext}}$ and no other axial <torque>, $\tau_{\mathrm{ext}}=\kappa\theta$. A perpendicular <force> $F$ at <lever arm> $a$ therefore gives $\theta=aF/\kappa$. The <torsion balance> measures a turning effect: a <force> whose line of action passes through the suspension axis gives no axial <torque>. The <Cavendish torsion balance> is the gravitational application; <Coulomb's law> supplies an electrical one. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Coulombs-Law-Apparatus-Manual-ES-9070.pdf[Bruce Lee, PASCO ES-9070 Coulomb Balance manual, pp. 1 and 7: twist measurement of electric force]{ref}

\Image[torsion-balance-readout.png]
{title=Torsion balance readout: fiber twist, force geometry, and returning the body to a fixed angle}

The original schematic separates two ways of reading the same <torque>. In deflection operation the top attachment stays fixed and the body rotates. In null operation the experimenter rotates the top attachment by $\alpha$ while returning the body's laboratory <angle> $\beta$ to its original value. If the initially untwisted configuration has $\alpha=\beta=0$, the fiber twist is $\theta=\beta-\alpha$, not $\beta$ alone. Thus
$$
\tau_{\mathrm{fiber}}=-\kappa(\beta-\alpha),\qquad \tau_{\mathrm{ext}}=\kappa(\beta-\alpha).
$$
For a positive applied <torque>, holding $\beta=0$ requires $\alpha=-\tau_{\mathrm{ext}}/\kappa$. Keeping the body at the same <angle> keeps the <lever arm> and source geometry fixed, making comparison of different applied <forces> easier. Either readout still requires a calibrated <torsion constant> and a stable reference orientation.

An illustrative calculation shows the amplification: with $a=0.050\,\mathrm m$, $F=2.0\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm N$, and $\kappa=1.0\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{N\,m/rad}$, the applied <torque> is $1.0\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm{N\,m}$ and the <equilibrium> twist is $0.010\,\mathrm{rad}\simeq0.573^\circ$. These are stipulated values, not measured data. Reducing the <torsion constant> tenfold increases this twist tenfold, provided the fiber remains in its calibrated <linear elasticity> range. The cost is a slower response, explained by the <oscillation period> in <torsion constant>.

A concrete laboratory experiment is PASCO's Coulomb balance: level its counterbalanced rod, discharge both conducting spheres, and set the unloaded zero. Charge the spheres at maximum separation; switch off the charging supply. Move the second sphere to a known center separation $R$, then turn the suspension dial until the rod returns to its index mark. Record the dial change and $R$, recharge, and repeat at several separations. The manual recommends a draft-free room, clean insulating supports, and recharging before each reading: leakage, especially in humid conditions, otherwise changes the <electric charges> during the sequence. At close separations the conducting spheres redistribute charge, so the <point charge> approximation needs correction. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Coulombs-Law-Apparatus-Manual-ES-9070.pdf[Bruce Lee, PASCO ES-9070 manual, pp. 4–8: setup, force-versus-distance procedure, and corrections]{ref}

For constant <electric charges> and a fixed perpendicular <lever arm>, the <Coulomb's law> prediction is $|\theta|\propto R^{-2}$. Plotting the <logarithm> of twist magnitude against the <logarithm> of separation gives $\log|\theta|=\text{constant}-2\log R$, so the predicted slope is $-2$. Absolute <force> measurement additionally needs the <torsion constant>; comparing ratios can eliminate it if it remains stable. Repeating a reference separation between other settings distinguishes a separation effect from a changing signal. A declining reading at the reference separation would invalidate the assumption of unchanged <electric charges>, even if a distance plot looks smooth.

The historical electrical example is Coulomb's first memoir of 1785. His apparatus enclosed a suspended needle with a small ball and a second ball; an adjustable head twisted the suspension. After charging the balls by contact, he reported angular separations $36^\circ$, $18^\circ$, and $8.5^\circ$ with head settings $0^\circ$, $126^\circ$, and $567^\circ$. The total twists were approximately $36^\circ$, $144^\circ$, and $576^\circ$: fourfold restoring <torque> accompanied approximately halved separation. These are his reported observations, not data from our diagrams. https://cnum.cnam.fr/pgi/redir.php?ident=8CA121-1&onglet=c[Coulomb, Premier mémoire (1785), reprinted in Mémoires de Coulomb, pp. 104–109, CNAM digitization]{ref} A precise reconstruction must use center distances and the changing <lever arm>, rather than identify angular separation exactly with distance; Alberto A. Martínez's replication explicitly analyzes that geometry. https://martinezwritings.com/m/Coulomb_files/CoulombExperiment-1.pdf[Martínez, Replication of Coulomb's Torsion Balance Experiment (2006), pp. 522–525]{ref}

Sensitivity also exposes changes of the apparatus itself. Fleischer and colleagues' cryogenic <torsion balance> used a temperature-controlled enclosure to reduce vacuum-vessel top-plate <temperature> fluctuations to approximately $\pm0.01\,\mathrm K$, limiting changes in suspension geometry from <thermal expansion>. They used a mild bake-out near $40^\circ\mathrm C$ to speed initial fiber relaxation and reduce slow zero motion. Their optical readout compared the pendulum mirror with an internal reference mirror to reject shared optical motion. Room-temperature <thermal radiation> entering through the optical opening heated the pendulum; a cold filter reduced that load. The authors could not directly measure the pendulum's <temperature> without disturbing it, and explicitly distinguished the cold shield's <temperature> from the body's. Cooling therefore requires checking the actual noise and thermal environment, rather than assuming that the fiber and body share the shield's <temperature>. https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.02890[Fleischer et al., A Cryogenic Torsion Balance Using a Liquid-Cryogen Free, Ultra-Low Vibration Cryostat (2022), §§II B–C and III A]{ref}

A useful control strategy is to record unloaded readings and <temperature> before and after each signal sequence, recalibrate the <torsion constant> under the same conditions, and compare forward and reverse settings. The algebra of <torsion balance zero drift> shows precisely which offsets such comparisons can cancel. A fine angular scale alone does not establish a small <measurement error>.

= Torsion constant
{parent=Torsion balance}
{title2=$\kappa$}

= Torsional spring constant
{synonym}

The <torsion constant>, or <torsional spring constant>, is the positive restoring <torque> per unit twist <angle> of a complete suspension in its <linear elasticity> range:
$$
\tau_{\mathrm{fiber}}=-\kappa\theta,\qquad \kappa=-\frac{d\tau_{\mathrm{fiber}}}{d\theta}>0.
$$
Its <SI> unit is $\mathrm{N\,m/rad}$. The minus sign describes restoration toward the untwisted orientation. The derivative specifies a local stiffness if the response is not globally linear; using one constant over a finite range additionally requires a straight calibration curve. Return to the same zero after unloading tests recoverable <elastic deformation>; repeat loading in both directions to check whether the response depends on its previous loading. Staying below permanent deformation is necessary, but does not alone prove proportionality between <torque> and twist.

For an ideal uniform circular wire of <length> $\ell$, radius $r$, and <shear modulus> $G_s$, the <filament torsional stiffness> is $C=G_sJ_p$, with polar <second moment of area> $J_p=\pi r^4/2$. Uniform twist gives $\theta/\ell$ as the twist per unit <length>, so
$$
\kappa=\frac C\ell=\frac{\pi G_s r^4}{2\ell},\qquad U=\frac12\kappa\theta^2.
$$
Here $U$ is stored <elastic energy>. Doubling the wire's radius multiplies the ideal <torsion constant> by $16$; doubling its <length> halves it. This explains why a long thin fiber is useful for weak <torque> measurement. The formula assumes <linear elasticity> with uniform material and circular section; it does not calibrate arbitrary ribbons, clamps, or a compound suspension. Within this ideal model, the <equipartition theorem> predicts an equilibrium thermal mean-square angular displacement $\langle\theta^2\rangle=k_B\mathcal T/\kappa$, where $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant> and $\mathcal T$ the uniform absolute <temperature>. A softer suspension magnifies both the signal and its <thermal fluctuations>.

Static calibration supplies known <torques> without assuming the unknown interaction being measured. Apply a known perpendicular <force> $F$ at <lever arm> $a$, record the change from the unloaded twist, and use
$$
\tau_{\mathrm{cal}}=aF,\qquad \kappa=\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{cal}}}{\Delta\theta}.
$$
Several positive and negative settings test proportionality; fit the slope of applied <torque> against twist and retain a possible intercept. A nonzero intercept must not be silently absorbed into the slope. Convert degrees to <radians> before reporting $\mathrm{N\,m/rad}$. PASCO's procedure turns the Coulomb balance on its side and adds small known <masses> to generate a <gravitational weight> at the sphere's <lever arm>, returning the rod to its index mark by turning the dial. Its tabulated coefficient is <force> per degree: multiply by the <lever arm> and $180/\pi$ to obtain our <torsion constant>. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Coulombs-Law-Apparatus-Manual-ES-9070.pdf[Bruce Lee, PASCO ES-9070 manual, pp. 9–10: mass calibration and force-per-degree convention]{ref}

\Image[torsion-balance-calibration.png]
{title=Toy torsion calibrations: static slope and a period corrected for viscous damping}

The figure's ideal static calibration uses $\kappa=1.0\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{N\,m/rad}$: an applied <torque> of $2.0\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm{N\,m}$ gives $0.020\,\mathrm{rad}$. Both signs lie on the same straight line. The plotted points are calculated examples, not experimental observations; they cannot demonstrate a real fiber's linearity.

Dynamic calibration instead measures the <oscillation period> $T_0$ with known total suspended <moment of inertia> $I$. For negligible <damping>, small free <oscillations> about a fixed reference obey <simple harmonic motion>:
$$
I\ddot\theta+\kappa\theta=0,\qquad T_0=2\pi\sqrt{\frac I\kappa},\qquad \kappa=\frac{4\pi^2I}{T_0^2}.
$$
Include the rod, mirrors, and any attachments in $I$ unless their contributions are demonstrably negligible. Keep the applied <torque> absent or independent of <angle>. If an external <torque> has derivative $g_\tau=d\tau_{\mathrm{ext}}/d\theta$ at <equilibrium>, linearization gives effective stiffness $\kappa-g_\tau$: timing alone then measures that combination, and stability requires $\kappa-g_\tau>0$. A constant applied <torque> merely shifts <equilibrium>. Non-negligible <damping> changes the measured period; <damped torsional oscillation> gives a correction under a specified damping model. Agreement between static and dynamic calibrations is a useful check on the assumed <moment of inertia>, reference stability, and restoring law, rather than a reason to ignore their assumptions.

= Damped torsional oscillation
{parent=Torsion constant}
{title2=$I\ddot\theta+b\dot\theta+\kappa\theta=0$}

A <damped torsional oscillation> is rotation about a fiber's fixed reference orientation with a restoring <torque> and dissipative <torque>. In the ideal viscous model, <damping> contributes $-b\dot\theta$ with constant $b\geq0$. This is a rotational <damped harmonic oscillator>. For known constant <moment of inertia> $I$ and <torsion constant> $\kappa$, define $\gamma=b/(2I)$. In the oscillatory regime $\gamma^2<\kappa/I$,
$$
\theta(t)=A e^{-\gamma t}\cos(\omega_dt+\varphi),\qquad \omega_d^2=\frac\kappa I-\gamma^2,\qquad T_d=\frac{2\pi}{\omega_d}.
$$
$A$ and $\varphi$ are set by the initial state. Successive positive peaks measured from the same <equilibrium> have magnitudes $p_n$ and $p_{n+1}$; their ratio gives $\delta=\ln(p_n/p_{n+1})=\gamma T_d$. Consequently a measured damped <oscillation period> and decay together recover
$$
\boxed{\kappa=I\left[\left(\frac{2\pi}{T_d}\right)^2+\gamma^2\right]=\frac I{T_d^2}(4\pi^2+\delta^2).}
$$
Using only $4\pi^2I/T_d^2$ underestimates the <torsion constant> in this model. The toy curve in <torsion constant> sets $I=2.0\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$, $\kappa=1.0\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{N\,m/rad}$, and $\gamma=0.020\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. It gives $T_0\simeq88.86\,\mathrm s$, $T_d\simeq92.64\,\mathrm s$, and $\delta\simeq1.853$. Ignoring the decay recovers $9.2\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm{N\,m/rad}$, an $8\%$ underestimate; including it recovers the stipulated <torsion constant>.

These equations assume a single rotational mode, a stationary zero, <linear elasticity>, and viscous <damping>. Internal fiber losses need not behave as a constant viscous coefficient: Fleischer and colleagues model their precision apparatus using a loss angle. Their response is evidence for specifying a damping model, not for applying the viscous correction universally. https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.02890[Fleischer et al. (2022), §I, equation (1): thermal torque noise and internal damping represented by a loss angle]{ref}

= Torsion balance zero drift
{parent=Torsion balance}
{title2=$\beta_{\mathrm{eq}}=\beta_0(t)+\tau_{\mathrm{ext}}/\kappa$}

<Torsion balance zero drift> is slow motion of the unloaded reference <angle> $\beta_0(t)$ during a measurement. If the <torsion constant> remains fixed, a constant applied <torque> gives $\beta_{\mathrm{eq}}=\beta_0(t)+\tau_{\mathrm{ext}}/\kappa$. For two equal and opposite applied <torques> measured at the same reference zero, their <equilibrium> <angles> differ by $2\tau_{\mathrm{ext}}/\kappa$. A stable offset cancels; a changing zero contributes $\beta_0(t_+)-\beta_0(t_-)$ and does not cancel automatically.

A simple symmetric sequence makes the limitation explicit. Let $b_1,b_2,b_3$ be settled <equilibrium> <angles> for applied <torques> $+\tau,-\tau,+\tau$ at equally spaced times $t_0-h,t_0,t_0+h$. If the unloaded zero is linear in <time>, $\beta_0(t)=u+vt$, and $\kappa$ and $\tau$ remain fixed, then
$$
\widehat\tau=\frac\kappa2\left(\frac{b_1+b_3}{2}-b_2\right)=\tau.
$$
The arithmetic average of the outer zero values equals the middle zero, so both the constant offset and linear drift cancel. Curved zero motion, unequal time spacing, changing <torsion constant>, changing signal strength, or unsettled <oscillations> violate these assumptions. This is an algebraic control example, not a claim that every <torsion balance> follows a linear drift law.

= Degree of freedom
{parent=Classical mechanics}

= Degrees of freedom
{synonym}

A degree of freedom is an independent coordinate needed to specify a mechanical configuration after all <mechanical constraints> have been imposed.

= Constraint
{disambiguate=mechanics}
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{wiki}

= Mechanical constraint
{synonym}

= Constraint
{synonym}

A mechanical constraint restricts the allowed configurations or velocities of a system and reduces its <degrees of freedom>.

= Bead on a parabolic wire
{parent=Constraint (mechanics)}
{title2=$T=2\sqrt{2a/g}\int_{-1}^1\sqrt{\frac{1+(h/a)u^2}{1-u^2}}\,du$}

For a smooth stationary wire $z=x^2/(4a)$ and release from rest at height $h$, the <normal reaction> does no work. The energy equation is $[1+x^2/(4a^2)]\dot x^2/2+gx^2/(4a)=gh$. Integrating the travel time between turning points gives the displayed period. Its small-amplitude expansion is $T=2\pi\sqrt{2a/g}[1+h/(4a)+O((h/a)^2)]$. The mass cancels because both gravitational and inertial forces are proportional to it.

= Constraint surface
{parent=Constraint (mechanics)}
{title2=$C_a=0$}

The common zero locus of constraint functions $C_a$ in <phase space> is the constraint surface. A physical trajectory lies on this surface, but off-surface identities are often needed when analyzing a <constraint algebra>.

= Holonomic constraint
{parent=Constraint (mechanics)}
{wiki}

A <holonomic constraint> is a relation $f(q,t)=0$ involving generalized coordinates and possibly time, rather than a genuinely nonintegrable velocity relation. Independent smooth constraints of full row rank define a local configuration submanifold. Ideal constraint forces are normal to its tangent virtual displacements and can be represented by <Lagrange multipliers>. A time-dependent <holonomic constraint> need not conserve mechanical <energy>.

= First-class constraint
{parent=Constraint (mechanics)}
{title2=$\varphi_i\approx0$}

= First class
{synonym}

A first-class constraint has <Poisson brackets> with all constraints that vanish on the constraint surface. For a regular constrained <Hamiltonian> system, first-class constraints generate <gauge transformations>; each independent constraint and its gauge direction remove one <canonical pair>.

= Regular first-class phase-space reduction
{parent=First-class constraint}
{title2=$\dim\Gamma_{\mathrm{phys}}=2N-2n$}

For $n$ independent regular <first-class constraints> in a $2N$-dimensional phase space, imposing the equations removes $n$ dimensions and quotienting independent gauge orbits removes another $n$. Reducibility, singular strata or vanishing gauge directions can invalidate that elementary count. Closure of the <constraint algebra> is necessary but does not by itself establish regularity.

= Canonical gauge transformation
{parent=First-class constraint}
{title2=$\delta F=\{F,\epsilon^i\varphi_i\}$}

A <first-class constraint> generates an infinitesimal <canonical transformation> $\delta F=\{F,\epsilon^i\varphi_i\}$. For the <phase-space action> $I=\int(p\dot q-\lambda^i\varphi_i)dt$, the multiplier transformation $\delta\lambda^k=\dot\epsilon^k-\lambda^i\epsilon^jC_{ij}{}^k$ cancels the bulk variation when $\{\varphi_i,\varphi_j\}=C_{ij}{}^k\varphi_k$. The remaining temporal boundary term vanishes for parameters that vanish at the endpoints.

= Constraint algebra
{parent=First-class constraint}
{title2=$\{\varphi_i,\varphi_j\}=f_{ij}{}^k\varphi_k$}

The <Poisson brackets> of <first-class constraints> close on the constraints. When the constraints are independent and the coefficients are constant, the <Jacobi identity for the Poisson bracket> makes the coefficients <structure constants> of a <Lie algebra>.

= Structure functions of a constraint algebra
{parent=Constraint algebra}
{title2=$C_{ij}{}^k(q,p)$}

The coefficients $C_{ij}{}^k(q,p)$ in $\{\varphi_i,\varphi_j\}=C_{ij}{}^k\varphi_k$ are structure functions. A finite real span of <first-class constraints> is a <Lie algebra> when it closes with constant coefficients. General structure functions instead describe closure with phase-space-dependent coefficients, while still permitting <canonical gauge transformations>.

= Position
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{wiki=Position_(geometry)}

Position specifies the location of a particle relative to a coordinate system.

= Plane polar coordinates
{title2=$(r,\theta)$}
{parent=Position}

Plane polar coordinates describe a point by its radial distance $r$ and polar angle $\theta$.

These are the planar <polar coordinates>; the general coordinate-system definition and transformations belong to that canonical concept.

= Acceleration in polar coordinates
{parent=Plane polar coordinates}
{title2=$\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2)\widehat{\mathbf r}+(2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta)\widehat{\boldsymbol\theta}$}

Differentiating $\mathbf r=r\widehat{\mathbf r}$ twice and using $\dot{\widehat{\mathbf r}}=\dot\theta\widehat{\boldsymbol\theta}$ and $\dot{\widehat{\boldsymbol\theta}}=-\dot\theta\widehat{\mathbf r}$ gives the displayed <acceleration>. For a <central force>, its angular component vanishes, so $r^2\dot\theta$ is constant and expresses <conservation of angular momentum>.

= Velocity
{parent=Position}
{title2=$\mathbf v=d\mathbf r/dt$}
{wiki}

<Velocity> is the <vector> giving the rate of change of <position> with <time>, measured in a specified <reference frame>. For a differentiable path $\mathbf r(t)$, its instantaneous <velocity> and its average <velocity> between two <times> are
$$
\mathbf v(t)=\frac{d\mathbf r}{dt},\qquad \mathbf v_{\mathrm{avg}}=\frac{\mathbf r(t_2)-\mathbf r(t_1)}{t_2-t_1},\qquad t_2>t_1.
$$
The <displacement> in the numerator retains direction. In <Cartesian coordinates>, each <vector component> is a <derivative>, for example $v_x=dx/dt$; its sign tells whether the corresponding coordinate is increasing or decreasing. The <SI> unit of <velocity> is $\mathrm{m/s}$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/3-2-instantaneous-velocity-and-speed[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics Volume 1, section 3.2: instantaneous velocity and speed]{ref}

For a concrete reversal, let $x(t)=(1\,\mathrm m)+(1\,\mathrm{m/s^2})t^2-(2\,\mathrm{m/s})t$ for $0\le t\le2\,\mathrm s$. Its signed <velocity> is $v_x(t)=(2\,\mathrm{m/s^2})t-2\,\mathrm{m/s}$: at $t=0$ it is $-2\,\mathrm{m/s}$, at $t=1\,\mathrm s$ it is zero, and at $t=2\,\mathrm s$ it is $+2\,\mathrm{m/s}$. The opposite endpoint <velocities> describe opposite directions, although the endpoint <speeds> agree. The <position> moves from $1\,\mathrm m$ to $0$ and back to $1\,\mathrm m$.

Recovering <displacement> from a measured <velocity> is an <integration> problem: $\mathbf r(t_2)-\mathbf r(t_1)=\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\mathbf v(t)\,dt$, with the <integral> taken component by component. In the reversal example, $\int_0^{2\,\mathrm s}v_x(t)\,dt=0$: the negative and positive contributions cancel. This distinction is useful in navigation, where the final <position> depends on direction as well as <speed>.

= Normal velocity
{parent=Velocity}
{title2=$v_n=v\cdot n$}

The <normal velocity> is the scalar component of a <velocity> in a specified unit-normal direction. Its sign depends on the orientation of the normal. At a material interface, no penetration equates the fluid and interface <normal velocities>; it does not require their tangential components to agree. <Surface acoustic impedance> uses a fixed inward normal-velocity convention to determine the sign of energy absorbed by a boundary.

= Speed
{parent=Velocity}
{title2=$v=|\mathbf v|$}
{wiki}

<Speed> is the nonnegative magnitude of <velocity>, a <scalar> rather than a <vector>. In orthonormal <Cartesian coordinates>,
$$
v(t)=|\mathbf v(t)|=\sqrt{v_x(t)^2+v_y(t)^2+v_z(t)^2}.
$$
Its <SI> unit is $\mathrm{m/s}$. In the reversal example under <velocity>, the endpoint <velocities> $-2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ and $+2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ both have <speed> $2\,\mathrm{m/s}$, but the <speed> falls to zero between them. A <speed> reading alone therefore cannot tell which way the body is moving.

For a smooth path, the total distance traveled is its <arc length>, $L=\int_{t_1}^{t_2}v(t)\,dt$. Unlike the <integral> of a signed <velocity> component, this <integral> cannot cancel outward and return motion. The reversal example travels $2\,\mathrm m$ in $2\,\mathrm s$, giving an average <speed> of $1\,\mathrm{m/s}$, while its average <velocity> is zero. This makes <speed> useful for estimating travel duration or fuel use, while <velocity> also tracks progress toward a destination. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/3-2-instantaneous-velocity-and-speed[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, section 3.2: distance traveled versus displacement]{ref}

= Acceleration
{parent=Velocity}
{title2=$\mathbf a=d\mathbf v/dt$}
{wiki}

<Acceleration> is the <time> <derivative> of <velocity>, equivalently the second <derivative> of <position>:
$$
\mathbf a(t)=\frac{d\mathbf v}{dt}=\frac{d^2\mathbf r}{dt^2}.
$$
It is a <vector> with <SI> unit $\mathrm{m/s^2}$. A change in direction produces <acceleration> even if <speed> stays constant. For example, a car rounding a bend at steady <speed> needs an inward resultant <force> by <Newton's second law>. Thus <acceleration> does not mean only speeding up. When $v>0$, the <dot product> gives $dv/dt=\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf a/v$: a perpendicular <acceleration> changes direction without changing <speed> at that instant.

Take counterclockwise <circular motion> about the origin with <radius> $R=2\,\mathrm m$ and constant <angular speed> $\omega=0.5\,\mathrm{rad/s}$. The <position>, <velocity> and <acceleration> are
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf r(t)&=(R\cos(\omega t),R\sin(\omega t)),\\
\mathbf v(t)&=(-R\omega\sin(\omega t),R\omega\cos(\omega t)),\\
\mathbf a(t)&=(-R\omega^2\cos(\omega t),-R\omega^2\sin(\omega t))=-\omega^2\mathbf r(t).
\end{aligned}
$$
The <speed> is $R\omega=1\,\mathrm{m/s}$ throughout, while the inward <centripetal acceleration> has magnitude $R\omega^2=v^2/R=0.5\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$. At the four cardinal points, reached at $t=0,\pi,2\pi,3\pi\,\mathrm s$, the <velocities> in $\mathrm{m/s}$ are $(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1),(1,0)$; the <accelerations> in $\mathrm{m/s^2}$ are $(-0.5,0),(0,-0.5),(0.5,0),(0,0.5)$. The <vectors> turn together, remaining perpendicular. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/4-4-uniform-and-nonuniform-circular-motion[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, section 4.4: uniform circular motion]{ref}

\Image[constant-speed-circular-acceleration.png]
{title=Constant speed with changing velocity and inward acceleration at four points of a circle}

The proof outline is just two applications of the <chain rule> to $\mathbf r(t)$; $\sin^2(\omega t)+\cos^2(\omega t)=1$ then fixes both magnitudes. The usefulness is geometric: turning requires a <force> even when a <speed> reading remains steady, and doubling <speed> at fixed <radius> quadruples the required <centripetal acceleration>. The same mechanism appears in a <circular orbit>.

Historically, Newton's \i[Principia], Book I, Section II, Proposition IV, relates the inward <forces> of uniform <circular motion> to the squared arcs traversed in equal <times>, divided by their <radii>. In modern notation, its dependence on <speed> and <radius> is $v^2/R$; Newton presents a geometric argument rather than the coordinate <derivatives> above. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Mathematical_Principles_of_Natural_Philosophy_(1729)/Book_1/Section_2[Newton, Principia, Book I, Proposition IV; Motte's 1729 translation]{ref}

= Jerk
{parent=Acceleration}
{disambiguate=kinematics}
{title2=$\mathbf j=d\mathbf a/dt=d^3\mathbf r/dt^3$}
{wiki=Jerk_(physics)}

= Kinematic jerk
{synonym}

The kinematic jerk is the time derivative of <acceleration>. For a <Kepler orbit> with relative position $\mathbf r$, relative velocity $\mathbf v$, $r=|\mathbf r|$, $\dot r=\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf v/r$ and total <mass> $M$, differentiation of <Newton's law of universal gravitation> gives $\mathbf j=-GM(\mathbf v-3\dot r\,\mathbf r/r)/r^3$.

= Constant speed
{parent=Velocity}

A path has <constant speed> when the magnitude of its <velocity> does not vary with <time>. Its <acceleration> may still be nonzero: uniform <circular motion> has turning <velocity> and inward <centripetal acceleration>. Constant <velocity> is stronger, requiring unchanged direction as well as unchanged <speed>.

= Terminal velocity
{parent=Velocity}
{title2=$\mathbf v_T$}
{wiki}

<Terminal velocity> is a constant limiting <velocity> approached when a <velocity>-dependent resistance balances the other applied <forces>. A falling body may keep moving while its net <force> and <acceleration> tend to zero. For downward motion in a stationary medium, its limiting <speed> is set by balancing <gravitational weight> against <drag force>, including <buoyancy> if appreciable. The balance, rather than a universal numerical value, is the defining idea. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/6-4-drag-force-and-terminal-speed[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, section 6.4: drag and terminal speed]{ref}

The <terminal velocity under linear drag> gives a simple example with an explicit approach to the limit. Real falling objects need an appropriate resistance model: <linear drag> is useful for slow motion of a small sphere in <Stokes flow>, whereas many faster falling objects require <quadratic drag>. Changing <mass> also changes the question: the existing <accreting raindrop with quadratic drag> has a <speed-radius attractor of an accreting raindrop>, rather than the fixed-<mass> balance used below.

= Terminal velocity under linear drag
{parent=Terminal velocity}
{title2=$v_T=Mg/b,\quad\tau=M/b$}

Consider a <point mass> of fixed <mass> $M>0$ released from rest in a stationary medium. Choose downward as positive, assume constant <gravitational acceleration> $g>0$, neglect <buoyancy>, and impose <linear drag> $F_d=-bv$ with constant <linear friction coefficient> $b>0$. Here $v$ is the signed downward <velocity> component; it equals <speed> during this descent. <Newton's second law> becomes
$$
M\frac{dv}{dt}=Mg-bv,\qquad v(0)=0,\qquad v_T=\frac{Mg}{b},\quad\tau=\frac{M}{b}.
$$
The <terminal velocity> balances the two <forces>, and the <time constant> $\tau$ controls how quickly that balance is approached:
$$
v(t)=v_T(1-e^{-t/\tau}),\qquad a(t)=\frac{dv}{dt}=ge^{-t/\tau},\qquad t\ge0.
$$
For the illustrative parameters $M=1\,\mathrm{kg}$, $b=2\,\mathrm{kg/s}$ and $g=9.8\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, the <time constant> is $0.5\,\mathrm s$ and the <terminal velocity> is $4.9\,\mathrm{m/s}$ downward. At $t=1\,\mathrm s=2\tau$, the downward <velocity> is $4.9(1-e^{-2})\,\mathrm{m/s}\simeq4.23686\,\mathrm{m/s}$ and the downward <acceleration> is $9.8e^{-2}\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\simeq1.32629\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$. This is a toy <linear drag> model, not a claim that every $1\,\mathrm{kg}$ falling object has this resistance law.

\Image[linear-drag-terminal-velocity.png]
{title=Exponential approach to terminal velocity and decay of acceleration in an illustrative linear drag model}

For a proof outline, subtract the equilibrium value: $u=v-v_T$ obeys $du/dt=-u/\tau$. The <exponential decay> solution and the initial condition give $u=-v_Te^{-t/\tau}$; one <derivative> gives the <acceleration>. Every <time constant> multiplies the remaining gap $v_T-v$ by $e^{-1}$. The exact limit is reached only as $t\to\infty$. This makes the model useful for estimating a settling duration without mistaking vanishing <acceleration> for vanishing <velocity>.

Newton already analyzed vertical motion under uniform <gravity> and resistance proportional to <velocity> in Book II, Section I, Proposition III of the \i[Principia]. His geometric construction includes a greatest downward <velocity>, illustrating that a limiting rate can emerge from balancing a driving <force> and resistance. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Mathematical_Principles_of_Natural_Philosophy_(1729)/Book_2/Section_1[Newton, Principia, Book II, Proposition III; Motte's 1729 translation]{ref}

= Momentum
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{title2=$\mathbf p$}
{wiki}

= Linear momentum
{synonym}

= Momenta
{synonym}

For a nonrelativistic <point mass> of fixed <mass> $m>0$, measured in an <inertial frame>, its <momentum> is
$$
\mathbf p=m\mathbf v.
$$
<Momentum> is a <vector> pointing along the <velocity>, with <International System of Units> unit $\mathrm{kg\,m/s}$. Doubling either <mass> or <velocity> doubles <momentum>. In one dimension, a chosen positive direction makes $p=mv$ a signed quantity: a $2\,\mathrm{kg}$ body moving right at $3\,\mathrm{m/s}$ has $p=+6\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}$; moving left at the same <speed> gives $p=-6\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/9-1-linear-momentum[OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, section 9.1: Linear Momentum]{ref}

For a collection, total <momentum> is the <vector> sum $\mathbf P=\sum_i m_i\mathbf v_i$. Two equal <masses> moving with opposite <velocities> can have zero total <momentum> while both have positive <kinetic energy>. Direction is therefore essential when using <momentum conservation>; adding <speeds> instead of signed <velocities> gives the wrong balance.

<Newton's second law> connects the net <force> on one fixed-<mass> <point mass> to its <momentum> through $\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}=d\mathbf p/dt$. Integrating that change gives <impulse>. Here $\mathbf p$ denotes <mechanical momentum>; when <canonical momentum> differs from <mass> times <velocity>, the distinction in <mechanical momentum> below applies. The nonrelativistic formula also has a limited regime: <special relativity> is needed at <speeds> comparable with the <speed of light>.

= Mechanical momentum
{parent=Momentum}
{title2=$\boldsymbol\pi=\mathbf p-q\mathbf A$}

For a charged particle minimally coupled to a magnetic <vector potential>, the mechanical momentum is $\boldsymbol\pi=\mathbf p-q\mathbf A$, with $\mathbf p$ the <canonical momentum>. It is the <mass> times <velocity> in nonrelativistic mechanics.

= Momentum conservation
{parent=Momentum}

= Conservation of momentum
{synonym}

<Momentum conservation> states that total <momentum> stays constant when the net <force> exerted from outside the chosen collection is zero. Use a closed collection, meaning the same fixed-<mass> <point masses> are followed throughout, and an <inertial frame>. Then
$$
\mathbf P=\sum_i\mathbf p_i,\qquad
\frac{d\mathbf P}{dt}=\sum_i\mathbf F_i^{\mathrm{ext}}.
$$
To see why, sum <Newton's second law> over the members. If $\mathbf F_{ij}$ is the <force> on member $i$ from member $j$, <Newton's third law> assumes $\mathbf F_{ij}=-\mathbf F_{ji}$. Each pair cancels in the total:
$$
\frac{d\mathbf P}{dt}
=\sum_i\mathbf F_i^{\mathrm{ext}}
+\sum_{i<j}(\mathbf F_{ij}+\mathbf F_{ji})
=\sum_i\mathbf F_i^{\mathrm{ext}}.
$$
The internal <forces> act on different bodies and can change their individual <momenta>; their contributions cancel only in the collection's sum. An <isolated system> has no external interaction, but balanced external <forces> also suffice for <momentum conservation>. A <closed system> merely excluding matter exchange need not have conserved <momentum>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/9-3-conservation-of-linear-momentum[OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, section 9.3: Conservation of Linear Momentum]{ref}

The usefulness is that the detailed internal <forces> during a <collision> need not be known. Both an <elastic collision> and a <perfectly inelastic collision> obey the same isolated <momentum> balance; <kinetic energy> conservation is an additional condition for the <elastic collision>. If the external <impulse> is small but nonzero, the total <momentum> change is correspondingly small. For a <variable-mass system> exchanging matter, the entering or leaving <momentum> must also be included.

In a translation-invariant field theory, integrating over the spacetime zero mode enforces momentum conservation at interactions and scattering amplitudes.

= Rocket equation
{parent=Momentum}

= Tsiolkovsky rocket equation
{c}
{synonym}

The ideal <rocket equation> gives the change in signed <velocity> produced by ejecting <rocket propellant>. Assume one-dimensional <Newtonian mechanics> in an <inertial frame>, no external <force>, and a fixed positive <rocket exhaust speed> $u$ directed rearward relative to the instantaneous rocket. The rocket's instantaneous <mass> $M$ includes its remaining <rocket propellant>; burning it gives $dM<0$. For initial and final <masses> $M_{\mathrm{initial}}\ge M_{\mathrm{final}}>0$,
$$
M\,dv=-u\,dM,\qquad
\Delta v=v_{\mathrm{final}}-v_{\mathrm{initial}}
=u\ln\frac{M_{\mathrm{initial}}}{M_{\mathrm{final}}}.
$$
Here $\ln$ is the <natural logarithm>, and its argument is the <rocket mass ratio>. This is the constant-<rocket exhaust speed>, zero-pressure-correction version of the ideal <rocket equation>. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/ideal-rocket-equation/[NASA Glenn Research Center: Ideal Rocket Equation]{ref}

For a toy rocket with $u=2\,\mathrm{km/s}$, $M_{\mathrm{initial}}=1000\,\mathrm{kg}$ and <rocket dry mass> $M_{\mathrm{final}}=250\,\mathrm{kg}$ after all <rocket propellant> is spent, the <rocket mass ratio> is $4$ and
$$
\Delta v=2\ln4\,\mathrm{km/s}\simeq2.7725887\,\mathrm{km/s}.
$$
Burning from $1000$ to $500\,\mathrm{kg}$ gains $2\ln2\,\mathrm{km/s}\simeq1.3863\,\mathrm{km/s}$. Burning from $500$ to $250\,\mathrm{kg}$ gains exactly the same <velocity> increment, using only $250\,\mathrm{kg}$ of <rocket propellant> instead of $500\,\mathrm{kg}$. Both burns halve the remaining <mass>. The second burn accelerates a lighter rocket; equal fractional <mass> losses give equal <velocity> gains.

\Image[rocket-mass-ratio-delta-v.png]
{title=Equal mass halvings give equal ideal velocity gains for an exhaust speed of 2 kilometres per second}

The <natural logarithm> makes the <rocket equation> both useful and revealing: multiplying successive <rocket mass ratios> adds their <velocity> gains. Conversely, the required <rocket mass ratio> is the <exponential function> $M_{\mathrm{initial}}/M_{\mathrm{final}}=e^{\Delta v/u}$. Each extra $u\ln2$ of ideal <velocity> gain doubles the required initial <mass> for fixed final <mass>. This explains why a larger <rocket exhaust speed> is valuable and why carrying ever more <rocket propellant> has diminishing returns. Changing the burn duration alone does not change this ideal endpoint result.

For a short proof outline, follow the rocket together with a tiny parcel about to be expelled. Before ejection, their total <momentum> is $Mv$. After ejection, the remaining rocket has <mass> $M+dM$ and <velocity> $v+dv$, while the parcel has positive <mass> $-dM$ and, to first order, inertial <velocity> $v-u$. <Momentum conservation> gives
$$
Mv=(M+dM)(v+dv)+(-dM)(v-u),\qquad
0=M\,dv+u\,dM+dM\,dv.
$$
Discarding the second-order product $dM\,dv$ gives $M\,dv=-u\,dM$; taking the <integral> gives $\Delta v=-u\int_{M_{\mathrm{initial}}}^{M_{\mathrm{final}}}dM/M$. Evaluating the <integral> yields the <natural logarithm> above. This is a <variable-mass system> calculation: the rocket alone is not a <closed system>, and its <momentum> changes as the expelled material carries away <momentum>. The fixed collection consisting of the rocket and all its expelled material has conserved total <momentum> when external <forces> vanish.

\Image[rocket-momentum-ejection.png]
{title=Momentum balance for a tiny expelled parcel: rearward relative exhaust can still move forward in the inertial frame}

Fixed <rocket exhaust speed> means fixed relative <speed> at release, not fixed inertial <velocity>. As the rocket's <velocity> $v$ changes, newly emitted material has changing inertial <velocity> $v-u$; previously released parcels coast at their own release <velocities> in this ideal model. If $v>u$, the exhaust even moves forward in the <inertial frame>, although it is emitted rearward relative to the rocket. No surrounding medium is needed for this exchange of <momentum>.

Real flight adds <gravity>, <drag force> and possibly a <pressure> correction to <rocket thrust>. A varying <rocket thrust> due solely to a changing <mass loss rate> still gives the same ideal <rocket equation> if $u$ is constant; it changes the <acceleration> and burn duration. A changing <rocket exhaust speed> instead requires $\Delta v=-\int u\,dM/M$, and external <forces> require their own contribution to the <velocity> change. The existing <constant mass-loss propulsion with quadratic drag> and <constant-burn rocket with ground friction> illustrate such additional <forces>; the <forward-exhaust rocket equation> reverses the ejection direction and therefore the sign of the recoil.

= Rocket exhaust speed
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$u>0$}

The <rocket exhaust speed> is the magnitude of the emitted material's <velocity> relative to the instantaneous rocket. In the ideal rearward-ejection convention of the <rocket equation>, a rocket with inertial <velocity> $v$ emits material with inertial <velocity> $v-u$ to first order. A constant $u$ does not make $v-u$ constant.

= Rocket propellant
{parent=Rocket equation}

<Rocket propellant> is the onboard material expelled to produce <rocket thrust>. A chemical rocket normally carries both fuel and oxidizer; both contribute to its <mass> and to the expelled material. In the ideal <rocket equation>, $M(t)=M_{\mathrm{dry}}+M_{\mathrm{propellant}}(t)$, with fixed <rocket dry mass> and decreasing remaining <rocket propellant>. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/rocket-thrust/[NASA Glenn Research Center: Rocket Thrust]{ref}

= Rocket dry mass
{parent=Rocket propellant}
{title2=$M_{\mathrm{dry}}$}

In this ideal <rocket equation> model, <rocket dry mass> is the <mass> remaining after all usable <rocket propellant> has been expelled: structure, engines and payload. It is the final <mass> in the worked example; if <rocket propellant> remains at shutdown, the final <mass> is larger than the <rocket dry mass>.

= Rocket mass ratio
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$R=M_{\mathrm{initial}}/M_{\mathrm{final}}$}

The <rocket mass ratio> compares the initial and final total rocket <masses> for a burn. It is <dimensionless>, and the ideal <rocket equation> becomes $\Delta v=u\ln R$. For the same fixed <rocket exhaust speed>, two successive burns with <rocket mass ratios> $R_1$ and $R_2$ have combined <rocket mass ratio> $R_1R_2$ and total <velocity> gain $u\ln R_1+u\ln R_2$.

= Rocket thrust
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$T=u(-\dot M)$}

<Rocket thrust> is the propulsive <force> associated with ejecting <rocket propellant>. In the ideal <rocket equation> with no <pressure> correction, $T=u(-\dot M)$, where $\dot M=dM/dt<0$ is the signed rate of change of rocket <mass> and $-\dot M$ is the positive <mass loss rate>. For $u=2000\,\mathrm{m/s}$ and $\dot M=-5\,\mathrm{kg/s}$, $T=10000\,\mathrm N$. With no other <force>, the <acceleration> is $T/M$: initially $10\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$ for $M=1000\,\mathrm{kg}$, rising as <mass> falls if this <rocket thrust> stays constant.

A real exhaust outlet can also contribute a <pressure> term: $T=u(-\dot M)+A_e(p_e-p_a)$, with exit <area> $A_e$, exit <pressure> $p_e$ and ambient <pressure> $p_a$. The ideal formula here assumes that term vanishes; it must not silently absorb this correction while still interpreting $u$ as the actual relative <rocket exhaust speed>. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/rocket-thrust/[NASA Glenn Research Center: Rocket Thrust and its pressure correction]{ref}

= Constant-burn rocket with ground friction
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$q(t)\dot v=su-\mu gq(t)$}

For fuel mass rate $s>0$ and backward relative exhaust speed $u$, a rocket sliding on a horizontal plane has remaining mass $q(t)$ and satisfies the displayed <rocket equation> with <kinetic friction>. Integration over a sliding interval gives $v(t)-v(t_0)=u\log[q(t_0)/q(t)]-\mu g(t-t_0)$. From initial rest, immediate forward sliding additionally requires thrust to overcome <static friction>. If both friction coefficients equal $\mu$, motion starts only when $su>\mu gq(t)$. An algebraically negative sliding speed is not the physical solution at rest.

= Constant mass-loss propulsion with quadratic drag
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$m\dot u=QV-\alpha u^2$}

A body ejecting <mass> at constant rate $Q>0$ and relative exhaust <speed> $V>0$, against <quadratic drag> $\alpha u^2$, has remaining <mass> $m=m_0-Qt$. A short-interval <momentum> balance gives $m\dot u=QV-\alpha u^2$. From rest, <separation of variables> gives
$$
u(t)=\sqrt{\frac{QV}{\alpha}}\tanh\left(\sqrt{\frac{\alpha V}{Q}}\log\frac{m_0}{m_0-Qt}\right).
$$
For final <mass> $m_f>0$, this formula applies until $t=(m_0-m_f)/Q$. The zero-drag limit is the <rocket equation> $u_f=V\log(m_0/m_f)$. Two <dimensionless variables> are $\alpha V/Q$ and $m_0/m_f$.

= Zero-relative-velocity mass shedding
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$q\dot v=F_{\rm ext}$}

When matter leaves a moving body with zero instantaneous relative <velocity>, it carries the body's current <momentum> per unit mass but creates no additional recoil term. A short-interval <momentum> balance including the newly released material gives $q\dot v=F_{\rm ext}$ for the remaining body's mass $q(t)$. The expression $\frac d{dt}(qv)=F_{\rm ext}$ omits the outgoing momentum flux. Nonzero relative ejection <velocity> instead produces the thrust term of the <rocket equation>.

= Dust-collecting rocket
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$m\dot v=\alpha u-\beta v^2$}

A classical rocket ejecting fuel at rate $\alpha>0$ and relative exhaust <speed> $u>0$, while collecting stationary dust of <mass> $\beta\,dx$ per traveled distance, obeys
$$
\dot m=\beta v-\alpha,\qquad m\dot v=\alpha u-\beta v^2.
$$
These are <mass conservation> and <momentum conservation> for the rocket, incoming dust and outgoing gas. The dust-loading term supplies the <momentum> needed to accelerate incoming stationary material. Put $\lambda=\sqrt{\alpha/(\beta u)}$ and $V=\lambda u$. While the engines operate and $0\leq v<V$, an initially stationary rocket satisfies
$$
\frac m{m_0}=\left(\frac{V-v}{V}\right)^{(\lambda-1)/2}\left(\frac{V+v}{V}\right)^{-(\lambda+1)/2}.
$$
For $\lambda>1$, continuing to $V$ would drive total <mass> to zero. For $0<\lambda<1$, total <mass> instead grows without bound and $V$ is approached only after infinite time under indefinitely maintained thrust. In both cases a finite fuel supply runs out before that limiting <speed>. Total stored <mass> must not be confused with remaining fuel.

= Rocket equation with stationary mass accretion
{parent=Dust-collecting rocket}
{title2=$M\dot v=u\dot m-v\dot D$}

For a <rocket equation> with exhaust mass rate $\dot m$ and incoming stationary mass rate $\dot D$, mass balance is $\dot M=\dot D-\dot m$. A short-interval <momentum conservation> calculation gives $M\dot v=u\dot m-v\dot D$. Constant time rates $\dot D=\alpha$, $\dot m=\beta$ give $M\dot v+\alpha v=u\beta$, unlike a constant accretion rate per distance, whose incoming mass rate is proportional to speed. For unequal rates and initial rest, $v=(u\beta/\alpha)[1-(M/M_0)^{\alpha/(\beta-\alpha)}]$.

= Equal-rate rocket burning and accretion
{parent=Rocket equation with stationary mass accretion}
{title2=$v(t)=u(1-e^{-\alpha t/M_0})$}

If stationary mass arrives at the same constant time rate as fuel is ejected, total rocket mass stays fixed. The <rocket equation with stationary mass accretion> is then $M_0\dot v+\alpha v=\alpha u$. For initial rest, speed approaches the relative exhaust speed exponentially with relaxation time $M_0/\alpha$. This is the regular equal-rate limit of the unequal-rate mass-power formula.

= Relativistic rocket equation
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$m\gamma^2\,dv+u\,dm=0$}

For collinear motion without external forces, a rocket ejecting material backwards at constant speed $u$ in its instantaneous rest frame obeys $m\gamma^2\,dv+u\,dm=0$. Here $m$ is the rocket's <rest mass>, including stored <internal energy>, and $\gamma$ is its <Lorentz factor> in the chosen <inertial frame>. The equation follows from <momentum conservation>, <conservation of relativistic energy>, and the <relativistic velocity-addition formula>; expelled rest mass is not simply the decrease in rocket rest mass.

= Two-stage rocket with proportional fuel and body masses
{parent=Rocket equation}
{title2=$v_2=u\log\frac{M+m_0}{M+(1-k)m_0}+u\log\frac{M+m_0}{M}$}

For zero initial <velocity>, body <masses> $kM,(1-k)M$ and fuel <masses> $km_0,(1-k)m_0$, the <rocket equation> gives the displayed final second-stage <speed> after burning the first-stage fuel, discarding its body without impulse, and burning the second-stage fuel. It assumes $M>0$ and $0\le k<1$. The speed exceeds the single-stage value when $0<k<1$ and fuel is positive. Its limit as $k\to1^-$ concerns a surviving second stage of vanishing <mass>; at $k=1$ no such stage exists.

= Forward-exhaust rocket equation
{parent=Rocket equation}

If a vehicle moving at speed $v$ ejects mass forward at relative speed $u>0$, then
$$
M\,dv=u\,dM.
$$
As $dM<0$, the vehicle slows down.

= Angular momentum
{title2=$\mathbf L=\mathbf r\times\mathbf p$}
{parent=Momentum}
{wiki}

= Angular momenta
{synonym}

<Angular momentum> measures how a <point mass>'s <momentum> passes around a chosen origin. Motion straight toward or away from that origin contributes zero; transverse <momentum> contributes more when it passes farther away. The picture shows the sign convention before the calculation.

\Image[angular-momentum-lever-arm.png]
{title=Positive, negative and zero angular momentum about the same origin}

Fix an origin $O$ and nonrotating axes in an <inertial frame>. For a nonrelativistic <point mass> of constant <mass> $m>0$, let $\mathbf r$ be its <position> from $O$ and $\mathbf p=m\mathbf v$ its <mechanical momentum> in that same <reference frame>. Its <angular momentum> is the <cross product>
$$
\mathbf L_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf p,\qquad
|\mathbf L_O|=rp\sin\phi.
$$
Here $r=|\mathbf r|$, $p=|\mathbf p|$, and $\phi\in[0,\pi]$ is the <angle> between them. The <right-hand rule> gives the direction: curl from $\mathbf r$ toward $\mathbf p$, and the thumb points along $\mathbf L_O$. Thus <angular momentum> is an <axial vector>, perpendicular to both <position> and <momentum>. Its <International System of Units> unit is $\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}$. A <point mass> need not follow a <circle> to have <angular momentum>.<University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/11.2 Angular Momentum>[section 11.2: Angular Momentum]{ref}

With $x$ rightward, $y$ upward, and $+z$ out of the page, the <cross product> gives $L_z=xp_y-yp_x$. Take
$$
\mathbf r=(2,0,0)\,\mathrm m,\qquad
\mathbf p=(0,3,0)\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}.
$$
Then $\mathbf L_O=(0,0,6)\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}$. Reversing the <momentum> makes $L_z=-6\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}$; radial <momentum> $\mathbf p=(3,0,0)\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}$ gives $\mathbf L_O=\mathbf0$. The factor $r\sin\phi$ is the <lever arm> to the line through the particle parallel to its <momentum>.

For motion in the $xy$ plane, <plane polar coordinates> give $\mathbf v=\dot r\widehat{\mathbf r}+r\dot\theta\widehat{\boldsymbol\theta}$, with $\widehat{\mathbf r}\times\widehat{\boldsymbol\theta}=\widehat{\mathbf z}$. Consequently
$$
L_z=mr^2\dot\theta,\qquad r^2\dot\theta=\frac{L_z}{m}.
$$
The signed <angular velocity> $\dot\theta$ is positive counterclockwise viewed from $+z$ toward the plane, with $\theta$ measured in <radians>. Radial <velocity> does not enter the <cross product>. Under <conservation of angular momentum>, this relation predicts faster angular motion at smaller $r$ and constant <areal velocity> $\dot A=L_z/(2m)$ for consistently oriented swept <area>. That is the geometric content of <Kepler's second law>.

For several <point masses>, add their <angular momenta> about the same origin: $\mathbf L_O=\sum_i\mathbf r_i\times\mathbf p_i$. The origin matters: <angular momentum under an origin shift> explains why the same moving particle can have different values about two fixed points. The <central axis of a momentum system> and <angular momentum decomposition about the centre of mass> make this dependence useful.

= Angular momentum under an origin shift
{parent=Angular momentum}
{title2=$\mathbf L_{O'}=\mathbf L_O-\mathbf a\times\mathbf P$}

Move the fixed origin from $O$ to $O'=O+\mathbf a$, keeping the axes parallel and the <inertial frame>'s <velocity> measurements unchanged. The new <positions> are $\mathbf r'_i=\mathbf r_i-\mathbf a$, so distributivity of the <cross product> gives
$$
\mathbf L_{O'}=\sum_i(\mathbf r_i-\mathbf a)\times\mathbf p_i
=\mathbf L_O-\mathbf a\times\mathbf P,\qquad
\mathbf P=\sum_i\mathbf p_i.
$$
Here $\mathbf P$ is total <linear momentum>. For the <angular momentum> example with $\mathbf a=(1,0,0)\,\mathrm m$, the new <position> is $(1,0,0)\,\mathrm m$ and $L_{O',z}=6-3=3\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}$. If total <momentum> is zero, <angular momentum> is independent of this origin shift. A moving origin additionally contributes $-\dot{\mathbf a}\times\mathbf P$ when this expression is differentiated while retaining the original <momenta>; see <angular momentum about the centre of mass> for an important case where that term vanishes.

= Central axis of a momentum system
{parent=Angular momentum}
{title2=$\mathbf b=\mathbf P\times\mathbf L/|\mathbf P|^2+\lambda\mathbf P$}

For nonzero total <momentum> $\mathbf P$, translating the origin by $\mathbf b$ changes <angular momentum> to $\mathbf L-\mathbf b\times\mathbf P$. The displayed line of origins makes this vector parallel to $\mathbf P$, equal to $(\mathbf L\cdot\mathbf P)\mathbf P/|\mathbf P|^2$. The invariant $\mathbf L\cdot\mathbf P$ cannot be removed by translation; if it is zero, angular momentum vanishes on this line.

= Gyroscope
{parent=Angular momentum}
{wiki}

A <gyroscope> is a rotating instrument whose rapidly spinning, nearly symmetric rotor provides a direction reference through its <angular momentum>. In an <inertial frame>, negligible external <torque> approximately preserves that direction by <conservation of angular momentum>. A transverse <torque> turns the <angular momentum> instead: the surprising sideways motion of a supported spinning wheel is <precession>. An ideal freely falling test gyroscope in <general relativity> has its spin undergo <parallel transport> along its <timelike geodesic>. Comparing that direction with distant reference axes measures <Lense-Thirring precession>; the direction need not remain fixed in arbitrary rotating coordinates.

A <gyroscope direction reference> is useful precisely because its direction can persist while its housing turns. A <spinning-wheel precession experiment> deliberately supplies gravitational <torque> to reveal how that persistence fails. The <gyroscope vector geometry> explains the direction, and <slow steady gyroscope precession> gives a numerical prediction with a check on its assumptions.

= Gyroscope vector geometry
{parent=Gyroscope}
{title2=$\boldsymbol\tau_O=d\mathbf L_O/dt$}

Choose a fixed support point $O$ and nonrotating axes in an <inertial frame>. All <angular momenta>, <torques>, and <moments of inertia> below refer to $O$. Let $\widehat{\mathbf e}$ point along the axle from $O$ toward the <centre of mass>, and let $\widehat{\mathbf z}$ point vertically upward. Write $\theta$ for their <angle>. For a rapidly spinning <symmetric top>, the dominant <angular momentum> is $\mathbf L_O\simeq L_s\widehat{\mathbf e}$, where $L_s=I_3\omega_3$ is signed and $I_3$ is the axial <principal moment of inertia>. The sign of the axial <angular velocity> $\omega_3$ follows the <right-hand rule>.

\Image[gyroscope-wheel-torque-vector-geometry.png]
{title=A fixed wheel support separates the downward gravitational force from the transverse torque and sideways precession}

With <mass> $M$, <centre of mass> distance $d$, and constant <gravitational acceleration> $g$, the gravitational <force> and its <torque> are
$$
\mathbf r=d\widehat{\mathbf e},\qquad \mathbf F_g=-Mg\widehat{\mathbf z},\qquad
\boldsymbol\tau_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F_g=Mgd\,\widehat{\mathbf z}\times\widehat{\mathbf e}.
$$
An ideal support applies a <force> at $O$, so its <lever arm> and <torque> about $O$ vanish. At the instant the axle lies in the $xz$ plane with a positive $x$ component, the gravitational <torque> points along $+y$. It adds $d\mathbf L_O=\boldsymbol\tau_O\,dt$ toward $+y$, rather than along the downward <force>. The <dot product> identity $d|\mathbf L_O|^2/dt=2\mathbf L_O\cdot\boldsymbol\tau_O$ shows why perpendicular <torque> changes direction without instantly changing magnitude. This vector balance and the slow-turning relation $\boldsymbol\tau_O=\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf L_O$ are developed in <The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 20: Rotation in space> (sections 20-2 and 20-3, equations 20.13–20.15).

For steady <precession> about the vertical, $\boldsymbol\Omega=\Omega\widehat{\mathbf z}$ and $d\widehat{\mathbf e}/dt=\Omega\widehat{\mathbf z}\times\widehat{\mathbf e}$. Equating the two <cross products> gives
$$
\Omega\simeq\frac{Mgd}{L_s}=\frac{Mgd}{I_3\omega_3},\qquad
|\boldsymbol\tau_O|=Mgd\sin\theta.
$$
The <angular momentum> tip traces a <circle> of <radius> $|L_s|\sin\theta$: its sideways <speed> is $|\Omega L_s|\sin\theta$. Both that <radius> and the gravitational <lever arm> contain $\sin\theta$, explaining its cancellation for $0<\theta<\pi$. Exactly vertical alignment has zero gravitational <torque> and no distinguishable azimuthal <precession> to infer by this cancellation. Positive $L_s$ gives positive <precession>, counterclockwise viewed from above; reversing the spin reverses the slow <precession>. <University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/11.4 Precession of a Gyroscope>[University Physics Volume 1, section 11.4, equation 11.12 and the torque and precession-circle derivation]{ref}

= Spinning-wheel precession experiment
{parent=Gyroscope}

https://www.physics.purdue.edu/demos/display_page.php?item=1Q-30[Purdue's Bicycle Wheel Gyroscope demonstration] uses a bicycle wheel on a long axle, with a ball at one end seated in a freely rotating socket on a heavy stand. Spin the wheel by hand or with the supplied motor, seat the ball, and release the nearly horizontal axle. The readout is the axle sweeping around the vertical while the wheel continues spinning. Compare with the stopped wheel, which falls when one end is released; reverse the spin with the same support and the <precession> reverses. These are the page's documented apparatus, presentation, and reversal checks. <Bicycle Wheel Gyroscope by Purdue University, Department of Physics and Astronomy>[1Q-30, apparatus paragraph, Directions, and Suggestions for Presentation; page updated 30 November 2023]{ref}

An alternative in https://sprott.physics.wisc.edu/demobook/CHAPTER1.HTM[Physics Demonstrations by Julien Clinton Sprott] (section 1.3, Materials and Procedure) suspends one axle end by stainless steel wire and a fishing-line swivel. The swivel allows <precession> without winding up the suspension. Sprott describes the axle bobbing as <nutation> and <precession> becoming faster as spin decreases. A suspended support can move, whereas the calculation here assumes a fixed $O$; use the socket version for the closest realization. <Physics Demonstrations: A Sourcebook for Teachers of Physics (1996 online manuscript) by Julien Clinton Sprott/Bicycle Wheel Gyroscope (section 1.3)>[section 1.3, Bicycle Wheel Gyroscope: Materials, Procedure, and Discussion]{ref}

A proposed quantitative extension is to record an overhead view of an axle marker against a fixed <angle> scale, together with a side view for $\theta(t)$. Time successive overhead passages to estimate $\Omega=2\pi/T$; count passages of a rim mark to estimate $|\omega_3|=2\pi f$. Record the spin sense too. Measure $M$ and the <centre of mass> offset $d$ of the whole moving assembly, and determine its axial <moment of inertia> $I_3$ independently. Wheel, axle, support, release <angle>, and observation interval should stay the same between spin trials. Measuring both $\Omega$ and $\theta(t)$ distinguishes azimuthal <precession> from <nutation>; an overhead movie alone can miss the latter. These camera readouts and parameter checks are proposed here, not reported measurements from the demonstration pages.

For slow <precession>, compare $\Omega I_3\omega_3$ with $Mgd$, and test whether reversing $\omega_3$ reverses $\Omega$ with nearly unchanged magnitude. A brief interval minimizes spin loss from <friction>; repeat at several starting spin rates rather than interpreting an entire slowing run as one steady state. A suspension restoring <torque> governed by its <torsion constant>, socket <friction>, support motion, a misplaced <centre of mass>, and a push at release can all supply extra <torque> or change the initial conditions. The swivel reduces the twist that activates the suspension's <torsion constant>; it does not demonstrate zero <torque>.

Steady <precession> keeps $\theta$ constant; <nutation> changes $\theta$ while the axle may also precess. Sudden release generally excites both. A small initial drop is consistent with <conservation of energy> and conserved vertical <angular momentum>: some <gravitational potential energy> supplies the motion around the vertical, and the axial contribution to vertical <angular momentum> adjusts. Bearing <friction> may damp the bobbing, but an ideal undamped <heavy symmetric top> need not settle into steady <precession>. <The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 20: Rotation in space> explicitly distinguishes uniform <precession> from release transients and <nutation> (section 20-3, paragraphs following equation 20.15).<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 20: Rotation in space>[section 20-3: sudden release, nutation, and the small drop needed for precessional angular momentum]{ref}

= Slow steady gyroscope precession
{parent=Gyroscope}
{title2=$\Omega_{\mathrm{slow}}\simeq Mgd/(I_3\omega_3)$}

Model the assembly as a <heavy symmetric top> with a fixed frictionless support, a uniform <gravitational field>, and <centre of mass> on its symmetry axis. Assume constant spin, negligible <damping>, and initial conditions prepared for <uniform precession of a heavy symmetric top>.
