Large spin means the transverse <angular momentum> from <precession> is small: $I_1|\Omega|\sin\theta\ll|I_3\omega_3|$. Also require $|\Omega|\ll|\omega_3|$. A large spin rate alone does not select steady motion after an arbitrary release.

For an original illustrative calculation, stipulate
$$
M=2.0\,\mathrm{kg},\quad d=0.15\,\mathrm m,\quad g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2},\quad
I_3=0.080\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2},\quad I_1=I_2=0.090\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2},\quad
\omega_3=100\,\mathrm{rad/s},\quad\theta=60^\circ.
$$
The transverse <principal moments of inertia> $I_1,I_2$ are about the pivot, including the <parallel axis theorem> contribution. These inputs give
$$
L_s=8.0\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s},\quad K=Mgd=2.943\,\mathrm{N\,m},\quad
|\boldsymbol\tau_O|=K\sin60^\circ=2.5487\,\mathrm{N\,m},
$$
$$
\Omega_0=K/L_s=0.367875\,\mathrm{rad/s},\qquad T_0=2\pi/\Omega_0\simeq17.080\,\mathrm s.
$$
The ratio $\Omega_0/\omega_3=0.00368$ is small; the more direct <angular momentum> ratio $I_1\Omega_0\sin60^\circ/L_s=0.00358$ is small too. These are model calculations, not experimental results.

Check the approximation against the existing exact <uniform precession of a heavy symmetric top> condition. Its total <angular momentum> is
$$
\mathbf L_O=I_1\Omega(\widehat{\mathbf z}-\cos\theta\widehat{\mathbf e})+L_s\widehat{\mathbf e}.
$$
At constant $\theta,\Omega,L_s$, differentiating this expression and equating it to the gravitational <torque> gives
$$
I_1\cos\theta\,\Omega^2-L_s\Omega+K=0.
$$
For $L_s>0$ the slow solution can be evaluated without subtracting two nearly equal numbers:
$$
\Omega_{\mathrm{slow}}=\frac{2K}{L_s+\sqrt{L_s^2-4I_1K\cos\theta}}
\simeq0.368639\,\mathrm{rad/s}.
$$
It exceeds $\Omega_0$ by about $0.208\%$. The other solution is about $177.409\,\mathrm{rad/s}$ and violates the assumed slow <precession>; the quadratic's two roots must not be conflated. Its <discriminant> must be nonnegative for these steady solutions to exist.

\Image[gyroscope-precession-spin-torque-sweep.png]
{title=Synthetic steady-precession calculations: larger spin slows the drift and larger transverse torque speeds it up}

The original curves hold $I_1,I_3,\theta$ fixed and vary spin and the imposed <torque> coefficient $K$ in $\boldsymbol\tau_O=K\widehat{\mathbf z}\times\widehat{\mathbf e}$. For gravity alone, $K=Mgd$; the other curves are hypothetical changes in the transverse <torque>, not observations of this wheel. Solid curves use the slow quadratic root; dashed curves use $K/(I_3\omega_3)$. The second panel displays their relative difference. Increasing spin improves this approximation for the parameters shown. It does not remove <nutation> caused by inappropriate initial conditions.

= Gyroscope direction reference
{parent=Gyroscope}

For a rotor spinning about a <principal axis> with its support at the <centre of mass>, uniform <gravity> supplies no <torque> about that centre. With other external <torques> negligible, <conservation of angular momentum> preserves its direction in an <inertial frame>. A mount that permits free axis motion lets the housing rotate relative to that persistent direction, giving an orientation reference when external landmarks are unavailable. This is the useful limit opposite to the deliberately off-centre support in the <spinning-wheel precession experiment>. It supplies a direction, not a position or a full three-axis orientation from one axis alone.

For a small accumulated transverse <torque> about the <centre of mass>, the change in the direction <unit vector> is, to first order,
$$
\Delta\widehat{\mathbf L}\simeq\frac{1}{L}\int\boldsymbol\tau_\perp\,dt,\qquad
\boldsymbol\tau_\perp=\boldsymbol\tau-(\boldsymbol\tau\cdot\widehat{\mathbf L})\widehat{\mathbf L},\qquad L=|\mathbf L|.
$$
Here $L$ is approximately constant over the short interval. Large <angular momentum> reduces drift for the same integrated transverse <torque>; parallel <torque> instead changes its magnitude. For $L=8.0\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}$, a constant transverse <torque> of $0.010\,\mathrm{N\,m}$ over $1.0\,\mathrm s$ produces approximately $0.00125\,\mathrm{rad}$, or $0.0716^\circ$, of direction change. This is another synthetic calculation. The persistent direction belongs to an <inertial frame>; its components can change in a rotating <reference frame> even with zero external <torque>.

= Magnetic axial angular-momentum invariant
{parent=Angular momentum}
{title2=$L_z+qB\rho^2/2$}

A charged particle in a uniform field $\boldsymbol B=B\boldsymbol e_z$ and an axially symmetric potential conserves the displayed canonical angular momentum, with $\rho$ the distance from the field axis and $q$ its charge. It follows directly by differentiating the expression and using the <Lorentz force>. For charge $q=-e$, the equivalent invariant is $m(\dot{\boldsymbol r}\times\boldsymbol r)\cdot\boldsymbol B+e|\boldsymbol r\times\boldsymbol B|^2/2$. A radial restoring force has no axial torque.

= Angular momentum decay under uniform linear drag
{parent=Angular momentum}
{title2=$L(t)=L(0)e^{-kt/m}$}

Consider particles of the same constant mass $m$, interacting through equal-and-opposite central internal forces, with external forces $F_i=-k\dot r_i$ for constant $k$. About a fixed point $a$, internal pair <torques> cancel and the external <torque> is $G_a=-k\sum_i(r_i-a)\times\dot r_i=-(k/m)L_a$. Hence $\dot L_a=-(k/m)L_a$ and the displayed exponential decay follows. The same result holds for <angular momentum about the centre of mass>, because the moving-origin term $-\dot R\times P=-\dot R\times M\dot R$ vanishes. Unequal masses do not generally permit factoring out a single $k/m$.

= Generalized angular momentum in a radial monopole field
{parent=Angular momentum}
{title2=$\mathbf L=m\mathbf r\times\dot{\mathbf r}-\mu\widehat{\mathbf r}$}

For a radial conservative force plus the magnetic-type force $\dot{\mathbf r}\times(\mu\mathbf r/r^3)$, the torque equals $\mu\,d\widehat{\mathbf r}/dt$. Subtracting $\mu\widehat{\mathbf r}$ from ordinary <angular momentum> therefore produces a constant vector. Its dot product with $\widehat{\mathbf r}$ is $-\mu$, so motion lies on a cone about that vector. At $\mu=0$, ordinary planar <central force> motion is recovered. This is the classical radial <magnetic monopole> angular-momentum correction.

= Conservation of angular momentum
{parent=Angular momentum}
{title2=$d\mathbf L_O/dt=\boldsymbol\tau_O^{\mathrm{ext}}=\mathbf0$}

= Angular momentum conservation
{synonym}

<Conservation of angular momentum> says that total <angular momentum> stays fixed when the net external <torque> about the chosen fixed inertial origin vanishes. Individual <angular momenta> and <angular velocities> can change. The <radius change at constant angular momentum> below makes this distinction visible: pulling two rotating <point masses> inward lowers the <moment of inertia>, raises the <angular velocity>, and supplies <work> that increases the <kinetic energy>.

\Image[angular-momentum-radius-change.png]
{title=Pulling two point masses inward preserves angular momentum and adds three joules of kinetic energy}

Follow the same constant-<mass> <point masses> throughout, in an <inertial frame>, with all <positions> measured from a fixed origin $O$. If the internal pair <forces> are opposite and central, meaning directed along each pair's separation, then
$$
\mathbf L_O=\sum_i\mathbf r_i\times\mathbf p_i,\qquad
\frac{d\mathbf L_O}{dt}=\boldsymbol\tau_O^{\mathrm{ext}}
=\sum_i\mathbf r_i\times\mathbf F_i^{\mathrm{ext}}.
$$
Thus zero net external <torque> throughout an interval implies constant total <angular momentum> throughout that interval. A vanishing external <torque> component, such as $\tau_z^{\mathrm{ext}}=0$, conserves the corresponding $L_z$ even when the other components change. This is the balance law for a fixed collection; <angular-momentum flux> must also be accounted for when matter crosses its boundary. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/11-3-conservation-of-angular-momentum[OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, section 11.3: Conservation of Angular Momentum]{ref}

The central qualification is essential. Write $\mathbf F_{ij}$ for the internal <force> on member $i$ from member $j$. Under <Newton's third law>, $\mathbf F_{ji}=-\mathbf F_{ij}$, but the sum of their <torques> is
$$
\mathbf r_i\times\mathbf F_{ij}+\mathbf r_j\times\mathbf F_{ji}
=(\mathbf r_i-\mathbf r_j)\times\mathbf F_{ij}.
$$
This vanishes when $\mathbf F_{ij}$ is parallel to $\mathbf r_i-\mathbf r_j$. Equal-and-opposite <forces> alone do not suffice: two opposite transverse <forces> with separated lines of action form a <couple> with nonzero <torque>. Cancellation of the internal pair <torques>, together with <Newton's second law>, proves the total balance above.

For one <point mass> under a <central force> about $O$, $\mathbf F=f(r)\widehat{\mathbf r}$ gives $\boldsymbol\tau_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F=\mathbf0$. The <force> may still change the <speed> and <kinetic energy>; its zero <torque> preserves <angular momentum>. With nonzero $\mathbf L_O$, $\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf L_O=0$ keeps the motion in a fixed plane. Choosing the $z$ axis perpendicular to that plane, $r^2\dot\theta=L_z/m$ governs its angular progress. This removes an unknown from the <central-force radial turning point> calculation and helps reduce the <Kepler orbit> to a radial problem.

There is a historical geometric version of this result. In Book I, Section II, Proposition I of the \i[Principia], Newton proves that motion under a <central force> toward a fixed centre stays in a plane and sweeps equal <areas> in equal <times>, using successive central <impulses> and equal <triangle> <areas>. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Mathematical_Principles_of_Natural_Philosophy_(1846)/BookI-II[Newton, Principia, Book I, Proposition I, Motte translation, 1846 edition]{ref} In the modern notation above, constant <areal velocity> is $\dot A=L_z/(2m)$: the old geometric proof and the <angular momentum> calculation express the same invariant.

= Radius change at constant angular momentum
{parent=Conservation of angular momentum}
{title2=$I\omega=L_z,\quad K_{\mathrm{rot}}=L_z^2/(2I)$}

Place two equal <point masses> of $1\,\mathrm{kg}$ each on opposite sides of a fixed origin, at equal <radius> $r$, and let them share a signed <angular velocity> $\omega=\dot\theta$ in a plane. Their total <mass> is $M=2\,\mathrm{kg}$ and their <moment of inertia> about the perpendicular axis is $I=Mr^2$. Radial pulling <forces> have zero <torque> about that origin, so
$$
L_z=I\omega=Mr^2\omega=\mathrm{constant},\qquad
K_{\mathrm{rot}}=\frac12I\omega^2=\frac{L_z^2}{2I}.
$$
Take an initial <radius> $r_i=1\,\mathrm m$ and <angular velocity> $\omega_i=1\,\mathrm{rad/s}$, and a final <radius> $r_f=0.5\,\mathrm m$. Then
$$
I_i=2\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2},\quad I_f=0.5\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2},\quad
L_z=2\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s},\quad \omega_f=4\,\mathrm{rad/s},
$$
and
$$
K_i=1\,\mathrm J,\qquad K_f=4\,\mathrm J,\qquad W=K_f-K_i=+3\,\mathrm J.
$$
These are endpoint states with $\dot r_i=\dot r_f=0$. During the pull, the full <kinetic energy> also contains radial motion: $K=M\dot r^2/2+K_{\mathrm{rot}}$. Assume the pulling apparatus supplies all net <work> to the two <point masses>; the <work-energy theorem> then fixes its net <work> at $+3\,\mathrm J$, including any radial acceleration and braking. Each <mass> spirals inward during the pull, allowing its inward <force> to do positive <work>. <Conservation of angular momentum> therefore predicts the final <angular velocity> without requiring <kinetic energy> conservation. For an apparatus included in a larger system, its stored <energy> supplies the increase consistently with <conservation of energy>. OpenStax uses the same mechanism to explain a skater pulling her arms inward. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/11-3-conservation-of-angular-momentum[OpenStax, section 11.3: work during a change of moment of inertia]{ref}

= Angular-momentum flux
{parent=Conservation of angular momentum}
{title2=$\dot J$}

The rate at which <angular momentum> crosses a surface, including advection and stresses. With positive inward <mass accretion rate>, the net inward flux in a steady <accretion disk> is $\dot M h-\mathcal G$.

= Central-force radial turning point
{parent=Conservation of angular momentum}

For unit mass in a <central force> with potential $\Phi(r)$ and conserved angular momentum $L$, a radial turning point $r_*$ has zero radial speed and therefore satisfies
$$
E=\frac{L^2}{2r_*^2}+\Phi(r_*).
$$

= Attractive and repulsive inverse-square closest approaches
{parent=Central-force radial turning point}
{title2=$p_-=\sqrt{b^2+q^2}-q,\quad p_+=\sqrt{b^2+q^2}+q$}

For unit <mass>, incoming <speed> $v>0$, <impact parameter> $b>0$, and <potential energy> $\mp\kappa/r$, put $q=\kappa/v^2>0$. At closest approach, <conservation of energy> and <conservation of angular momentum> give the displayed radii and tangential <speeds> $u_\mp=bv/p_\mp$. Consequently $p_-p_+=b^2$ and $u_-u_+=v^2$. These relations compare equal force magnitudes. The attractive radial case $b=0$ is a singular collision, so the speed-product statement does not extend to that endpoint.

= Angular momentum transport
{parent=Angular momentum}

Angular momentum transport moves angular momentum between locations through matter, stresses, waves, or fields. An <accretion disk> must transport angular momentum outward for most of its mass to move inward.

= Torque
{title2=$\boldsymbol\tau_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F$}
{parent=Angular momentum}
{wiki}

= Moment of force
{synonym}

<Torque> measures a <force>'s turning effect about a specified origin. The geometry in the <angular momentum> diagram applies with <force> replacing <momentum>: transverse <forces> give positive or negative <torque>, while a radial <force> gives zero. A longer perpendicular <lever arm> makes the same <force> more effective, explaining why pushing near the end of a handle is useful.

For a <force> $\mathbf F$ applied at <position> $\mathbf r$ from a fixed origin $O$ in an <inertial frame>, its <torque>, or <moment of force>, is
$$
\boldsymbol\tau_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F,\qquad
|\boldsymbol\tau_O|=rF\sin\phi=F\ell.
$$
Here $F=|\mathbf F|$, $\phi$ is the <angle> between <position> and <force>, and $\ell=r\sin\phi$ is the perpendicular <lever arm>. Direction follows the <right-hand rule>; in the $xy$ plane $\tau_z=xF_y-yF_x$, positive counterclockwise as viewed from $+z$. The <International System of Units> unit is $\mathrm{N\,m}=\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s^2}$. Although <torque> and <energy> have the same <physical dimensions>, <torque> is an <axial vector> and <energy> is a <scalar>; their roles differ.

For $\mathbf r=(2,0,0)\,\mathrm m$ and $\mathbf F=(0,3,0)\,\mathrm N$, $\tau_z=+6\,\mathrm{N\,m}$. Applying the same <force> at $(1,0,0)\,\mathrm m$ gives $+3\,\mathrm{N\,m}$; reversing it gives a negative <torque>; directing it along $\mathbf r$ gives zero. More generally, sum $\mathbf r_i\times\mathbf F_i$ to find the total <torque> about one common origin.

For a constant-<mass> <point mass>, the <product rule> and <Newton's second law> show why net <torque> changes <angular momentum>:
$$
\frac{d\mathbf L_O}{dt}
=\mathbf v\times(m\mathbf v)+\mathbf r\times\frac{d\mathbf p}{dt}
=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}
=\boldsymbol\tau_O^{\mathrm{net}}.
$$
The first <cross product> vanishes because its factors are parallel. For a collection with canceling internal <torques>, the remaining sum is external <torque>, giving <conservation of angular momentum> when it vanishes.<University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/11.2 Angular Momentum>[section 11.2: torque and the angular momentum balance]{ref}

A nonzero <torque> can turn the direction of <angular momentum> as well as change its magnitude. Indeed, the <dot product> gives $d|\mathbf L_O|^2/dt=2\mathbf L_O\cdot\boldsymbol\tau_O$: a perpendicular <torque> changes direction without instantaneously changing the magnitude. This is the geometry behind <gyroscope> <precession>. For a rapidly spinning <symmetric top> with a fixed tip, <centre of mass> distance $d$ from the tip, <mass> $M$, and spin-dominated <angular momentum> magnitude $L\simeq I\omega$, the slow steady <precession> approximation gives $\Omega\simeq Mgd/L$, where $g$ is <gravitational acceleration>. <University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/11.4 Precession of a Gyroscope>[OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, section 11.4: Precession of a Gyroscope]{ref} This is an approximation with $\Omega\ll\omega$; the <uniform precession of a heavy symmetric top> retains contributions omitted by the spin-dominated estimate.

= Lever arm
{parent=Torque}
{title2=$\ell=r\sin\phi$}

= Moment arm
{synonym}

The <lever arm> of a nonzero <force> about an origin is the shortest distance from that origin to the line through the application point parallel to the <force>. Its length is $\ell=|\mathbf r\times\widehat{\mathbf F}|$, with <unit vector> $\widehat{\mathbf F}=\mathbf F/|\mathbf F|$. Thus <torque> magnitude is $F\ell$, maximal at a right <angle> for fixed $r$ and $F$, and zero if the line passes through the origin. Sliding the application point along that line leaves <torque> unchanged, since $(\mathbf r+s\widehat{\mathbf F})\times\mathbf F=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F$. The same perpendicular-distance construction gives <angular momentum> magnitude $p\ell$ for a line parallel to nonzero <momentum> through the particle.

= Couple
{disambiguate=mechanics}
{parent=Torque}
{title2=$\mathbf G=(\mathbf x_1-\mathbf x_2)\times\mathbf F$}
{wiki}

= Force couple
{synonym}

= Couple
{synonym}

A pair of equal and opposite <forces> acting along distinct lines has zero resultant <force> but a nonzero <torque>. If $\mathbf F$ acts at $\mathbf x_1$ and $-\mathbf F$ at $\mathbf x_2$, the total <torque> about any origin is $(\mathbf x_1-\mathbf x_2)\times\mathbf F$. Shifting that origin changes the moment by the <cross product> of the shift with the resultant <force>, which is zero. A prescribed <couple> therefore specifies a pure turning moment independently of its reference point. In <Stokes flow> it can drive a <rotating sphere in Stokes flow> without a directly imposed translational <force>.

= Moment balance
{parent=Torque}

Moment balance equates the rate of change of angular momentum to applied torques. A static slender rod obeys $\partial_s\mathbf M+\mathbf t\times\mathbf F+\mathbf m=0$.

= Areal velocity
{title2=$\dot A$}
{parent=Angular momentum}
{wiki}

Areal velocity measures how fast a <position> <vector> from a chosen origin sweeps <signed swept area>. It connects a geometric picture of motion to <angular momentum>: a long <position> <vector> with slow transverse motion can sweep the same <area> per unit <time> as a short one with fast transverse motion. Constancy requires a dynamical condition, given by the <torque criterion for constant areal velocity>.

Choose a fixed origin $O$ in an <inertial frame> and a fixed <plane> through $O$, with constant <unit vector> $\mathbf n$ normal to it. For a differentiable trajectory in that <plane>, let $\mathbf r(t)$ be <position> relative to $O$ and $\mathbf v=\dot{\mathbf r}$ its <velocity>. Positive swept <area> follows the <right-hand rule> about $\mathbf n$. In positively oriented Cartesian coordinates in the <plane>, and in <plane polar coordinates> with <angle> $\theta$ in <radians>,
$$
\dot A=\frac12\mathbf n\cdot(\mathbf r\times\mathbf v)
=\frac12(x\dot y-y\dot x)
=\frac12r^2\dot\theta.
$$
Here the <dot product> selects the normal component of the <cross product>. Motion away from or towards $O$ alone contributes no <area>. The signed <specific angular momentum> component $h_n=\mathbf n\cdot(\mathbf r\times\mathbf v)$ obeys
$$
\dot A=\frac{h_n}{2}=\frac{L_n}{2m}
$$
for a constant <mass> $m$. If $\mathbf n$ is chosen along a nonzero conserved <specific angular momentum> <vector>, then $h_n=h=|\mathbf r\times\mathbf v|$ and the familiar positive formula is $\dot A=h/2$. Reversing the orientation changes the sign; the instantaneous unsigned swept-<area> rate is $|\dot A|$.

= Signed swept area
{parent=Areal velocity}
{title2=$A(t)-A(t_0)=\frac12\int_{t_0}^t(x\dot y-y\dot x)\,ds$}

Signed swept area counts the <area> traversed by a <position> <vector>, positively or negatively according to its direction of turning. For a continuously differentiable planar trajectory, its accumulated value relative to $t_0$ is the <line integral>
$$
A(t)-A(t_0)=\frac12\int_{t_0}^t\mathbf n\cdot(\mathbf r(s)\times\dot{\mathbf r}(s))\,ds.
$$
A short segment contributes the oriented <triangle> <area> $\tfrac12\mathbf n\cdot(\mathbf r\times d\mathbf r)$; taking its <time> <derivative> gives <areal velocity>. Repeated turns count repeated <area>; a reversal subtracts it. Thus this quantity need not equal the <area> of the union of all previously swept regions. The origin matters: the relevant origin for <Kepler's second law> is the attracting focus of the <ellipse>, rather than its geometric centre.

= Equal-time sectors of a Kepler ellipse
{parent=Areal velocity}

Take an illustrative <elliptic Kepler orbit> with <semi-major axis> $a=1$, <orbital eccentricity> $e=0.6$ and <gravitational parameter> $\mu=1$, in consistent dimensionless units. Its <semi-minor axis> is $b=a\sqrt{1-e^2}=0.8$, <mean motion> is $n=\sqrt{\mu/a^3}=1$, and <specific angular momentum> magnitude is $h=\sqrt{\mu a(1-e^2)}=0.8$. About the attracting focus,
$$
\dot A=0.4,\qquad \Delta A=0.4\,\Delta t.
$$
The two sectors below each span $\Delta t=0.5$, giving $\Delta A=0.2$. Near <periapsis> the short radius sweeps a wide <angle>; near <apoapsis> the long radius sweeps a narrow <angle>. Constant <areal velocity> therefore does not require constant <angular velocity> or constant <speed>.

\Image[kepler-ellipse-equal-time-swept-sectors.png]
{title=Equal <time> intervals sweep equal <areas> about the focus of an illustrative <elliptic Kepler orbit>}

To compute the sectors without mistaking equal <angles> for equal <times>, parametrize the <ellipse> by <eccentric anomaly> $E$:
$$
x=a(\cos E-e),\qquad y=b\sin E,\qquad
A(E_2)-A(E_1)=\frac{ab}{2}\bigl(E_2-E_1-e(\sin E_2-\sin E_1)\bigr).
$$
This follows by integrating $\tfrac12(x\,dy-y\,dx)$. <Kepler's equation> gives $E-e\sin E=M=n(t-t_{\mathrm p})$, with <mean anomaly> $M$ measured from <periapsis> <time> $t_{\mathrm p}$. Consequently $\Delta A=abn\Delta t/2=h\Delta t/2$. The displayed sectors use <mean anomaly> intervals $[-0.25,0.25]$ and $[\pi-0.25,\pi+0.25]$, rather than equal <eccentric anomaly> increments.

= Orbital observations behind the area law
{parent=Areal velocity}

Why infer an <area> law from <astronomy>? In the authored account <The great Martian catastrophe and how Kepler fixed it by Owen Gingerich>, a 13 August 1593 <Mars> observation has <ecliptic longitude> $346^\circ7.5'$, against predicted $342^\circ0'$ and $351^\circ26'$. These are Earth-based directions with a date, not measured Sun-based <areas>. The observer's <armillary sphere> used graduated circles and sighting arms to read <declination>; reversing it allowed a second reading. Comparing <stars> at different altitudes exposed <refraction>, and a planet-based check revealed an erroneous solar correction table. The apparent <Mars> distance signal had been contaminated by that correction. <Johannes Kepler> subsequently corrected the observer's <Earth> <orbit>, then tested <Mars> trajectories. The account describes typical angular errors of $2'$ or less ($120$ <arcseconds>): small residuals needed care.<The great Martian catastrophe and how Kepler fixed it by Owen Gingerich>[pp. 51-53: Fig. 2, refraction checks, 13 August log table, Earth-orbit correction and angular errors]{ref}

The Tufts class notes <The Newtonian Revolution – Part One; Philosophy 167: Science Before Newton's Principia/Kepler's Astronomia Nova and the Orbit of Mars> explain the key limitation: an <area> requires a trajectory and distances, so <Kepler's second law> cannot be directly tested from angular observations alone. They describe checking the combined <ellipse> and <area> law against 28 dated observations around seven oppositions during 1583-1595, using an <Earth> model to predict Earth-based directions; agreement improved greatly but some discrepancies remained.<The Newtonian Revolution – Part One; Philosophy 167: Science Before Newton's Principia/Kepler's Astronomia Nova and the Orbit of Mars>[p. 20, items 3-5, and pp. 26-27, section IV.D: predictions, residuals and dependence of the area test on a trajectory]{ref}

The inferential chain is useful today too: recover relative <positions> and <times>, compare swept <areas>, and ask what <forces> are compatible with their evolution. Agreement with equal <areas> supports zero <torque> about the chosen origin under the assumptions below; it alone does not identify an <inverse-square law>. Recovering the radial <force> also needs the trajectory and its timing, as in <inferring an inverse-square force from Kepler laws>. The two diagrams here are calculated examples, not the historical measurements.

= Armillary sphere
{parent=Orbital observations behind the area law}
{wiki}

= Ecliptic longitude
{parent=Orbital observations behind the area law}
{title2=$\lambda$}

An angular coordinate around the <celestial sphere> measured along the <Earth>'s orbital reference <plane>. An Earth-based <ecliptic longitude> specifies a viewing direction; it does not by itself give a body's distance or its Sun-based <position>.

= Torque criterion for constant areal velocity
{parent=Areal velocity}
{title2=$\ddot A=\tau_n/(2m)$}

Assume a constant positive <mass> $m$, a twice differentiable trajectory in the fixed <plane>, a fixed origin in an <inertial frame>, and <Newton's second law> for the net <force>. The <product rule> gives the short proof
$$
2\ddot A=\mathbf n\cdot\frac{d}{dt}(\mathbf r\times\mathbf v)
=\mathbf n\cdot(\mathbf r\times\mathbf a)
=\frac{\tau_n}{m},\qquad
\boldsymbol\tau=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}.
$$
The term $\mathbf v\times\mathbf v$ vanishes. This is the planar <angular momentum> balance written in <area> language.<University Physics Volume 1>[Section 11.2, equations 11.5-11.6: particle angular momentum and its torque balance]{ref}

On a connected <time> interval, <areal velocity> is constant if and only if $\tau_n=0$ throughout. Since both <position> and <acceleration> lie in the fixed <plane>, the net <torque> has only that normal component. For $r\ne0$, zero net <torque> is therefore equivalent to net <force> parallel to $\mathbf r$: its instantaneous direction is central, possibly inward, outward or zero. This diagnoses the net <force> along this motion, not its dependence on <position> or <time> elsewhere. Opposing transverse <forces> may cancel.

A <central force> about a fixed origin gives <conservation of angular momentum>, irrespective of its radial dependence: an <inverse-square law> is sufficient but unnecessary. With nonzero conserved <angular momentum>, $\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf L=0$ fixes the <orbital plane>. Zero <angular momentum> gives radial motion and zero <areal velocity>; it does not select a unique <orbital plane>. The converse needs the fixed-plane assumption: a constant projected <areal velocity> in three dimensions only constrains one <torque> component. Also, $r=0$ makes the instantaneous <torque> vanish regardless of <force> and makes <plane polar coordinates> singular. A moving origin or changing plane requires additional terms.

= Torque-driven circular swept area
{parent=Torque criterion for constant areal velocity}

A path along a <circle> can have variable <areal velocity>. As an illustrative controlled trajectory, keep a <point mass> on a <circle> of radius $R$ with <angle>
$$
\theta(t)=\omega_0t+\frac12\alpha t^2.
$$
Its <acceleration in polar coordinates> and the required net <force> are
$$
\mathbf a=-R(\omega_0+\alpha t)^2\mathbf e_r+R\alpha\mathbf e_\theta,
\qquad \mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}=m\mathbf a,
$$
where $\mathbf e_r$ and $\mathbf e_\theta$ are the radial and transverse <unit vectors>. A circular guide can supply the radial <force>, while a tangential drive supplies $mR\alpha$. Hence
$$
\tau_n=mR^2\alpha,\qquad
\dot A=\frac{R^2}{2}(\omega_0+\alpha t),\qquad
\ddot A=\frac{R^2\alpha}{2}.
$$
For $R=m=\omega_0=\alpha=1$ in illustrative units, the three equal <time> intervals $[0,0.5]$, $[0.5,1]$ and $[1,1.5]$ sweep <areas> $0.3125$, $0.4375$ and $0.5625$. Their difference comes from the transverse <force>'s positive <torque>, even though the radius and path shape stay fixed. The <area> under each <areal velocity> graph segment equals the corresponding swept <area>.

\Image[torque-driven-circular-area-rate.png]
{title=A transverse <force> gives increasing <areal velocity> and unequal swept <areas> in equal <times>}

= Energy
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{wiki}

Energy is a conserved scalar quantity associated with time-translation symmetry.

= Energy function
{parent=Energy}

For a Lagrangian $L(q,\dot q,t)$, its energy function is
$$
E=\sum_i\dot q_i\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot q_i}-L.
$$
It is conserved along Euler-Lagrange trajectories when $L$ has no explicit time dependence.

= Mechanical energy
{title2=$E=T+V$}
{parent=Energy}
{wiki}

Mechanical energy is the sum of <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>. It is conserved when all forces are conservative and the potential has no explicit time dependence.

= Conservation of mechanical energy
{parent=Mechanical energy}
{title2=$T+V=\mathrm{constant}$}

For constant masses, a time-independent <potential energy> $V$ and forces $\boldsymbol F_i=-\nabla_iV+\boldsymbol R_i$ with zero total extra power $\sum_i\boldsymbol R_i\cdot\boldsymbol v_i=0$, <Newton's second law> gives $dT/dt=-dV/dt$. Thus <mechanical energy> is conserved. Smooth stationary constraint reactions and a purely magnetic <Lorentz force> supply zero power. This is more restrictive than <conservation of energy> in general, which may include thermal or other internal forms.

= Work
{parent=Energy}
{title2=$W=\int_C\mathbf F\cdot d\mathbf r$}
{wiki=Work_(physics)}

<Work> measures the <energy> transferred by a <force> as its point of application moves. For a <point mass> following a path $C$, add the contribution of the <force> along each small <displacement>:
$$
W=\int_C\mathbf F\cdot d\mathbf r
=\int_{t_i}^{t_f}\mathbf F(t)\cdot\mathbf v(t)\,dt.
$$
This <line integral> uses the actual path, and the <dot product> selects the component of <force> along the motion. A large <force> perpendicular to every small <displacement> does zero <work>; holding something still also does zero mechanical <work> on it, even if holding it feels tiring. The <SI> unit is $\mathrm J=\mathrm{N\,m}$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/7-1-work[OpenStax University Physics 1, §7.1: Work]{ref}

The usefulness of <work> is that it connects a description in terms of <forces> and distances to one in terms of <energy>. The <work-energy theorem> gives the change in <kinetic energy> from the total net <work>, often without solving for the entire motion. The <work> of one selected <force> equals that change only when the other <forces> contribute zero total <work>.

= Work done by a constant force
{parent=Work}
{title2=$W=Fd\cos\theta$}

For a fixed <force> $\mathbf F$ and endpoint <displacement> $\Delta\mathbf r$, the <line integral> reduces to a <dot product>:
$$
W=\mathbf F\cdot\Delta\mathbf r=Fd\cos\theta,
\qquad F=|\mathbf F|,\quad d=|\Delta\mathbf r|.
$$
Here $\theta$ is the <angle> between the <force> and the <displacement>. For straight motion, $d$ is also the distance traveled. A $3\,\mathrm N$ <force> acting along a $4\,\mathrm m$ <displacement> does $12\,\mathrm J$ of <work>. Turning the same <force> perpendicular to the <displacement> gives $\cos(\pi/2)=0$ and zero <work>; reversing it gives $\cos\pi=-1$ and $-12\,\mathrm J$. At $\theta=\pi/3$, the <work> is $6\,\mathrm J$. These signs describe <energy> transferred to or from the moving body. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/7-1-work[OpenStax University Physics 1, §7.1: Constant-force work]{ref}

\Image[work-force-and-displacement.png]
{title=<Work> from parallel, perpendicular, and opposing <forces>}
{description=Each left panel shows a $3\,\mathrm N$ <force> and a rightward $4\,\mathrm m$ <displacement>. Each right panel plots the along-path <force> component against distance; its signed area is the <work>: $12$, $0$, or $-12\,\mathrm J$. The arrows compare directions; their lengths do not compare quantities with different units. The three panels prescribe motion to illustrate <work>; they are separate cases, not successive stages of one journey.}

= Work-energy theorem
{parent=Work}
{title2=$W_{\mathrm{net}}=\Delta T$}

For a <point mass> of constant <mass> $m$ observed in an <inertial frame>, the net <work> along its actual path equals its change in <kinetic energy>:
$$
W_{\mathrm{net}}=\int_{t_a}^{t_b}\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}(t)\cdot\mathbf v(t)\,dt
=\int_a^b\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}\cdot d\mathbf r
=\frac12m|\mathbf v_b|^2-\frac12m|\mathbf v_a|^2.
$$
The middle expression is a <line integral>; the <dot product> selects the component of <force> along the <displacement>. Positive net <work> increases <speed>, negative net <work> decreases it, and zero net <work> leaves the endpoint <speeds> equal. Equal endpoint <speeds> do not imply equal <velocities>: a perpendicular <force> can continually change direction while doing zero <work>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-01sc-classical-mechanics-fall-2016/mit8_01scs22_chapter13.pdf[MIT mechanics, Chapter 13, section 13.11]{ref}

The bridge to <Newton's second law> is short: take the <dot product> of $m\dot{\mathbf v}=\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}$ with <velocity>. The <chain rule> gives $d(m|\mathbf v|^2/2)/dt=\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}\cdot\mathbf v$, equating the rate of change of <kinetic energy> to instantaneous <power>. Integrating gives the theorem. No <conservative force> assumption entered this argument; <friction> also contributes to the net <work>.

For the <harmonic oscillator> with <spring> stiffness $k=2\,\mathrm{N/m}$, release a <mass> $m=0.5\,\mathrm{kg}$ from rest at $x=0.3\,\mathrm m$. The <spring> exerts $F=-kx$. Its <work> before the first passage through $x=0$ is
$$
W=\int_{0.3\,\mathrm m}^{0}(-kx)\,dx
=\frac12k(0.3\,\mathrm m)^2=0.09\,\mathrm J.
$$
Thus $mv^2/2=0.09\,\mathrm J$ gives <speed> $0.6\,\mathrm{m/s}$ there. The <velocity> is negative on this passage because the <position> is decreasing. The <energy> calculation alone determines its magnitude, so the direction still requires information about the motion.

For a time-independent <potential energy> $V$, the <conservative force> contributes <work> $-\Delta V$. Separating all other <work> as $W_{\mathrm{other}}$ gives $\Delta(T+V)=W_{\mathrm{other}}$. Consequently <conservation of mechanical energy> requires zero net other <work>; the <work-energy theorem> itself does not require that conservation. With <damping>, <mechanical energy> decreases even though the theorem remains valid. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-01sc-classical-mechanics-fall-2016/mit8_01scs22_chapter14.pdf[MIT mechanics, Chapter 14: mechanical energy and nonconservative work]{ref}

= Power
{parent=Work}
{title2=$P=dW/dt$}
{wiki=Power_(physics)}

<Power> describes how quickly <energy> is transferred. For mechanical <work>, the instantaneous <power> supplied by a <force> is
$$
P(t)=\frac{dW}{dt}=\mathbf F(t)\cdot\mathbf v(t),
\qquad W=\int_{t_i}^{t_f}P(t)\,dt.
$$
The <SI> unit is $\mathrm W=\mathrm{J/s}=\mathrm{N\,m/s}$, distinct from the <velocity> unit $\mathrm{m/s}$. The italic symbol $W$ for <work> and the upright unit $\mathrm W$ for <power> mean different things. Two processes can transfer the same <energy> while requiring different <powers>: doing $12\,\mathrm J$ of <work> in $2\,\mathrm s$ requires an average $6\,\mathrm W$, whereas doing it in $4\,\mathrm s$ requires an average $3\,\mathrm W$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/7-4-power[OpenStax University Physics 1, §7.4: Power]{ref}

= Instantaneous power
{parent=Power}
{title2=$P(t)=\mathbf F(t)\cdot\mathbf v(t)$}

<Instantaneous power> is <power> at a particular moment. Consider a <point mass> with <mass> $m=2\,\mathrm{kg}$, initially at rest, acted on by a constant net <force> $F=3\,\mathrm N$ along a straight path. <Newton's second law> gives constant <acceleration> $a=F/m=1.5\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, hence <velocity> $v(t)=at$ and
$$
P(t)=Fat=(4.5\,\mathrm{W/s})t.
$$
The <instantaneous power> starts at zero even though the <force> is nonzero, because the initial <velocity> is zero. At distance $4\,\mathrm m$, the <kinetic energy> example below gives <speed> $\sqrt{12}\,\mathrm{m/s}$, so the final <instantaneous power> is $3\sqrt{12}\,\mathrm W\approx10.3923\,\mathrm W$. Negative <instantaneous power> means the selected <force> is removing <energy> at that moment.

= Average power
{parent=Power}
{title2=$\overline P=W/(t_f-t_i)$}

<Average power> divides the <work> over a nonzero interval by the elapsed <time>. For the same $2\,\mathrm{kg}$ <point mass> driven from rest through $4\,\mathrm m$ by a net $3\,\mathrm N$ <force>, the <displacement> is $x(t)=at^2/2$. Thus
$$
t_f=\sqrt{\frac{2(4\,\mathrm m)}{1.5\,\mathrm{m/s^2}}}
=\sqrt{16/3}\,\mathrm s\approx2.309401\,\mathrm s,
\qquad \overline P=\frac{12\,\mathrm J}{t_f}
\approx5.19615\,\mathrm W.
$$
This <average power> is half the final <instantaneous power> because $P(t)$ is a <linear function> of <time>, starting at zero. Its <graph of a function> bounds a <triangle> of area $P(t_f)t_f/2=12\,\mathrm J$; the horizontal line at <average power> encloses a <rectangle> with the same area. In general, an endpoint value of <instantaneous power> does not determine <average power>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/7-4-power[OpenStax University Physics 1, §7.4: Average and instantaneous power]{ref}

= Kinetic energy
{parent=Energy}
{title2=$K=\frac12mv^2$}
{wiki}

<Kinetic energy> is the <energy> associated with motion. For a nonrelativistic <point mass> of <mass> $m>0$, measured in a specified <inertial frame>,
$$
K=\frac12m\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf v=\frac12mv^2,
\qquad v=|\mathbf v|.
$$
Here $\mathbf v$ is <velocity> and $v$ is <speed>; $T$ is another common symbol for <kinetic energy>. The <dot product> makes $K$ nonnegative regardless of the direction of <velocity>. At fixed <mass>, doubling <speed> quadruples <kinetic energy>: a $2\,\mathrm{kg}$ <point mass> at $2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ has $4\,\mathrm J$, whereas at $4\,\mathrm{m/s}$ it has $16\,\mathrm J$. The <SI> units are $\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s^2}=\mathrm J$. Different <reference frames> can assign different <velocities> and therefore different <kinetic energies>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/7-2-kinetic-energy[OpenStax University Physics 1, §7.2: Kinetic energy]{ref}

A short outline explains why <kinetic energy> has this formula. In a nonrelativistic <inertial frame>, constant <mass> and <Newton's second law> give, using the <chain rule>,
$$
\frac{dK}{dt}=m\mathbf v\cdot\frac{d\mathbf v}{dt}
=\mathbf F_{\mathrm{net}}\cdot\mathbf v.
$$
Integrating over <time> proves the <work-energy theorem>, $W_{\mathrm{net}}=K_f-K_i$. The net <force> is the sum of all <forces>, and $W_{\mathrm{net}}$ is their total <work>, not merely the <work> of a chosen driving <force>. This route to final <speed> is useful when the <forces> as <functions> of <position> are known but the full motion as a function of <time> would be harder to find. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/7-3-work-energy-theorem[OpenStax University Physics 1, §7.3: Work–energy theorem]{ref}

In the running example, a $2\,\mathrm{kg}$ <point mass> starts at rest and receives a constant net $3\,\mathrm N$ <force> through $4\,\mathrm m$. Its net <work> is $12\,\mathrm J$, so
$$
K_f=12\,\mathrm J,
\qquad v_f=\sqrt{\frac{2K_f}{m}}
=\sqrt{12}\,\mathrm{m/s}\approx3.4641\,\mathrm{m/s}.
$$
At any intermediate distance $x\ge0$, $K(x)=(3\,\mathrm N)x$ and $v(x)=\sqrt{2Fx/m}$: <kinetic energy> is a <linear function> of distance, while <speed> is proportional to the <square root> of distance. If a net opposing $3\,\mathrm N$ braking <force> subsequently acts for $4\,\mathrm m$, it does $-12\,\mathrm J$ of <work> and brings this <point mass> to rest. Negative net <work> decreases <kinetic energy>; it does not make <kinetic energy> negative. Beyond the stopping point the motion must be reconsidered, since the <point mass> cannot keep moving forward with a negative $K$.

\Image[kinetic-energy-work-and-power.png]
{title=<Kinetic energy>, <velocity>, and <power> for a constant net <force>}
{description=A $2\,\mathrm{kg}$ <point mass> starts at rest under a net $3\,\mathrm N$ <force>. The left two panels show rightward <velocity> $v(x)=\sqrt{2Fx/m}$ and <kinetic energy> $K(x)=Fx$ over $0\le x\le4\,\mathrm m$, with separate axes for their different units. The right panel shows <instantaneous power> $P(t)=Fat$ up to $t_f\approx2.309401\,\mathrm s$ and <average power> $\overline P\approx5.19615\,\mathrm W$. The shaded area is $12\,\mathrm J$ of <work>. All values follow from the stated idealized model.}

<Kinetic energy> also makes the exchange with <potential energy> intuitive: a falling body's loss of <gravitational potential energy> can appear as increased <kinetic energy>. The formula above applies when <speed> is small compared with the <speed of light>; at higher <speeds>, <special relativity> changes the expression. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_04.html[Feynman Lectures I, chapter 4, §§4–2 and 4–3: Energy of position and motion]{ref}

= Kinetic energy decomposition about the center of mass
{parent=Kinetic energy}
{title2=$T=\frac12M|\dot R|^2+\frac12\sum_i m_i|\dot\rho_i|^2$}

= König theorem for kinetic energy
{c}
{synonym}

Writing particle positions relative to the <center of mass> as $\rho_i=r_i-R$ makes $\sum_i m_i\rho_i=0$, so the cross term in the squared <velocities> vanishes. The total <kinetic energy> splits into center-of-mass translation and relative motion as displayed. For a <rigid body>, the latter is $\tfrac12 I\omega^2$, where $I$ is the <moment of inertia> about the instantaneous rotation axis through the <center of mass>.

= Unit-direction kinetic-energy identity
{parent=Kinetic energy}
{title2=$v^2=(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf v)^2-r^2\mathbf u\cdot\ddot{\mathbf u}$}

For a twice differentiable position away from the origin, write $\mathbf r=r\mathbf u$ with radial <unit vector> $\mathbf u$. Its unit-length constraint gives $\mathbf u\cdot\dot{\mathbf u}=0$ and $\mathbf u\cdot\ddot{\mathbf u}=-|\dot{\mathbf u}|^2$, separating radial and directional contributions to speed and <kinetic energy>. The identity is kinematic and does not require a particular force law.

= Kinetic energy density
{parent=Kinetic energy}

The kinetic energy density of a continuum with mass density $\rho$ and velocity $\mathbf v$ is $\rho|\mathbf v|^2/2$. For a displacement field, the material velocity is its time derivative.

= Rotational kinetic energy
{title2=$K=\frac12I\omega^2$}
{parent=Kinetic energy}
{wiki}

<Rotational kinetic energy> is the <kinetic energy> of a <rigid body>'s rotation. For pure rotation about an axis fixed in an <inertial frame>,
$$
K_{\mathrm{rot}}=\frac12I\omega^2,
\qquad I=\int r_\perp^2\,dm,
\qquad \omega=|\boldsymbol\omega|.
$$
Here $r_\perp$ is the perpendicular distance of a <mass> element $dm$ from that axis, $I$ is the <moment of inertia> about that same axis, and $\omega$ is the common <angular speed>, measured in <radians> per second. The <rigid body> assumption fixes the relative positions of the <mass> elements; they share <angular velocity> but have different <speeds> $v=\omega r_\perp$. Summing their <kinetic energies> $dm\,v^2/2$ gives the formula. This is a nonrelativistic model; deformation or internal motion can contribute additional <kinetic energy>.<University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/10.4 Moment of Inertia and Rotational Kinetic Energy>[§10.4]{ref}

For a computed example, take a uniform disc and an ideal thin ring, each of <mass> $M=2\,\mathrm{kg}$ and radius $R=0.30\,\mathrm m$, rotating about its central perpendicular axis at <angular speed> $10\,\mathrm{rad/s}$. The <moment of inertia of a uniform disc> is $MR^2/2=0.090\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$, while the ring has <moment of inertia> $MR^2=0.180\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$. Their <rotational kinetic energies> are therefore $4.5\,\mathrm J$ and $9.0\,\mathrm J$. The ring stores twice the <energy> at the same <angular speed>, because more <mass> lies farther from the axis. At the same nonzero <rotational kinetic energy>, its <angular speed> would instead be $1/\sqrt{2}$ times the disc's. This makes radial placement useful for storing <energy> in a rotating wheel, although a real design must also withstand the stresses of rotation.

For fixed-axis rotation with constant <moment of inertia>, write the signed rotation rate as $\Omega=\dot\theta$, with <angle> $\theta$ in <radians>. The axial component of net external <torque> obeys $\tau_{\mathrm{axis}}=I\dot\Omega$, so the <work-energy theorem> becomes
$$
\frac{dK_{\mathrm{rot}}}{dt}=\tau_{\mathrm{axis}}\Omega,
\qquad \Delta K_{\mathrm{rot}}=\int\tau_{\mathrm{axis}}\,d\theta.
$$
An opposing <torque> removes <rotational kinetic energy>; bearing <friction> must be included in the net <torque>. The signed $\Omega$ matters when the direction reverses. Rotation about a fixed axis need not be about a <principal axis>: the scalar <energy> formula still holds, but transverse bearing <torques> can be needed to maintain that axis.

For general <rigid body> motion, use the <inertia tensor> $\mathsf I_G$ about the <centre of mass> $G$:
$$
K_{\mathrm{total}}=\frac12M|\mathbf V_G|^2+\frac12\boldsymbol\omega^{\mathsf T}\mathsf I_G\boldsymbol\omega,
\qquad \mathbf L_G=\mathsf I_G\boldsymbol\omega.
$$
This is the <kinetic-energy decomposition about the centre of mass>. In a <principal body frame>, the rotational term is $\tfrac12(I_1\omega_1^2+I_2\omega_2^2+I_3\omega_3^2)$, using the <principal moments of inertia>. Away from a <principal axis>, <angular momentum> and <angular velocity> need not be parallel. A freely rotating asymmetric <rigid body> can change the direction of <angular velocity> relative to the body even while <rotational kinetic energy> remains constant; <Euler equations for a torque-free rigid body> govern that motion. One constant axial <moment of inertia> is then insufficient.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 20: Rotation in space>{ref}

= Potential energy
{parent=Energy}
{title2=$\mathbf F=-\nabla V$}
{wiki}

<Potential energy> assigns <energy> to a system's arrangement, so a change of arrangement accounts for the <work> done by a <conservative force>. For a time-independent <potential energy> $V(\mathbf r)$,
$$
\mathbf F=-\nabla V,
\qquad W_{\mathrm{conservative}}=V_i-V_f=-\Delta V.
$$
The <gradient> points toward increasing <potential energy>; the <force> points toward decreasing <potential energy>. Only differences matter: adding a constant to $V$ changes neither the <force> nor its <work>. This endpoint rule follows by substituting $-\nabla V$ into the <line integral> for <work>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/8-2-conservative-and-non-conservative-forces[OpenStax University Physics 1, §8.2: Conservative forces and potential energy]{ref}

Near <Earth>'s surface, stipulate constant <gravitational acceleration> $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$ and measure height $h$ upward from a chosen zero. Then <gravitational potential energy> is $V=mgh$. Raising a $2\,\mathrm{kg}$ <mass> by $0.5\,\mathrm m$ increases its <gravitational potential energy> by $9.81\,\mathrm J$, while <gravity> does $-9.81\,\mathrm J$ of <work>. These are computed model values, not measurements of a particular lift. An ideal <spring> obeying <Hooke's law> instead has $V=kx^2/2$, with <displacement> $x$ from its relaxed state and <spring constant> $k$: $k=100\,\mathrm{N/m}$ and $x=0.2\,\mathrm m$ give $V=2\,\mathrm J$ relative to $x=0$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/8-1-potential-energy-of-a-system[OpenStax University Physics 1, §8.1: Gravity and spring examples]{ref}

= Repulsive inverse-square potential
{parent=Potential energy}
{title2=$V(r)=c/r^2,\quad c>0$}

This <potential energy> generates a repulsive <central force> $F_r=-V'(r)=2c/r^3$. It is an inverse-square potential but an inverse-cube force; the powers must not be confused with the Coulomb or gravitational potential proportional to $1/r$.

= Scattering in a repulsive inverse-square potential
{parent=Repulsive inverse-square potential}
{title2=$r=r_{\min}\sec[\beta(\theta-\theta_0)]$}

For $V(r)=c/r^2$ with $c>0$, nonzero physical <angular momentum> $h$ and positive <energy> $E$, put $\beta=\sqrt{1+2mc/h^2}>1$ and $r_{\min}=\sqrt{(c+h^2/(2m))/E}$. Direct integration of the <effective potential> radial equation gives the displayed <orbit>. It has one radial minimum and two straight-line asymptotes separated by polar angle $\pi/\beta$. The repulsive scattering deflection is $\pi-\pi/\beta$; no bounded or circular orbit exists at finite positive energy.

= Gravitational energy
{parent=Potential energy}
{title2=$E_{\rm grav}$}
{wiki}

= Gravitational potential energy
{synonym}

The potential energy associated with gravitational interactions. For a self-gravitating spherical body with a fixed dimensionless density profile, $E_{\rm grav}=-C GM^2/R$ for a positive profile-dependent constant $C$; a uniform sphere has $C=3/5$. The $R^{-1}$ scaling competes with the same radius dependence of ultrarelativistic <electron degeneracy pressure> in a white dwarf, giving the <Chandrasekhar mass> scale.

= Available potential energy
{parent=Potential energy}
{title2=$A$}

The portion of a fluid system's <potential energy> that can be released by admissible rearrangement relative to a stable reference state. In a uniformly stratified incompressible fluid, the quadratic density is $A=\rho_0N^2\xi_z^2/2=\rho_0b^2/(2N^2)$, where $\xi_z$ is vertical <fluid displacement> and $b$ the buoyancy perturbation. It is positive for stable stratification and measures the restoring energy of <internal gravity waves>.

= Background potential energy
{parent=Available potential energy}
{title2=$P_b$}

The background potential energy is the <gravitational potential energy> of the statically stable state obtained by adiabatically sorting a fluid's parcels. The difference $A=P-P_b$ is <available potential energy>. Pure rearrangement changes $A$ but not $P_b$; irreversible density mixing can increase $P_b$. Molecular exchange with <internal energy> must be separated when defining an irreversible mixing rate.

= Potential function
{parent=Potential energy}

A potential function $V$ assigns a <scalar> to each point of a configuration or spatial domain. In mechanics it represents <potential energy>, and its negative <gradient> gives the corresponding <conservative force>.

= Central potential
{parent=Potential energy}
{wiki}

A central potential depends only on distance from a fixed centre, $V(\mathbf x)=V(|\mathbf x|)$. Its force is radial, and rotational symmetry implies conservation of <angular momentum>.

= Conservative force
{parent=Potential energy}
{wiki}

A force is conservative when its work depends only on the endpoints. On a simply connected domain this is equivalent to $F=-\nabla V$ for a <potential energy> $V$.

= Nonconservative force
{parent=Conservative force}

A nonconservative force has path-dependent work. On a simply connected domain, a continuously differentiable force is nonconservative exactly when its curl does not vanish identically.

= Inverse-square potential
{title2=$V(r)=-k/r$}
{parent=Potential energy}

Here <inverse-square potential> means the <potential energy> associated with an attractive <inverse-square law> for a <central force>: the <potential energy> itself varies as inverse distance. For $k>0$ and <position> $\mathbf r$ with $r=|\mathbf r|>0$,
$$
V(r)=-\frac{k}{r},\qquad V(\infty)=0,\qquad \mathbf F=-\nabla V=-\frac{k}{r^2}\widehat{\mathbf r}=-\frac{k\mathbf r}{r^3}.
$$
The <gradient> makes the distinction explicit: doubling $r$ halves the magnitude of <potential energy> but quarters the magnitude of <force>. The neighboring <repulsive inverse-square potential> instead has <potential energy> proportional to $r^{-2}$ and an inverse-cube <force>; the two naming conventions must be distinguished.

For <Newton's law of universal gravitation>, $k=GMm$, where $G$ is the <gravitational constant>. Treat the attracting <mass> $M$ as an isolated fixed <point mass>, or as a body with <spherical symmetry> and exterior <test particle> of constant <mass> $m\ll M$. The <spherical shell theorem> gives the same exterior <Newtonian gravitational potential energy> $U=-GMm/r$ without requiring uniform <mass density>. Here $r$ is measured from the centre, and the formula applies outside the body; it does not describe its interior. The <Newtonian gravitational potential> per unit <mass> is $\Phi=U/m=-GM/r$.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 13: Work and Potential Energy (A)>[§13–2, equations 13.11–13.12: radial work and conserved energy; §13–4: gravitational field outside a sphere]{ref}

The negative sign records binding relative to infinite separation, rather than a negative <kinetic energy>. Moving from $r_1$ to $r_2>r_1$ increases <potential energy> by
$$
\Delta V=k\left(\frac1{r_1}-\frac1{r_2}\right)>0,\qquad W_{\mathrm{gravity}}=-\Delta V.
$$
Because the <force> is a <conservative force>, this <work> depends on the endpoints, including for a curved path. Integrating $dV/dr=k/r^2$ with zero at infinity gives $V=-k/r$; adding a constant leaves the <force> unchanged but shifts the numerical escape <energy> threshold by that same constant. This is why the reference at infinity must accompany an <escape velocity> calculation.

For an exact synthetic comparison, stipulate the <standard gravitational parameter> $\mu=GM=8\,\mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ and a $1\,\mathrm{kg}$ <test particle>. At $r=2\,\mathrm m$, $U=-4\,\mathrm J$ and the inward <force> has magnitude $2\,\mathrm N$; at $r=4\,\mathrm m$, $U=-2\,\mathrm J$ and the inward <force> has magnitude $0.5\,\mathrm N$. The increase is $2\,\mathrm J$ of <potential energy>. These stipulated parameters are an arithmetic example, not observations of a particular body. The <escape-energy balance diagram> uses the same parameters.

= Angular momentum and the escape energy threshold
{parent=Inverse-square potential}
{title2=$\varepsilon=\tfrac12\dot r^2+\tfrac{h^2}{2r^2}-\tfrac\mu r$}

In the isolated model, <conservation of energy> and <angular momentum conservation> reduce the exterior <test particle>'s motion to
$$
\varepsilon=\frac{E}{m}=\frac{\dot r^2}{2}+\frac{h^2}{2r^2}-\frac{\mu}{r},\qquad h=|\mathbf r\times\mathbf v|,\qquad \mu=GM.
$$
Here $\varepsilon$ is <specific orbital energy> and $h$ is <specific angular momentum>. The <effective potential> is $\Phi_{\mathrm{eff}}=h^2/(2r^2)-\mu/r$. A zero radial <velocity> is a radial <turning point>, and need not mean zero total <speed>: at the <periapsis> or <apoapsis> of a nonradial <Kepler orbit>, tangential <velocity> remains. Thus the vertical gap between $\varepsilon$ and $-\mu/r$ represents total <kinetic energy> per unit <mass>, whereas the gap above $\Phi_{\mathrm{eff}}$ represents its radial part.

For $h>0$, the <orbital eccentricity> obeys $e^2=1+2\varepsilon h^2/\mu^2$. Negative $\varepsilon$ gives an <elliptic orbit>, including a <circular orbit> at its minimum allowed <energy>; zero $\varepsilon$ gives a <parabolic trajectory>; positive $\varepsilon$ gives a <hyperbolic Kepler orbit>. The <effective potential> tends to zero from below at infinity, so its outer region creates no extra positive escape barrier. This explains why <escape velocity> depends on <energy> even though <angular momentum> changes the path. The $h=0$ case is radial motion, rather than a nondegenerate <parabola> or <hyperbola>; inward radial motion can end in collision.

= Falling bodies and lunar motion as tests of gravity
{parent=Inverse-square potential}

One motivating laboratory demonstration compares two balls initially at the same height. A switch releases one vertically when the other leaves a horizontal track; arranging their paths to meet gives a collision as the readout of equal downward <displacement> over equal <time>. Equal initial height and synchronized release control the comparison. This tests the local independence of horizontal motion and <free fall>, under nearly constant <gravitational acceleration>; it does not measure an <inverse-square law>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 7: The Theory of Gravitation>[§7–4, figure 7–3: synchronized release and midair collision]{ref}

A complementary astronomical comparison uses the <Moon>'s distance and <orbital period> to infer inward <centripetal acceleration>, $a=4\pi^2r/T^2$, and compares it with surface <gravitational acceleration> scaled by $(R/r)^2$. The approximately sixtyfold distance ratio gives the approximately $1/3600$ prediction. The inference links terrestrial <free fall> to a lunar <orbit>. Distance calibration matters; the <circular orbit> approximation and the <Sun>'s <tidal force> limit this elementary comparison.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 7: The Theory of Gravitation>[§7–4: lunar distance, period, and the inverse-square comparison]{ref}

= Lunar laser ranging
{parent=Inverse-square potential}

<Lunar laser ranging> gives a more discriminating <orbit> readout. An observatory directs short <laser> pulses at a <retroreflector> array on the <Moon>, and a detector records returning <photons>. Round-trip <time>, statistically combined over several returns, constrains distance; <pressure> and <temperature> accompany the readout. The fitted model includes <Newtonian gravity>, terrestrial and lunar rotation, geophysical effects, and corrections from <general relativity>. Comparing <orbit determination> residuals across stations and model choices checks consistency. The inspected study reports $1.8\,\mathrm{cm}$ weighted <root mean square> residuals for its 2005–2011 ranges, rather than for a bare isolated <inverse-square potential>. It tests the <equivalence principle> using the <Earth> and <Moon>'s response to the <Sun>; it is not a direct <escape velocity> measurement. Uneven coverage near new and full <Moon>, <radiation pressure>, and thermal expansion of reflector mounts limit that inference.<Lunar Laser Ranging Tests of the Equivalence Principle by James G. Williams and others>[§§2–3, pp. 3–6: pulse timing, normal points, model and figure 1; §4, pp. 7–8: consistency checks and radiation/thermal effects]{ref}

= Retroreflector
{parent=Lunar laser ranging}

A <retroreflector> returns incident <light> approximately toward its source. Corner-cube arrays make the <lunar laser ranging> round trip possible.

= Exponential cusp potential
{parent=Potential energy}

The even potential
$$
V(x)=V_\infty(1-e^{-|x|/\lambda})
$$
has a cusp at the origin and approaches the finite value $V_\infty$. Motions below that value oscillate between symmetric turning points; motions above it escape.

= Escape velocity
{parent=Potential energy}
{wiki}
{title2=$v_{\mathrm{esc}}=\sqrt{2GM/r}$}

= Escape speed
{synonym}

The <escape velocity> is the least initial <speed> that permits a freely coasting body to move arbitrarily far from an isolated attracting body. Despite its conventional name, it is a <speed>, not a unique <velocity> vector. For a fixed <point mass> $M$, or outside a body with <spherical symmetry>, set <Newtonian gravitational potential energy> zero at infinity. An exterior <test particle> of constant <mass> $m\ll M$ then has
$$
U(r)=-\frac{GMm}{r},\qquad E=\frac12mv^2-\frac{GMm}{r},\qquad v_{\mathrm{esc}}(r)=\sqrt{\frac{2GM}{r}}=\sqrt{\frac{2\mu}{r}}.
$$
Here $\mu=GM$ is the <standard gravitational parameter>, and $r$ is distance from the centre, not height above the surface. Set $r=R$ for surface <escape velocity>. Evaluate <velocity> in the body's rest <inertial frame>. Neglect <drag>, later <rocket thrust>, other bodies' <gravity>, and relativistic effects.<University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/13.3 Gravitational Potential Energy and Total Energy>[§13.3, equations 13.4–13.6 and “Escape velocity”: infinity reference, small test mass and exterior launch]{ref}

<Conservation of energy> gives the threshold by requiring $E\geq0$: at infinity the <potential energy> tends to zero and the <kinetic energy> cannot be negative. If $E=0$, the outward motion loses <speed> continuously toward zero. If $E>0$, the asymptotic <speed> is $v_\infty=\sqrt{2E/m}=\sqrt{v_0^2-v_{\mathrm{esc}}(r_0)^2}$. If $E<0$, infinity is inaccessible. For a radial outward launch, its outer <turning point> is $r_{\max}=-GMm/E$. For nonradial motion use <angular momentum and the escape energy threshold> to distinguish zero radial <velocity> from zero total <speed>.

Direction still determines the <orbit> and whether the body hits an obstacle. An initially inward <velocity> can cause collision even when $E\geq0$. For an isolated spherical body, an exterior outward launch, or a tangential launch at or above <escape velocity>, has an outward escape branch without intersecting the body. A <parabolic trajectory> is the nonradial threshold path; a <hyperbolic Kepler orbit> has positive <energy>. The formula specifies their required local <speed>, not their orientation.<University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/13.3 Gravitational Potential Energy and Total Energy>[§13.3, “Energy and gravitationally bound objects”: scalar energy and finite-body collisions]{ref}

= Escape-energy balance diagram
{parent=Escape velocity}

Use the synthetic <standard gravitational parameter> $\mu=8\,\mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ and launch distance $r_0=2\,\mathrm m$. Then <escape speed> is $2\sqrt2\,\mathrm{m/s}$. Three outward radial launches give exact comparisons: $v_0=2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ has <specific orbital energy> $-2\,\mathrm{J/kg}$ and stops outward at $r=4\,\mathrm m$; $v_0=2\sqrt2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ has zero <specific orbital energy>; $v_0=2\sqrt3\,\mathrm{m/s}$ has <specific orbital energy> $+2\,\mathrm{J/kg}$ and asymptotic <speed> $2\,\mathrm{m/s}$. The second case has <speed> $2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ at $r=4\,\mathrm m$. Doubling $r_0$ divides <escape speed> by $\sqrt2$, whereas doubling $M$ at fixed $r_0$ multiplies it by $\sqrt2$. Changing the <test particle>'s <mass> changes $E$ but cancels from the <escape speed>.

\Image[inverse-square-escape-energy-balance.png]
{title=The <inverse-square potential> and <kinetic energy> budget for three radial launch <speeds>}
{description=Computed curves use $x=r/r_0$ and $\mathcal E=\varepsilon/(\mu/r_0)$. The left panel compares $U/m=-\mu/r$ with constant <specific orbital energies> $\mathcal E=-1/2,0,+1/2$. The right panel shows <kinetic energy> per unit <mass>, divided by $\mu/r_0$, equal to $\mathcal E+1/x$. Only the physically allowed outward branch is drawn for negative <energy>, ending at $x=2$. These are synthetic predictions, not measurements.}

= Tangential launch trajectories across the escape threshold
{parent=Escape velocity}
{title2=$r=\tfrac{h^2/\mu}{1+e\cos f}$}

The same initial distance can lead to strikingly different <orbits>. Let $s=v_0/\sqrt{\mu/r_0}\geq1$ and launch tangentially in one <orbital plane>. Then <periapsis> is the launch point, <specific angular momentum> is $h=r_0v_0$, and
$$
\frac{\varepsilon}{\mu/r_0}=\frac{s^2}{2}-1,\qquad e=s^2-1,\qquad \frac{r}{r_0}=\frac{s^2}{1+(s^2-1)\cos f},
$$
where $f$ is <true anomaly>. These follow by combining the initial <specific orbital energy> with the <orbital eccentricity> relation and the <Kepler orbit> equation. With $\mu=8\,\mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ and $r_0=2\,\mathrm m$, the initial <speeds> $2$, $\sqrt6$, $2\sqrt2$, and $2\sqrt3\,\mathrm{m/s}$ give $e=0,1/2,1,2$: a <circular orbit>, an <elliptic orbit> with <apoapsis distance> $6\,\mathrm m$, a <parabolic trajectory>, and a <hyperbolic Kepler orbit>. The two open paths have asymptotic <speeds> zero and $2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ respectively. Their different directions at large distance are part of the <Kepler orbit>, not an extra scalar escape threshold.

\Image[escape-threshold-tangential-kepler-trajectories.png]
{title=Tangential launches give circular, elliptic, parabolic, and hyperbolic <Kepler orbits>}
{description=All panels use equal spatial scales, a fixed attracting centre at the origin, a launch point $(r_0,0)$, and an initially upward <velocity>. The gray body's radius is stipulated as $R=r_0/2$, so all displayed paths remain exterior. Colored solid curves follow motion after launch; dashed curves on the open <orbits> are their incoming extensions, not an additional part of the launch. Open curves continue beyond the viewing window. The normalized launch <speeds> and <specific orbital energies> are computed from the stated isolated model.}

= Zero-energy escape takes infinite time
{parent=Escape velocity}
{title2=$r(t)=[r_0^{3/2}+\tfrac32\sqrt{2\mu}\,t]^{2/3}$}

“Arrives at infinity with zero <speed>” is an asymptotic statement. For outward radial motion at zero <specific orbital energy>,
$$
\dot r=\sqrt{\frac{2\mu}{r}},\qquad t(r)=\frac{2}{3\sqrt{2\mu}}\left(r^{3/2}-r_0^{3/2}\right),\qquad r(t)=\left(r_0^{3/2}+\frac32\sqrt{2\mu}\,t\right)^{2/3}.
$$
Every finite distance takes finite <time>, but $t(r)\to\infty$ as $r\to\infty$. There is no instant of rest at an infinitely distant destination. Differentiating the displayed solution gives $\ddot r=-\mu/r^2$, independently checking <Newton's second law>. In the synthetic example, reaching $r=4\,\mathrm m$ takes exactly $(4-\sqrt2)/3\,\mathrm s\approx0.861929\,\mathrm s$. For nonzero <specific angular momentum>, the <Barker equation> gives the corresponding infinite-<time> limit along a <parabolic trajectory>. Positive-<energy> escape also takes infinite <time> to reach infinity; it retains nonzero asymptotic <speed>.

= Escape speed, circular speed, and propulsion
{parent=Escape velocity}

For a <circular orbit>, <gravity> supplies <centripetal acceleration>, giving $v_c^2/r=\mu/r^2$. Thus
$$
v_c=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r}},\qquad v_{\mathrm{esc}}=\sqrt2\,v_c,\qquad \varepsilon_c=-\frac{\mu}{2r}.
$$
A body in a <circular orbit> is bound despite its continuous <free fall>. At the same $r$, escape needs twice its <kinetic energy> per unit <mass>. An instantaneous forward tangential <velocity> increment from the <circular orbit> requires $\Delta v=(\sqrt2-1)v_c$, rather than the full <escape speed> from rest. In the synthetic example this is exactly $2(\sqrt2-1)\,\mathrm{m/s}\approx0.828427\,\mathrm{m/s}$.<University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/13.4 Satellite Orbits and Energy>[§13.4, “Circular Orbits” and “Energy in Circular Orbits”: centripetal balance and negative total energy]{ref}

<Escape speed> is a coasting threshold, not a required continuously maintained <speed> or a measure of <rocket propellant>. A powered vehicle can start at rest and acquire <energy> gradually. The <rocket equation> relates an ideal <velocity> increment to <rocket exhaust speed> and <rocket mass ratio>; <rocket thrust>, <drag>, and the timing and direction of propulsion affect a real ascent. Rotation also changes the relation between surface-relative <velocity> and the <inertial frame> used in the calculation.

Reaching another body is a finite-destination <spacecraft trajectory> problem. In the synthetic radial example, a launch at $2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ reaches $r=4\,\mathrm m$ while remaining bound. A spacecraft can similarly encounter the <Moon> on a bound geocentric transfer; actual planning must include the <Moon>'s <gravity>, target motion, arrival <velocity>, and any propulsion needed for capture. Escaping <Earth> alone does not imply escaping the <Sun>, because the relevant <reference frame> and attracting <mass> have changed. The isolated formula describes one gravitational well; a full mission requires more than its scalar threshold.

= Spacecraft tracking and the limits of the escape model
{parent=Escape velocity}

Operational evidence comes from reconstructing a spacecraft's motion. A ground antenna transmits a coded signal; the spacecraft returns it. Corrected round-trip <time> constrains distance, while the <Doppler effect> of a coherent carrier constrains the line-of-sight <relative velocity>. NASA's navigation account describes calibration of onboard turnaround delays before launch, calibration of ground delays before use, and corrections for the <terrestrial atmosphere>. Measurements over multiple stations and epochs constrain the <orbit>; range and <Doppler effect> alone do not directly give all transverse motion. Navigators compare a fitted <spacecraft trajectory> with later tracking and remeasure after propulsion to assess the maneuver. The account identifies <radiation pressure> and imperfect propulsion as disturbances. This provides practical checks of a dynamical model and its corrections, rather than a laboratory measurement of a universal <escape speed>. The simple <inverse-square potential> predicts an unpowered exterior arc; accurate navigation includes additional <forces> and measurement delays.<Basics of Space Flight (2017 edition) by Dave Doody/Chapter 13: Navigation>{ref}

= Spacecraft trajectory
{parent=Escape velocity}

A <spacecraft trajectory> is the path traced by a spacecraft's <position> over <time>. An unpowered exterior arc can approximate a <Kepler orbit>; other bodies' <gravity>, <radiation pressure>, and propulsion can change it.

= Orbit determination
{parent=Spacecraft trajectory}

<Orbit determination> infers a body's <position> and <velocity> from observations and an <equation of motion>. It is an <inverse problem>; a <residual of an inverse problem> compares an observed readout with its modeled prediction.

= Escape and circular speeds inside an isolated uniform sphere
{parent=Escape velocity}
{title2=$v_e^2=V_c^2(3r_0^2/r^2-1)$}

For a sphere of radius $r_0$ and uniform <mass density> $\rho_0$, with no surrounding <mass> and <gravitational potential> zero at infinity, the <spherical shell theorem> gives $V_c^2=4\pi G\rho_0r^2/3$ inside. Matching to the exterior point-mass potential gives $\Phi(r)=-(2\pi G\rho_0/3)(3r_0^2-r^2)$. Since $v_e^2=-2\Phi$, the displayed relation follows for $0<r\leq r_0$. Exterior shells leave the interior <circular speed> unchanged but deepen the potential and change the <escape velocity>; a uniform core alone is insufficient to determine escape.

= Equipotential surface
{parent=Potential energy}

An equipotential surface is a level set of a scalar potential. Its gradient and the corresponding conservative force are normal to every smooth level surface.

= Equipotential curve
{parent=Equipotential surface}
{title2=$\phi(x,y)=C$}

An equipotential curve is a planar level curve of a scalar potential. Where the gradient is nonzero, its tangent is perpendicular to that gradient. In <potential flow>, it is therefore perpendicular to the <velocity field>; this distinguishes equipotential curves from <streamlines>.

= Turning point
{parent=Energy}

A turning point of one-dimensional motion is a position where the kinetic energy and instantaneous velocity vanish before the direction of motion reverses.

= Period of an oscillation
{title2=$T$}
{parent=Classical mechanics}

= Oscillation period
{synonym}

The period is the time required for a periodic motion to complete one full cycle.

= Force
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{wiki}

A force is an interaction that changes momentum; in Newtonian mechanics it satisfies $F=ma$ for constant mass.

= Weight
{disambiguate=physics}
{parent=Force}
{wiki=Weight}
{title2=$\mathbf W=m\mathbf g$}

= Gravitational weight
{synonym}

Here <gravitational weight> means the <force> on a body due to <gravity>. In the Newtonian model, a body of <mass> $m$ in a uniform <gravitational acceleration> $\mathbf g$ has <gravitational weight> $\mathbf W=m\mathbf g$, with magnitude $W=mg$. Its direction follows $\mathbf g$; a <buoyant force> is a separate contribution to the body's net <force>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/5-4-mass-and-weight[OpenStax: mass and gravitational weight]{ref}.

= Apparent weight
{parent=Weight (physics)}
{title2=$N=m(g+a)$}
{wiki}

Apparent weight measures support rather than the gravitational <force> itself. In an ideal scale model, it is the magnitude $N$ of the <normal force> exerted on the body. A scale calibrated to display <mass> divides that support force by its calibration value of $g$.

Take upward as positive in a constant downward <gravitational acceleration> of magnitude $g$. If a body stays in contact with a horizontal scale and accelerates upward at $a$, <Newton's second law> gives $N-mg=ma$, hence $N=m(g+a)$. At rest, $N=mg$. In common <free fall>, $a=-g$, so $N=0$ despite the nonzero <gravitational weight> $mg$. If the formula would require $N<0$, ordinary supporting contact cannot realize it: the body loses contact instead.

= Weightlessness
{parent=Apparent weight}
{wiki}

Weightlessness is the absence of supporting or constraining <forces> that would give a body an <apparent weight>. It does not require zero <gravitational acceleration>. A freely falling cabin and a small freely falling object inside share the same acceleration in a uniform field, so neither needs to support the other.

The <circular orbit as continuous free fall> gives a sustained example. Real spacecraft have small residual relative accelerations from <tidal forces>, atmospheric <drag force> and onboard disturbances; the ideal common-fall explanation does not assert that every point in an extended spacecraft experiences exactly the same <Newtonian gravitational field>. https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/20000083212[Ramachandran, Numerical and Experimental Investigation of the Effects of Acceleration Disturbances on Microgravity Experiments (2000), abstract: vibration, atmospheric drag and gravity-gradient effects]{ref}

= Restoring force
{parent=Force}
{wiki}
{title2=$F(x)=-kx$}

A restoring force acts toward an <equilibrium> when a body is displaced from it. In one dimension, taking <displacement> $x=0$ at <equilibrium>, this means $xF(x)<0$ for sufficiently small nonzero $x$. An ideal linear <spring> has $F(x)=-kx$ with positive <spring constant> $k$, producing <simple harmonic motion> for a constant <mass> without <damping>. More general restoring forces need not be linear.

= Tension
{parent=Force}
{title2=$T$}

Tension is a pulling <force> transmitted along a taut string, rope, or similar body. For an ideal uniform string with <linear mass density> $\mu$, constant tension $T$ sets the small transverse <wave> speed $c=\sqrt{T/\mu}$.

= Van der Waals force
{c}
{parent=Force}
{wiki}

The Van der Waals force is a distance-dependent nonbonded interaction between atoms or molecules, including dispersion and dipolar contributions. In the attractive nonretarded pair model, an interaction energy $U(r)=-\beta/r^6$ gives force magnitude $6\beta/r^7$. Macroscopic integration introduces a <Hamaker constant>; it does not replace the sign change and molecular cutoff needed at very short distances.

= Sphere-plane dispersion force
{parent=Van der Waals force}
{title2=$F\simeq Aa/(6d^2)$}

For a sphere of radius $a$ at separation $d\ll a$ from a half-space, pairwise integration of the attractive $r^{-6}$ molecular potential gives $F\simeq Aa/(6d^2)$. Equivalently, integrate the planar <disjoining pressure> magnitude $A/(6\pi h^3)$ over the local gap $h=d+r^2/(2a)$. Across a third medium the effective <Hamaker constant> can instead correspond to repulsion; state its sign convention explicitly.

= Static friction
{parent=Force}
{title2=$|f_s|\le\mu_sN$}

<Static friction> is the tangential contact <force> that maintains zero <contact slip>. “Static” describes the contact state: both surfaces can move together in the chosen <inertial frame>. In the ideal Coulomb dry-contact model, the allowable magnitude is bounded by
$$
|f_s|\le\mu_sN,
$$
where $N\ge0$ is the magnitude of the <normal force> and $\mu_s\ge0$ is the <coefficient of static friction>. The actual <static friction> is whatever the <equations of motion> require to maintain zero <contact slip>, provided it lies within this bound. Equality specifies limiting <static friction>; a smaller load generally requires a smaller contact <force>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/6-2-friction[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, OpenStax University Physics 1, §6.2: responsive static friction]{ref}

Use the stipulated toy model from <kinetic friction>: <mass> $m=1\,\mathrm{kg}$, <normal force> $N=10\,\mathrm N$, <coefficient of static friction> $\mu_s=0.5$, and <coefficient of kinetic friction> $\mu_k=0.3$. A box initially at rest on stationary horizontal ground is pushed right by $F\ge0$. To remain at rest, <Newton's second law> requires $F+f_s=ma=0$, hence $f_s=-F$. For $F=4\,\mathrm N$, this gives $f_s=-4\,\mathrm N$ and $a=0$, within the $5\,\mathrm N$ bound. Automatically setting $|f_s|=\mu_sN$ would predict the wrong <force> and <acceleration>.

For $F=6\,\mathrm N$, maintaining rest would require $|f_s|=6\,\mathrm N>5\,\mathrm N$, so rest cannot persist. Once the box slides right, <kinetic friction> is $f_k=-3\,\mathrm N$ and the <acceleration> is $a=(6-3)/1=3\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$. At exactly $F=5\,\mathrm N$, the inequality still permits rest with $f_s=-5\,\mathrm N$. An already right-sliding box under the same applied <force> instead has $f_k=-3\,\mathrm N$ and $a=2\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$. Applied <force> alone therefore does not specify the <friction> branch: the contact state and motion history matter.

\Image[static-friction-versus-applied-force.png]
{title=State-dependent friction magnitudes: a resting box can balance up to 5 N, while a box already sliding right has 3 N of friction}

The diagram compares conditional states, rather than specifying a force-ramp trajectory. The static branch includes its $5\,\mathrm N$ endpoint; the sliding branch also exists below that load when rightward <contact slip> is already present. To solve a new problem, first impose zero <contact slip>, find the required <static friction> and <normal force> from the <equations of motion>, and check the inequality. If that contact condition fails, use <kinetic friction> with the actual slip direction.

This distinction makes <static friction> useful for traction. For an ideal driven tire undergoing <rolling without slipping> on stationary ground, the road can exert a forward <static friction> that accelerates the vehicle while the tire's contact point has zero <velocity>. Thus <static friction> can point along the <center of mass> motion; its role is to prevent <contact slip>, rather than always to oppose the motion of the whole body.

= Coefficient of static friction
{parent=Static friction}
{title2=$\mu_s$}

= Granular friction coefficient
{synonym}

The <static friction> force at impending sliding has magnitude $\mu_s$ times the normal contact <force>. In a simple grain model this coefficient represents resistance to downstream motion; the model excludes lift and detailed contact torques.

= Force balance
{parent=Force}
