An axial measurement determines one scalar <moment of inertia>, not the complete <inertia tensor> needed for general rotation.

= Moment of inertia of a uniform solid hemisphere
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{title2=$I_G=83ma^2/320$}

The centroid of a uniform solid hemisphere of radius $a$ lies $3a/8$ from its flat face. About a diameter of the underlying sphere parallel to that face, its <moment of inertia> is $2ma^2/5$. The <parallel axis theorem> therefore gives $I_G=2ma^2/5-m(3a/8)^2=83ma^2/320$ about the parallel centroidal axis. A thin hemispherical shell has a different centroid and inertia.

= Moment of inertia of a uniform solid cylinder
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{title2=$I=ma^2/2$}

For a uniform solid cylinder of radius $a$, length $L$ and <mass density> $\rho$, a coaxial shell carries <mass> $dm=2\pi\rho Lr\,dr$. The axial <moment of inertia> is $I=\int_0^a r^2dm=\pi\rho La^4/2=ma^2/2$. The axial <moment of inertia> is independent of the length once <mass> and radius are fixed. A thin cylindrical shell instead has $I=ma^2$, demonstrating why the uniform solid assumption matters.

= Reflected inertia of a no-slip wheel and roller
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{title2=$K=I+J(R/S)^2$}

If a fixed-axis wheel of radius $R$ drives a fixed-axis roller of radius $S$ without slip, their angular-speed magnitudes satisfy $\sigma=(R/S)\omega$. Their <kinetic energy> is $\tfrac12[I+J(R/S)^2]\omega^2$, so the displayed reflected <moment of inertia> governs the wheel coordinate. A roller resisting torque of magnitude $\gamma\sigma^2$ consumes power $\gamma\sigma^3$ and therefore reflects to wheel torque $\gamma(R/S)^3\omega^2$. The squared ratio belongs to inertia; the cubed ratio belongs to this quadratic resisting torque.

= Moment of inertia of a uniform annulus
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{title2=$I=\frac M2(a^2+b^2)$}

A uniform planar annulus of outer radius $a$, inner radius $b<a$ and mass $M$ has surface density $M/[\pi(a^2-b^2)]$. Integrating $r^2$ over its mass gives the axial <moment of inertia> $I=M(a^2+b^2)/2$. Subtracting the inner disc from a complete disc gives the same result, provided their surface densities match.

// Target: electromagnetism.bigb

= Moment of inertia of a uniform disc
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{title2=$I=Ma^2/2$}

For a uniform disc of radius $a$ and mass $M$, annuli of radius $r$ and thickness $dr$ have mass $(M/\pi a^2)2\pi r\,dr$. Their axial <moment of inertia> sums to $I=\int_0^a r^2\,dm=Ma^2/2$.

= Moment of inertia of a uniform solid sphere
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{title2=$I=\frac25Ma^2$}

A uniform solid sphere of mass $M$ and radius $a$ has the indicated <moment of inertia> about any diameter. Integrating the squared perpendicular distance against uniform <mass density> gives $I=8\pi\rho a^5/15=2Ma^2/5$. It is different from the value for a thin spherical shell and determines the rotational contribution to <kinetic energy> during <rolling without slipping>.

= Mass second-moment tensor
{title2=$I_{jk}$}
{parent=Moment of inertia}

The mass second-moment tensor about a <centre of mass> is $I_{jk}=\int y_jy_k\,dm$. It measures the shape of a mass distribution and appears in <tidal torque theory>. It differs from the mechanical <inertia tensor>, which is $\mathcal I_{jk}=\operatorname{tr}(I)\delta_{jk}-I_{jk}$. For a uniform <ellipsoid> of mass $M$ and semiaxes $a_1,a_2,a_3$, its principal values are $Ma_j^2/5$.

= Inertia tensor
{title2=$I_{ij}$}
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{wiki}

The <inertia tensor> packages the <moments of inertia> about every axis through a specified point $O$ into one <symmetric matrix>. An axis still needs a direction: if $\mathbf n$ is a <unit vector>, its scalar <moment of inertia> is $I_{O,\mathbf n}=\mathbf n^T\mathbf I_O\mathbf n$. The same <mass> layout can therefore be easy to spin about one direction and harder about another. The <inertia tensor> also determines <rotational kinetic energy> and relates <angular velocity> to <angular momentum> in the appropriate <rigid body> model.

Take <position> vectors $\mathbf r=(x,y,z)$ relative to $O$, expressed in an <orthonormal basis>. For <point masses> $m_a$, or a continuous distribution with <mass density> $\rho$ and $dm=\rho\,dV$,
$$
(\mathbf I_O)_{ij}=\sum_a m_a\bigl(|\mathbf r_a|^2\delta_{ij}-r_{ai}r_{aj}\bigr),
\qquad
(\mathbf I_O)_{ij}=\int\bigl(|\mathbf r|^2\delta_{ij}-r_ir_j\bigr)\,dm.
$$
Here $\delta_{ij}$ is the <Kronecker delta>. Equivalently, using the <identity matrix> $\mathbf1$ and an <outer product>,
$$
\mathbf I_O=\int\bigl(|\mathbf r|^2\mathbf1-\mathbf r\mathbf r^T\bigr)\,dm
=\int\begin{pmatrix}
y^2+z^2&-xy&-xz\\
-xy&x^2+z^2&-yz\\
-xz&-yz&x^2+y^2
\end{pmatrix}\,dm.
$$
Every entry has <SI> units $\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$. The diagonal entries are axial <moments of inertia>; the off-diagonal entries carry a minus sign in this convention. Some texts instead define the integrals without the prefixed minus sign $\int xy\,dm$, $\int xz\,dm$, $\int yz\,dm$ as their named off-diagonal quantities and put the minus signs into the displayed <matrix>. Check that convention before combining formulas. <Newtonian Dynamics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Moment of Inertia Tensor>{ref} <16.07 Dynamics/Lecture L26 - 3D Rigid Body Dynamics: The Inertia Tensor>{ref}

The <symmetric matrix> is <positive semidefinite>, because its <quadratic form> is
$$
\mathbf u^T\mathbf I_O\mathbf u
=\int\bigl(|\mathbf u|^2|\mathbf r|^2-(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf r)^2\bigr)\,dm
=\int|\mathbf u\times\mathbf r|^2\,dm\geq0.
$$
The <dot product> and <cross product> identify this as a sum of squared distances when $\mathbf u$ is a <unit vector>. A zero <moment of inertia> occurs precisely when all the <mass> lies on that axis, up to sets of zero <mass>. A genuine three-dimensional body with positive <mass density> in a volume has strictly positive axial <moments of inertia>. Do not confuse this <inertia tensor> with the <mass second-moment tensor> $\mathbf S=\int\mathbf r\mathbf r^T\,dm$: they obey $\mathbf I_O=\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf S)\mathbf1-\mathbf S$, where $\operatorname{tr}$ denotes the <matrix trace>.

If $O$ is fixed in an <inertial frame> and each <velocity> is $\mathbf v=\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r$, summing the individual <kinetic energies> and <angular momenta> gives
$$
\mathbf L_O=\mathbf I_O\boldsymbol\omega,
\qquad
K=\frac12\boldsymbol\omega^T\mathbf I_O\boldsymbol\omega
=\frac12\boldsymbol\omega\cdot\mathbf L_O.
$$
These formulas also describe <angular momentum> and <rotational kinetic energy> relative to the <centre of mass> $G$ of a translating <rigid body>. In that case the total <kinetic energy> in the <inertial frame> is $K_{\mathrm{total}}=\tfrac12M|\mathbf V_G|^2+\tfrac12\boldsymbol\omega^T\mathbf I_G\boldsymbol\omega$. For a general moving point $O$, the body's actual <velocities> include $\mathbf V_O$, so $\mathbf I_O\boldsymbol\omega$ alone need not be its <angular momentum> about $O$. <Newtonian Dynamics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Rotational Kinetic Energy>{ref} <16.07 Dynamics/Lecture L26 - 3D Rigid Body Dynamics: The Inertia Tensor>{ref}

Changing the <orthonormal basis> changes components, rather than the physical distribution: if the columns of an <orthogonal matrix> $Q$ are the new basis vectors expressed in the old basis, then $\mathbf I'_O=Q^T\mathbf I_OQ$. Changing the reference point is a different operation, governed by the <tensor parallel-axis theorem>. Both <principal axes> and <principal moments of inertia> must therefore be quoted with their reference point.

= Inertia tensor of paired point masses
{parent=Inertia tensor}

An exact planar example makes the off-diagonal entries tangible. Join four equal <point masses> $m$ with negligible-mass rigid connectors at $a(2,1,0)$, $a(-2,-1,0)$, $a(1,-1,0)$ and $a(-1,1,0)$, where $a>0$ is a length. Their <centre of mass> is $O=(0,0,0)$. Summing $x^2$, $y^2$ and $xy$ gives $10a^2$, $4a^2$ and $2a^2$, respectively, so
$$
\mathbf I_{\mathrm{planar}}=ma^2\begin{pmatrix}4&-2&0\\-2&10&0\\0&0&14\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The $z$ axis is a <principal axis>, and $I_{zz}=I_{xx}+I_{yy}$ illustrates the <perpendicular axis theorem>. The $x$ axis is not a <principal axis>: <angular velocity> $\boldsymbol\omega=(\Omega,0,0)$ gives <angular momentum> $ma^2\Omega(4,-2,0)$, which points in a different direction. A bearing can enforce fixed-axis rotation despite this difference, supplying the necessary transverse <torque>.

\Image[inertia-tensor-paired-masses-rotational-energy.png]
{title=Paired <point masses> and direction-dependent <rotational kinetic energy>}
{description=Original exact model. Four equal <point masses> lie in the $xy$ plane with negligible-mass connectors. The curves use <angular velocity> $\boldsymbol\omega=\Omega(\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0)$ and plot $2K/(ma^2\Omega^2)$. Adding two more equal <point masses> at $a(0,0,\pm1)$ raises this curve by two. Markers depict ideal <point masses>, not finite-sized weights; no experimental data are plotted.}

For an in-plane <unit vector> $\mathbf n=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0)$, the planar model has
$$
\frac{I_{O,\mathbf n}}{ma^2}=4\cos^2\theta+10\sin^2\theta-4\sin\theta\cos\theta.
$$
Its extrema are the <principal moments of inertia> $(7-\sqrt{13})ma^2$ and $(7+\sqrt{13})ma^2$; the remaining <principal moment of inertia> is $14ma^2$. These values follow from the two-by-two <characteristic polynomial> $(4-\lambda)(10-\lambda)-4$, so they can be checked without trusting a numerical <eigenvalue> routine. At a stipulated <angular speed> $\Omega=1\,\mathrm{rad/s}$, with $m=1\,\mathrm{kg}$ and $a=1\,\mathrm m$, rotation about $x$, $y$ or $z$ has <rotational kinetic energy> $2$, $5$ or $7\,\mathrm J$, respectively.

A truly three-dimensional example adds two more equal <point masses> at $a(0,0,1)$ and $a(0,0,-1)$. Each contributes $ma^2\operatorname{diag}(1,1,0)$ to the <inertia tensor>, giving
$$
\mathbf I_{\mathrm{3D}}=ma^2\begin{pmatrix}6&-2&0\\-2&12&0\\0&0&14\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
(I_1,I_2,I_3)=ma^2(9-\sqrt{13},9+\sqrt{13},14).
$$
The in-plane <principal axes> stay the same while their <principal moments of inertia> each increase by $2ma^2$. The $z$ <moment of inertia> stays unchanged because the added <point masses> lie on that axis. This comparison changes the total <mass> from $4m$ to $6m$; it holds the original four <point masses> fixed rather than redistributing a fixed total <mass>.

= Rotational inertia measurement with a falling mass
{parent=Inertia tensor}

PASCO's <PASPORT Rotary Motion Sensor Experiment Guide>, Experiment 1, specifies a PS-2120A sensor, a rod carrying two equal-radius weights, a three-step drive wheel, a thread and hanging <mass>, a clamp-on guide wheel, a support, a balance and calipers. The thread leaves the drive wheel tangentially. The sensor records <angular velocity> against <time>; a <linear regression> over the accelerating interval supplies <angular acceleration> $\alpha$. The guide measures the drive radius with calipers and repeats the run with the weights removed to subtract the apparatus's own <moment of inertia>. Its tables are blank: it prescribes a measurement and does not report an observed numerical agreement. <PASPORT Rotary Motion Sensor Experiment Guide>{ref}

With hanging <mass> $m_h$ including its hanger, effective drive radius $b$, no thread slip and negligible guide-wheel inertia, <Newton's second law> gives thread <tension> $F=m_h(g-b\alpha)$. The axial <torque> is $bF$. Under negligible <friction>, the inference is therefore
$$
I_{\mathrm{loaded}}=\frac{m_hgb}{\alpha}-m_hb^2.
$$
Repeat the inference for the unloaded assembly and subtract: $I_{\mathrm{weights}}=I_{\mathrm{loaded}}-I_{\mathrm{empty}}$. Omitting the $m_hb^2$ term incorrectly treats thread <tension> as the hanging weight. Comparing with $(M_1+M_2)R^2$ tests the <point mass> approximation; finite-sized weights also have their own <moments of inertia> about their <centres of mass>. <PASPORT Rotary Motion Sensor Experiment Guide>{ref}

Useful proposed checks go beyond that ideal inference. Verify the <rotary encoder>'s angle scale by turning the shaft through a marked full revolution; check the recorded <time> against an independent clock and the balance against a known <mass>. Measure $b$ at the thread's running surface, including thread thickness; avoid overlapping wraps that change $b$. Release without a push, inspect the <angular velocity> record for a straight interval, and stop before the hanger lands or the thread runs out. Repeat with several hanging <masses>, weight radii and rotation directions. A consistent inferred <moment of inertia> across drive <torques> supports the model; curvature, direction dependence or drift indicates a limitation rather than a new <principal moment of inertia>.

For approximately constant resisting <torque> $\tau_f\geq0$ in one rotation direction, an original calibration model is
$$
m_hgb-m_hb^2\alpha=I\alpha+\tau_f.
$$
Fit the left-hand quantity against $\alpha$ across several drives: the slope estimates the axial <moment of inertia> and the intercept estimates $\tau_f$. This calibration is conditional on <friction> remaining constant over the fitted <angular speeds>; a freely slowing run can expose speed dependence. Sensor differentiation noise, guide-wheel <moment of inertia>, thread slip, flexing connectors and uncertain weight radii limit the inference. Removing weights can also change bearing loads, so empty-apparatus subtraction does not automatically cancel <friction>. These are proposed controls and a model extension, not results claimed by PASCO.

A fixed-axis readout measures $\mathbf n^T\mathbf I_O\mathbf n$, not the full <inertia tensor>. In a proposed generalization, repeat about $x$, $y$, $z$ and the three diagonal directions $(\mathbf e_x+\mathbf e_y)/\sqrt2$, $(\mathbf e_x+\mathbf e_z)/\sqrt2$, $(\mathbf e_y+\mathbf e_z)/\sqrt2$, all through the same $O$. For example,
$$
I_{xy}=I_{O,(\mathbf e_x+\mathbf e_y)/\sqrt2}-\frac{I_{xx}+I_{yy}}2.
$$
Six independent scalar readings then determine the six entries of a <symmetric matrix>. Each mounting needs its own apparatus correction, and different reference points require the <tensor parallel-axis theorem> before the readings can be combined.

= Rotary encoder
{parent=Rotational inertia measurement with a falling mass}
{wiki}

A <rotary encoder> converts shaft rotation into a readout of <angle> or changes in <angle>. The PS-2120A uses interruptions of a <light> beam to encode shaft motion; its product specification gives 4000 divisions per revolution. That count is a resolution specification, not a guarantee of the precision of an inferred <angular acceleration> or <moment of inertia>. <PASPORT Rotary Motion Sensor - PS-2120A>{ref}

= Tensor parallel-axis theorem
{parent=Inertia tensor}
{title2=$I(P)=I(G)+M(|X|^2I-XX^T)$}

If $G$ is the <center of mass> and $X=P-G$, expanding the defining <inertia tensor> about $P$ gives the displayed formula. The cross terms vanish because $\int r\,dm=0$ in coordinates centred at $G$. Contracting with a unit axis $u$ gives the <parallel axis theorem>, with the squared distance between the parallel axes equal to $|X|^2-(u\cdot X)^2$.

= Principal moment of inertia
{title2=$I_i$}
{parent=Inertia tensor}

= Principal moments of inertia
{synonym}

The <principal moments of inertia> about a specified point $O$ are the <eigenvalues> $I_1,I_2,I_3$ of the <inertia tensor> $\mathbf I_O$. Their associated <unit vectors> that are <eigenvectors> specify <principal axes> through $O$. The <spectral theorem for real symmetric matrices> guarantees an <orthonormal basis> of these <eigenvectors>, even for an irregular <rigid body>. Choose their signs to make a right-handed basis and collect them as the columns of an <orthogonal matrix> $Q$:
$$
\mathbf I_O\mathbf e_i=I_i\mathbf e_i,
\qquad Q^T\mathbf I_OQ=\operatorname{diag}(I_1,I_2,I_3).
$$
Thus <diagonalization> removes the off-diagonal coupling in the <rotational kinetic energy>. The <eigenvalues> belong to the <mass> distribution and reference point, independently of which <orthonormal basis> initially describes them. <Newtonian Dynamics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Principal Axes of Rotation>{ref}

In this <principal body frame>, write <angular velocity> as $\boldsymbol\omega=\sum_i\omega_i\mathbf e_i$. Then
$$
\mathbf L_O=\sum_i I_i\omega_i\mathbf e_i,
\qquad
K=\frac12\sum_i I_i\omega_i^2.
$$
Use these as total <angular momentum> and <kinetic energy> only under the reference-point conditions stated under <inertia tensor>. Along a single <principal axis> with $I_i>0$, <angular momentum> is parallel to <angular velocity>. For a mixture of directions with different <principal moments of inertia>, it generally is not. A <principal axis> need not be a geometric symmetry axis: it is selected by the complete <mass> distribution. A <principal axis> also need not give stable free rotation; for three distinct <principal moments of inertia>, the <intermediate axis theorem> supplies the stability distinction.

For any <unit vector> $\mathbf n=\sum_i n_i\mathbf e_i$,
$$
I_{O,\mathbf n}=\sum_i I_i n_i^2,
\qquad \sum_i n_i^2=1,
\qquad \min_i I_i\leq I_{O,\mathbf n}\leq\max_i I_i.
$$
The axial <moment of inertia> is a weighted average of the <principal moments of inertia>. At fixed <angular speed>, the least and greatest <rotational kinetic energies> occur in the corresponding <eigenspaces>. At fixed nonzero <angular momentum> magnitude $L$, instead, $K=\tfrac12\sum_i L_i^2/I_i$, so the largest <principal moment of inertia> gives the smallest <rotational kinetic energy>. These comparisons hold the reference point fixed.

There is an additional physical restriction beyond <positive semidefiniteness>. In <principal axes> coordinates,
$$
I_1+I_2-I_3=2\int z^2\,dm\geq0,
$$
with two analogous cyclic inequalities. Therefore three arbitrary positive numbers do not always represent a possible <inertia tensor>. For a distribution in the $xy$ plane through $O$, $I_3=I_1+I_2$, as in the planar <inertia tensor of paired point masses>. A uniform solid <ellipsoid> of <mass> $M$ and semiaxes $a,b,c$ supplies a continuous three-dimensional example: using its <mass second-moment tensor>, its central <principal moments of inertia> are $M(b^2+c^2)/5$, $M(a^2+c^2)/5$, $M(a^2+b^2)/5$. Setting $a=b=c=R$ recovers the <moment of inertia of a uniform solid sphere>.

= Rotational energy ellipsoid
{parent=Principal moment of inertia}
{title2=$\boldsymbol\omega^T\mathbf I_O\boldsymbol\omega=2K_0$}

For strictly positive <principal moments of inertia> and a fixed <rotational kinetic energy> $K_0>0$, the allowed <angular velocity> components form an <ellipsoid>:
$$
I_1\omega_1^2+I_2\omega_2^2+I_3\omega_3^2=2K_0,
\qquad
s_i=\sqrt{\frac{2K_0}{I_i}}.
$$
Its semiaxis lengths $s_i$ have units of <angular speed>. The longest semiaxis corresponds to the smallest <principal moment of inertia>: that direction permits the greatest <angular speed> for the same <rotational kinetic energy>. This is a surface in <angular velocity> space, not the physical outline of the body. If one <principal moment of inertia> vanishes in an ideal line-mass model, this constant-energy surface is unbounded in that direction and is no longer an <ellipsoid>.

\Image[inertia-tensor-principal-axis-energy-ellipsoid.png]
{title=<Principal axes> and the constant-<rotational kinetic energy> <ellipsoid>}
{description=Original exact model using the six <point masses> in <inertia tensor of paired point masses>. With $K_0=ma^2\Omega_0^2$, the displayed surface in $\boldsymbol\omega/\Omega_0$ has semiaxes $\sqrt{2/(9-\sqrt{13})}$, $\sqrt{2/(9+\sqrt{13})}$ and $\sqrt{2/14}$. The left view uses the original coordinate directions and the right view the <principal body frame>. Colors identify the same <principal axes> in both views; the surface describes possible <angular velocities>, not a material shell or a measured trajectory.}

The <rotational energy ellipsoid> gives the geometry behind <Poinsot's ellipsoid>, but a constant-energy surface alone does not determine a free-rotation trajectory. A torque-free trajectory must also satisfy the conserved <angular momentum> constraint and the <Euler equations for a torque-free rigid body>.

= Degeneracy of principal moments of inertia
{parent=Principal moment of inertia}

If two <principal moments of inertia> coincide, for example $I_1=I_2\ne I_3$, every direction in their two-dimensional <eigenspace> is a <principal axis>. The perpendicular axis is determined, but there is no privileged orthogonal pair within that plane. Rotating the pair in the plane leaves the diagonal <inertia tensor> unchanged. If all three <principal moments of inertia> coincide, $\mathbf I_O=I_0\mathbf1$ and every axis through $O$ is principal. This follows from the <eigenvalue multiplicity> and <eigenspace>, rather than from a failure of <diagonalization>. <Newtonian Dynamics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Matrix Eigenvalue Theory>{ref}

Equal <principal moments of inertia> do not prove that a body's outline has continuous rotational symmetry. For example, six equal <point masses> at $(\pm a,0,0)$, $(0,\pm a,0)$ and $(0,0,\pm a)$ have $\mathbf I_O=4ma^2\mathbf1$. This discrete layout has the same axial <moment of inertia> in every direction, although its actual <mass> distribution changes under most rotations. Conversely, even when all three <principal moments of inertia> are distinct, <principal axes> are unoriented lines: reversing an <eigenvector>'s sign gives the same axis. Near repeated <eigenvalues>, small measurement errors can rotate fitted <principal axes> substantially within the nearly degenerate <eigenspace>, so quoted directions need an uncertainty assessment as well as quoted <principal moments of inertia>.

= Poinsot's ellipsoid
{c}
{parent=Inertia tensor}
{wiki}

= Poinsot construction
{c}
{synonym}

For torque-free rigid-body motion, the kinetic-energy ellipsoid fixed in the body rolls without slipping on the invariable plane perpendicular to the conserved angular momentum. Its point of contact is the angular-velocity vector.

= Parallel axis theorem
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{wiki}

The <parallel axis theorem> transfers a <moment of inertia> from an axis through the <centre of mass> $G$ to a parallel axis through $O$:
$$
I_O=I_G+Md^2.
$$
Here $M$ is the total <mass> and $d$ is the shortest perpendicular distance between the two axes. Both <moments of inertia> concern the same <mass> distribution and the same axis direction. The axis for $I_G$ must pass through the <centre of mass>; $I_O$ may refer to an axis outside the body. No uniform-density or planar-shape assumption is needed. Within any family of parallel axes, the <centre of mass> axis minimizes the <moment of inertia>.<University Physics Volume 1 by William Moebs and others/10.5 Calculating Moments of Inertia>[§10.5]{ref}

A short proof isolates why the <centre of mass> matters. Choose parallel axes perpendicular to an $xy$ plane, put $G$ at the origin, and place $O$ at $(d,0)$. Then
$$
I_O=\int\bigl[(x-d)^2+y^2\bigr]\,dm
=I_G-2d\int x\,dm+d^2\int dm
=I_G+Md^2,
$$
because $\int x\,dm=0$ in <centre of mass> coordinates. Individual <mass> elements can move closer to the new axis, but their weighted cross terms cancel. For arbitrary parallel axes $A$ and $B$, use $I_B-I_A=M(d_B^2-d_A^2)$, where each distance is measured from the parallel <centre of mass> axis; adding $M$ times the squared separation of $A$ and $B$ is generally incorrect. A displacement along the axis has $d=0$. Changing the axis direction instead requires the <inertia tensor> or, in its applicable planar setting, the <perpendicular axis theorem>.

For a uniform disc, <mass> $M$ and radius $R$, the central perpendicular <moment of inertia> is $I_G=MR^2/2$. Shifting that axis to the rim gives $d=R$ and $I_O=3MR^2/2$, three times $I_G$. In the computed $M=2\,\mathrm{kg}$, $R=0.30\,\mathrm m$ example, $I_G=0.090\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$, $Md^2=0.180\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$ and $I_O=0.270\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$. At <angular speed> $10\,\mathrm{rad/s}$ the rim-pivoted disc has <rotational kinetic energy> $13.5\,\mathrm J$. Its <centre of mass> now moves, so $\tfrac12I_O\omega^2$ already includes that translation; adding a separate <centre of mass> translation term to it would count that contribution twice.

\Image[rotational-inertia-energy-parallel-axis-shift.png]
{title=<Parallel axis theorem>: a uniform disc's central and rim axes}
{description=Original geometric and numerical model, not measured data. Both axes are perpendicular to the disc; the separation is $d=R$. The bar chart separates $I_G=MR^2/2$ from the additional $Md^2=MR^2$ in $I_O=3MR^2/2$, normalized by $MR^2$. All parts of the disc remain in the calculation, including those nearer to the shifted axis.}

The <physical pendulum> measurement described under <moment of inertia> provides a practical check: infer $I_O=Mgh(P/2\pi)^2$ at several pivot positions, then subtract $Mh^2$ to obtain $I_G$. Keep all pivot axes parallel and measure $h$ to the <centre of mass> of the entire moving assembly. The same assembly should yield the same $I_G$ within measurement precision. If a smartphone is attached, its <mass> and its own <moment of inertia> belong in both the geometric prediction and the <centre of mass> calculation. The published bar-and-phone study explicitly adds both contributions using the <parallel axis theorem>.<Experimental analysis of a physical pendulum with variable suspension point by Martín Monteiro and others>[equations 2-4]{ref}

For a careful replication, release small swings without a push, repeat timings at several small <amplitudes>, and check that the extrapolated <oscillation period> is stable as <amplitude> decreases. Time many complete <oscillations> and repeat the run; two crossings of a photogate per cycle must not be mistaken for two cycles. Inspect decay of <amplitude> and compare early and late timings to detect significant pivot <friction> or air <drag>. Mark the bar and phone mount to detect relative slip, tighten the attachment, and check that the pivot support does not rock or migrate between holes. Repeat with an independent photogate or video readout to check the phone's timing. These are proposed controls for the replication, not additional results reported by the authors. The ideal model excludes appreciable <damping>, large swings and flexing; subtraction of $Mh^2$ also magnifies relative errors when $I_G$ is much smaller than $I_O$.

The <oscillation period> need not increase monotonically with pivot distance: the shift raises the <moment of inertia> but also increases the gravitational restoring <torque>. For a computed slender uniform bar model of length $L$, $I_G=ML^2/12$ and
$$
\frac{P^2}{4\pi^2}=\frac1g\left(h+\frac{L^2}{12h}\right),
\qquad h>0.
$$
This expression has its minimum at $h=L/\sqrt{12}$. With $L=1\,\mathrm m$, $h=0.50\,\mathrm m$ and stipulated $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, the prediction is $P\simeq1.638\,\mathrm s$; it is not a recorded timing or a prediction for the bar with a phone attached. The competing terms explain why a timing experiment can reveal a <mass> distribution rather than merely its weight.

= Perpendicular axis theorem
{parent=Moment of inertia}
{wiki}

For a planar lamina in the $xy$-plane, the moments about three mutually perpendicular axes through one point satisfy
$$
I_z=I_x+I_y.
$$
This follows pointwise from $x^2+y^2=y^2+x^2$ in the defining mass integrals.

= Angular rocket equation
{parent=Moment of inertia}

For a uniformly filled, negligibly light disc of fixed radius $R$, remaining mass $m(t)$, angular velocity $\omega(t)$, and tangential exhaust speed $u$ relative to its rim, angular-momentum balance gives
$$
m\dot\omega=\dot m\left(\omega+\frac{2u}{R}\right).
$$
At zero relative exhaust speed, $\omega/m$ is constant.

= Moment of inertia of a solid cone about its vertex
{parent=Moment of inertia}

For a uniform solid cone of height $l$, base radius $R$, and mass $M$, whose symmetry axis points from vertex to base, the centre of mass lies at $3l/4$ from the vertex. The principal moments at the vertex are
$$I_1=I_2=\frac{3M}{20}(R^2+4l^2),
\qquad I_3=\frac{3MR^2}{10}.$$

= Moment of inertia of a pointed cubic-profile solid of revolution
{parent=Moment of inertia}

Consider the uniform <solid of revolution> about the $x_3$-axis whose boundary is
$$
x_1^2+x_2^2=x_3^2-\frac{x_3^3}{h},
\qquad 0\leq x_3\leq h.
$$
For total mass $M$, the center of mass lies a distance $3h/5$ from the pointed origin, and the principal moments about that origin are
$$
I_1=I_2=\frac37Mh^2,
\qquad
I_3=\frac2{35}Mh^2.
$$

= Lagrange, Euler, and Kovalevskaya tops
{c}
{parent=Rigid body dynamics}
{wiki}

The <Lagrange, Euler, and Kovalevskaya tops> are three integrable cases of fixed-point <rigid body dynamics>. The Euler top has no external torque; the Lagrange top has axial symmetry and its center of mass on the symmetry axis; the Kovalevskaya top has two equal principal moments twice the third and its center of mass in the corresponding principal plane.

= Euler angles
{parent=Rigid body dynamics}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$(\phi,\theta,\psi)$}

<Euler angles> describe the orientation of a <rigid body> through three successive angular rotations. Their convention specifies the axes and order of the rotations. For a symmetric top, a common convention takes $\theta$ as the inclination, $\phi$ as precession and $\psi$ as spin about the body axis.

= Euler angles for a symmetric top
{parent=Euler angles}
{c}

With the convention in which $\theta$ is inclination, $\phi$ is precession, and $\psi$ is body-axis spin, an axisymmetric body's kinetic energy is
$$T=\frac I2(\dot\theta^2+\sin^2\theta\dot\phi^2)
+\frac J2(\dot\psi+\cos\theta\dot\phi)^2.$$

= Heavy symmetric top Lagrangian
{parent=Euler angles for a symmetric top}

For a fixed point a distance $a$ from the centre of mass and inclination measured from the upward vertical,
$$L=T-Mga\cos\theta.$$
The spin and precession angles are cyclic.

This is the Lagrangian of a <Lagrange top>, one of the <Lagrange, Euler, and Kovalevskaya tops>.

= Lagrange top
{c}
{parent=Heavy symmetric top Lagrangian}

A Lagrange top is an axisymmetric rigid body with one point fixed on its symmetry axis, moving in a uniform gravitational field with its center of mass on that axis.

= Steady precession of a Lagrange top
{c}
{parent=Lagrange top}

For a Lagrange top whose inclination $\theta$ is measured from the upward vertical, steady precession at angular speed $\Omega$ obeys
$$
I_1\Omega^2\cos\theta-p_\psi\Omega+Mgl=0.
$$
Thus steady precession at a prescribed $0<\theta<\pi/2$ exists precisely when
$$
p_\psi^2\geq4MglI_1\cos\theta.
$$

= Heavy symmetric top Hamiltonian
{parent=Heavy symmetric top Lagrangian}

With transverse and axial moments $I,J$, the Hamiltonian is
$$H=\frac{p_\theta^2}{2I}
+\frac{(p_\phi-p_\psi\cos\theta)^2}{2I\sin^2\theta}
+\frac{p_\psi^2}{2J}+Mga\cos\theta.$$

= Heavy symmetric top reduction
{parent=Heavy symmetric top Hamiltonian}

Fixing the cyclic momenta $p_\psi=\lambda$ and $p_\phi=\mu$ leaves the canonical pair $(\theta,p_\theta)$ and a one-degree-of-freedom Hamiltonian with effective potential
$$V_{\lambda,\mu}(\theta)
=\frac{(\mu-\lambda\cos\theta)^2}{2I\sin^2\theta}
+\frac{\lambda^2}{2J}+Mga\cos\theta.$$

= Gyroscopic stabilization of an inverted symmetric top
{parent=Heavy symmetric top reduction}

At the upright spinning state, $p_\phi=p_\psi=\lambda$. The reduced potential is locally minimized at zero inclination when
$$\lambda^2>4IMga,$$
so sufficiently large axial angular momentum stabilizes the gravitationally inverted top.

= Rolling without slipping
{parent=Rigid body dynamics}
{wiki}

Rolling without slipping imposes that the contact point is instantaneously at rest, giving v=R omega.

= Rolling-time factor on a straight incline
{parent=Rolling without slipping}
{title2=$t_{\rm roll}/t_{\rm slide}=\sqrt{1+I/(MR^2)}$}

For a rigid body rolling from rest down a straight incline with fixed rolling radius $R$ and axial <moment of inertia> $I$, <translation> and <rotation> give $(M+I/R^2)\ddot s=Mg\sin\theta$. A smooth slide has $\ddot s=g\sin\theta$. Travel over the same distance from rest therefore gives the displayed time ratio. <Static friction> must suffice for <rolling without slipping>; its contact point is at rest, so this ideal friction does no work. A uniform solid cylinder has $I=MR^2/2$ and ratio $\sqrt{3/2}$.

= Rolling uniform solid hemisphere
{parent=Rolling without slipping}
{title2=$\dot\theta^2=15g\cos\theta/[a(28-15\cos\theta)]$}

Let the flat face make angle $\theta$ with horizontal while the rounded face contacts a horizontal plane. The centroid has height $a-(3a/8)\cos\theta$ and contact distance squared $b^2=a^2+(3a/8)^2-2a(3a/8)\cos\theta$. For <rolling without slipping>, its speed is $b|\dot\theta|$. Combining translation and rotation gives kinetic energy $\tfrac12(I_G+mb^2)\dot\theta^2$. Conservation from rest at $\theta=\pi/2$ gives the displayed result until the flat-face/contact geometry changes.

= Slipping solid cylinder on an incline
{parent=Rolling without slipping}
{title2=$\tan\alpha>3\mu$}

For a uniform solid cylinder, take $s$ positive uphill and $\omega$ positive for uphill <rolling without slipping>. The velocity of the contact point is $w=\dot s-a\omega$. Uphill <kinetic friction> gives $\ddot s=-g(\sin\alpha-\mu\cos\alpha)$ and $a\dot\omega=-2\mu g\cos\alpha$. Starting from $\dot s(0)=a\omega(0)$, one obtains $w=-gt(\sin\alpha-3\mu\cos\alpha)$. Thus $\tan\alpha>3\mu$ makes the assumed uphill <kinetic friction> self-consistent for the entire ascent and descent. Without slip the required uphill <static friction> is $mg\sin\alpha/3$; it is available exactly when $\tan\alpha\leq3\mu$ under the same limiting coefficient.

= Rolling inside a fixed circular cylinder
{parent=Rolling without slipping}
{title2=$\Omega=-\frac{R_1-R_0}{R_0}\dot\theta$}

A round body of radius $R_0$ in a fixed cylinder of radius $R_1$ has <center of mass> path radius $a=R_1-R_0$. With orbital angle $\theta$ and spin <angular velocity> $\Omega$ both positive about the same axis, <rolling without slipping> gives $a\dot\theta+R_0\Omega=0$. For <moment of inertia> $I=\gamma mR_0^2$, its <kinetic energy> is $\frac12ma^2(1+\gamma)\dot\theta^2$. Release from rest at angle $\alpha$ gives, by <conservation of energy>, a descent time $\sqrt{1+\gamma}$ times that of frictionless sliding along the same <center of mass> path. The spin sign reverses if its positive direction is chosen opposite to the orbital angle.

= Lagrangian mechanics
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{c}
{wiki}

Lagrangian mechanics derives motion from the stationary action of $L=T-V$ through the Euler--Lagrange equations.

= Cartan form of a regular mechanical Lagrangian
{c}
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}
{title2=$\theta_L=L_{v^i}\,dq^i,\quad\omega_L=-d\theta_L$}

A <regular Lagrangian> $L:TQ\to\mathbb R$ defines a fibre derivative $\mathbb FL:TQ\to T^*Q$ and the Cartan one-form $\theta_L=(\mathbb FL)^*\theta$. Its two-form $\omega_L=-d\theta_L$ is nondegenerate when the velocity Hessian is invertible. The energy is $E_L=v^iL_{v^i}-L$. The equation $\iota_{\Gamma_L}\omega_L=dE_L$ determines the Euler–Lagrange vector field. A <hyperregular Lagrangian> makes the fibre derivative a global diffeomorphism and transports this dynamics to Hamiltonian phase space.

= Helical symmetry of a potential
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

A potential $V(r,\phi,z)=f(r,\phi-kz)$ is invariant under the screw transformation $(\phi,z)\mapsto(\phi+ks,z+s)$. The <Noether conserved quantity for a mechanical point symmetry> is
$$
p_z+kp_\phi,
$$
a linear combination of axial <linear momentum> and axial <angular momentum>.

= Cyclic coordinate
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}
{wiki}

A generalized coordinate absent from the <Lagrangian> is cyclic. Its conjugate <generalized momentum> is conserved by the <Euler-Lagrange equations>.

= Action
{title2=$S$}
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}
{wiki=Action_(physics)}

= Action functional
{synonym}

The action of a path is the time integral of its <Lagrangian>. The <principle of stationary action> requires its first variation to vanish for every admissible fixed-endpoint variation.

= Principle of stationary action
{parent=Action}
{wiki}

The principle of stationary action says that the physical path between fixed endpoints is a <stationary point> of the <action>.

= Total-time-derivative invariance of a Lagrangian
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

Replacing a Lagrangian by
$$
L'(q,\dot q,t)=L(q,\dot q,t)+\frac d{dt}F(q,t)
$$
adds only the endpoint term $F(q(t_2),t_2)-F(q(t_1),t_1)$ to the action. Fixed-endpoint variations therefore give the same Euler--Lagrange equations.

= Charged-particle electromagnetic Lagrangian
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

For electromagnetic scalar and vector potentials $\phi$ and $A$,
$$
L=\frac12m\dot r^2-q\phi+q\dot r\cdot A.
$$
With
$$
E=-\nabla\phi-\partial_tA,
\qquad
B=\nabla\times A,
$$
the Euler--Lagrange equations give the Lorentz force $m\ddot r=q(E+\dot r\times B)$, while the canonical momentum is $p=m\dot r+qA$.

= Hamiltonian of a charged particle in an electromagnetic field
{parent=Charged-particle electromagnetic Lagrangian}

The <Legendre transform> of the <charged-particle electromagnetic Lagrangian> is
$$
H(\mathbf x,\mathbf p,t)=\frac{[\mathbf p-q\mathbf A(\mathbf x,t)]^2}{2m}+q\phi(\mathbf x,t).
$$

= Gauge variation of the charged-particle Lagrangian
{parent=Charged-particle electromagnetic Lagrangian}

Under $\phi\mapsto\phi-\partial_tf$ and $A\mapsto A+\nabla f$, the charged-particle Lagrangian changes by
$$
L' - L=q\bigl(\partial_tf+\dot r\cdot\nabla f\bigr)
=q\frac{df(r(t),t)}{dt}.
$$
It therefore has unchanged equations of motion by total-time-derivative invariance.

= Canonical momentum parallel to a uniform magnetic field
{parent=Charged-particle electromagnetic Lagrangian}

For a constant uniform magnetic field $B$, choose the symmetric gauge $A=\tfrac12B\times r$. The coordinate parallel to $B$ is cyclic, so its canonical momentum is conserved. Since $A\cdot B=0$, this component is also the mechanical momentum $m\dot r\cdot\widehat B$.

= Generalized momentum
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

The generalized, or conjugate, momentum associated with a coordinate $q_i$ is
$$
p_i=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot q_i}.
$$

In <Lagrangian mechanics>, the coordinate variable describes the configuration; its conjugate momentum need not equal the Cartesian mechanical momentum.

= Ignorable coordinate
{parent=Generalized momentum}

A coordinate $q_i$ is ignorable, or cyclic, when $\partial L/\partial q_i=0$. The Euler--Lagrange equation then gives $\dot p_i=0$, so its generalized momentum is conserved.

= Conserved quantities from cyclic coordinates
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

If a Lagrangian is independent of a coordinate, the conjugate momentum to that coordinate is conserved by the Euler--Lagrange equations.

= Axially symmetric isotropic-mass Lagrangian
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

If $L=|\dot q|^2/2-V(q)$ and $V$ depends only on $|q|^2$ and $(q\cdot n)^2$, then rotations about the axis $n$ leave $L$ invariant. The quantity supplied by <Noether theorem> is the axial angular momentum
$$
J_n=n\cdot(q\times\dot q).
$$
If $L$ also has no explicit time dependence, the energy $|\dot q|^2/2+V(q)$ is conserved independently.

= Double pendulum
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}
{wiki}

A double pendulum consists of two pendula coupled because the second pivot moves with the first mass.

= Double pendulum Lagrangian
{parent=Double pendulum}

The kinetic energy contains the coupling $ml_1l_2\cos(\theta_1-\theta_2)\dot\theta_1\dot\theta_2$, while gravity supplies one cosine potential for each mass height.

= Double pendulum equations of motion
{parent=Double pendulum}

The nonlinear Euler--Lagrange equations couple both angular accelerations and contain centrifugal terms quadratic in angular velocity.

= Kinetic energy from Cartesian position vectors
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

For point masses with generalized coordinates, kinetic energy follows by differentiating their Cartesian position vectors and summing $m_i|\dot r_i|^2/2$.

= Small oscillation
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

Near a stable equilibrium, retaining quadratic Lagrangian terms gives a linear system $\mathsf M\ddot z+\mathsf Kz=0$.

The resulting linearized motion decomposes into <normal modes> near the stable equilibrium.

= Stable equilibrium in Lagrangian mechanics
{parent=Small oscillation}

At an equilibrium, a positive-definite <Hessian matrix> of the potential gives stable <small oscillations>. If a continuous symmetry supplies zero modes, positive definiteness on the directions transverse to the symmetry gives stability of the relative configuration.

= Angular frequency
{parent=Small oscillation}
{wiki}

Angular frequency measures phase advance per unit time. For $x(t)=A\cos(\omega t+\varphi)$ it is $\omega=2\pi/T$, where $T$ is the period.

= Two vertically suspended springs
{parent=Small oscillation}

For two masses hung in series from springs of natural length $\ell$, the lower equilibrium spring tension is $m_2g$ and the upper tension is $(m_1+m_2)g$. Hence their equilibrium lengths are
$$
L_2=\ell+\frac{m_2g}{k_2},
\qquad
L_1=\ell+\frac{(m_1+m_2)g}{k_1}.
$$

= Vertical normal modes of two equal suspended masses
{parent=Two vertically suspended springs}

For equal masses $m$ and spring constants $k$, vertical displacements from equilibrium have stiffness and mass matrices
$$
K=k\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix},
\qquad M=mI.
$$
The two squared frequencies and displacement ratios are
$$
\omega_\pm^2=\frac{k}{2m}(3\pm\sqrt5),
\qquad
\frac{\eta_2}{\eta_1}=\frac{1\mp\sqrt5}{2}.
$$
Gravity shifts the equilibrium but does not enter these vertical frequencies.

= Small-oscillation mass and stiffness matrices
{parent=Small oscillation}

The Hessians of kinetic and potential energy at equilibrium give the symmetric mass matrix $\mathsf M$ and stiffness matrix $\mathsf K$.

= Generalized eigenvalue problem for small oscillations
{parent=Small oscillation}

Normal frequencies satisfy $\det(\mathsf K-\Omega^2\mathsf M)=0$, and each null vector gives the fixed amplitude ratio of a mode.

= Mass-matrix orthogonality of normal modes
{parent=Generalized eigenvalue problem for small oscillations}

For symmetric stiffness matrix $K$ and positive-definite mass matrix $M$, eigenvectors $a_i,a_j$ of
$$
Ka=\omega^2Ma
$$
with distinct squared frequencies satisfy
$$
a_i^TMa_j=0.
$$
Thus normal modes are orthogonal in the kinetic-energy inner product.

= Normal modes of a double pendulum
{parent=Generalized eigenvalue problem for small oscillations}

For equal lengths and mass ratio $\mu=m/M$, the squared frequencies are $\omega_0^2(1+\mu\pm\sqrt{\mu(1+\mu)})$.

= Square-root splitting of nearly degenerate frequencies
{parent=Generalized eigenvalue problem for small oscillations}

A weakly coupled degenerate pair can split by order $\sqrt\mu$ when the small parameter enters a singular mass or stiffness matrix.

= Degenerate normal modes from permutation symmetry
{parent=Generalized eigenvalue problem for small oscillations}

If the mass and stiffness matrices commute with a <permutation representation>, each symmetry subspace is invariant under the <generalized eigenvalue problem for small oscillations>. On an irreducible subspace, <Schur lemma> forces a commuting operator to be scalar, producing a degenerate <normal mode> multiplet.

= Normal modes of three equal masses with a symmetric gap potential
{parent=Degenerate normal modes from permutation symmetry}

Let three equal masses move on a circle of radius $r$, and let the potential be $U(\alpha)+U(\beta)+U(\gamma)$ with $\alpha+\beta+\gamma=2\pi$. At equal spacing $s_0=2\pi/3$, angular displacements have a zero-frequency rigid-rotation mode $(1,1,1)$ and a two-dimensional sum-zero eigenspace with
$$
\omega^2=\frac{3U''(s_0)}{mr^2}.
$$
The two vibrational modes are degenerate by the permutation symmetry.

= Normal modes of three symmetrically coupled trapped masses
{parent=Degenerate normal modes from permutation symmetry}

For three unit masses with unit springs between every pair and one unit spring anchoring each mass to the origin,
$$
M=I,\qquad K=4I-J,
$$
where $J$ is the all-ones matrix. The collective mode $(1,1,1)$ has angular frequency $1$, while the two-dimensional sum-zero subspace has angular frequency $2$.

= Normal modes of two equal masses between three springs
{parent=Generalized eigenvalue problem for small oscillations}

For equal masses $m$, wall-spring constants $k_1,k_2$, and coupling-spring constant $k_3$, the <small-oscillation mass and stiffness matrices> are
$$
\mathsf M=mI,
\qquad
\mathsf K=
\begin{pmatrix}
k_1+k_3&-k_3\\
-k_3&k_2+k_3
\end{pmatrix}.
$$
If $k_1=k(1+\varepsilon\delta)$, $k_2=k(1-\varepsilon\delta)$, and $k_3=k\varepsilon$, the squared normal frequencies are
$$
\omega_\pm^2=\frac{k}{m}
\left[1+\varepsilon\left(1\pm\sqrt{1+\delta^2}\right)\right].
$$

= Conservation of energy from time-translation invariance
{parent=Lagrangian mechanics}

A Lagrangian with no explicit time dependence conserves $E=\sum_i\dot q_i\,\partial L/\partial\dot q_i-L$.

= Center of mass
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{wiki}

= Centres of mass
{synonym}

= Centre of mass
{synonym}

For masses $m_i$ at positions $\mathbf r_i$, the center of mass is $\mathbf R=(\sum_i m_i\mathbf r_i)/(\sum_i m_i)$, with the analogous mass-density integral for a continuous body.

= Barycenter
{disambiguate=astronomy}
{parent=Center of mass}
{wiki}

= Astronomical barycenter
{synonym}

The <centre of mass> of an astronomical system. In an isolated <two-body problem>, both bodies follow <orbits> around their shared astronomical barycenter; the <Kepler orbit> of their relative displacement has a <semi-major axis> equal to the sum of their individual <semi-major axes>.

= Centre of mass after removing material
{parent=Center of mass}
{title2=$\mathbf R=(M_0\mathbf R_0-M_c\mathbf R_c)/(M_0-M_c)$}

Suppose a body of <mass> $M_0$ has <centre of mass> $\mathbf R_0$, and material of <mass> $M_c<M_0$ with <centre of mass> $\mathbf R_c$ is removed. Additivity of the first <mass> moment gives the remaining <centre of mass>
$$
\mathbf R=\frac{M_0\mathbf R_0-M_c\mathbf R_c}{M_0-M_c}.
$$
When the original <centre of mass> is at the origin, the remaining <centre of mass> points opposite the removed material's first <mass> moment. For a constrained <rigid body> rotating about a fixed axis, this displaced <centre of mass> undergoes <centripetal acceleration> even if the applied axial <torque> is zero.

= Center-of-mass motion
{parent=Center of mass}

The center of mass moves as if the total external force acted on the total mass; internal forces cancel in its equation.

= Center-of-mass reduction
{parent=Center-of-mass motion}

Center-of-mass reduction separates translational motion from internal coordinates by using the conserved or externally forced center coordinate.

= Kinetic-energy decomposition about the centre of mass
{parent=Center-of-mass reduction}
{title2=$T=\tfrac12M|\dot{\mathbf R}|^2+\tfrac12\sum_i m_i|\dot{\mathbf y}_i|^2$}

For fixed particle <masses> and positive total <mass>, write $\mathbf x_i=\mathbf R+\mathbf y_i$ with $\mathbf R$ the <centre of mass>. The weighted relative <velocities> sum to zero, so expanding the <kinetic energy> eliminates the mixed term and yields the displayed decomposition. For a <rigid body>, the relative contribution is the <rotational kinetic energy> $I\omega^2/2$ about the chosen centre-of-mass axis.

= Jacobi coordinates
{c}
{parent=Center-of-mass reduction}
{wiki}

For particles with masses $m_j$ and positions $r_j$, <Jacobi coordinates> consist of the total <center of mass> and successive relative coordinates $\rho_j=r_{j+1}-(\sum_{l=1}^j m_l r_l)/(\sum_{l=1}^j m_l)$. They separate translation from internal motion and diagonalize the <kinetic energy> with the corresponding reduced masses.

= Jacobi coordinates for three particles
{c}
{parent=Jacobi coordinates}

For three particles, choose the relative position of particles one and two, the position of their center of mass relative to particle three, and the total center of mass. The kinetic energy diagonalizes with masses
$$
\frac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2},
\qquad
\frac{(m_1+m_2)m_3}{m_1+m_2+m_3},
\qquad
m_1+m_2+m_3.
$$

= Angular momentum decomposition in three-body Jacobi coordinates
{parent=Jacobi coordinates for three particles}

For Jacobi coordinates $\mathbf a,\mathbf b,\mathbf c$ and their corresponding reduced masses $\alpha,\beta,\gamma$, total angular momentum is
$$
\mathbf L
=\alpha\mathbf a\times\dot{\mathbf a}
+\beta\mathbf b\times\dot{\mathbf b}
+\gamma\mathbf c\times\dot{\mathbf c}.
$$

= Collective rotation and gap coordinates on a circle
{title2=$\Phi$}
{parent=Center-of-mass reduction}

For three ordered angular positions $q_1,q_2,q_3$, define consecutive gaps
$$
\alpha=q_2-q_1,
\qquad
\beta=q_3-q_2,
\qquad
\gamma=2\pi-q_3+q_1
$$
and the collective angle $\Phi=(q_1+q_2+q_3)/3$. Then $\alpha+\beta+\gamma=2\pi$. For three equal masses on a circle of radius $r$,
$$
T=\frac{3mr^2}{2}\dot\Phi^2
+\frac{mr^2}{3}
\left(\dot\alpha^2+\dot\alpha\dot\beta+\dot\beta^2\right).
$$
Thus the <ignorable coordinate> $\Phi$ decouples from the relative gap dynamics whenever the potential depends only on the gaps.

= Reduced mass
{title2=$\mu$}
{parent=Center of mass}
{wiki}

The <reduced mass> is the effective <mass> for the <two-particle relative coordinate> of two bodies with constant positive <masses> $m_1,m_2$:
$$
\mu=\frac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2},\qquad \frac1\mu=\frac1{m_1}+\frac1{m_2}.
$$
Both bodies can respond to their mutual <force>, so their relative <acceleration> is greater than if one were fixed. Consequently $0<\mu<\min(m_1,m_2)$. This explains why using either individual <mass>, or their sum, generally gives the wrong internal <oscillation> rate. The same <reduced mass> describes a <two-body problem>, a <free two-mass spring>, and the approximate <molecular vibration> of a <diatomic molecule>.<Newtonian Dynamics by Richard Fitzpatrick>[Reduced Mass, equations 322–328: equal-opposite forces, relative acceleration and reduced mass]{ref}

For equal <masses> $m_1=m_2=m$, $\mu=m/2$. Two $1\,\mathrm{kg}$ bodies therefore have $\mu=0.5\,\mathrm{kg}$: in their stationary <centre of mass> frame a relative <speed> $2\,\mathrm{m/s}$ means individual <speeds> $1\,\mathrm{m/s}$ and total <kinetic energy> $1\,\mathrm J$. With $m_1=1\,\mathrm{kg}$ and $m_2=100\,\mathrm{kg}$, instead $\mu=100/101\,\mathrm{kg}\approx0.990099\,\mathrm{kg}$. At the same relative <speed>, the individual <speeds> are $200/101$ and $2/101\,\mathrm{m/s}$, and the total <kinetic energy> is $200/101\,\mathrm J$. These are calculations for stipulated <masses> and <velocities>.

For fixed $m_1$ and $q=m_2/m_1>0$, $\mu/m_1=q/(1+q)$. The heavy-partner limit $q\to\infty$ gives $\mu\to m_1$ from below; the relative error incurred by replacing $\mu$ with $m_1$ is $(m_1-\mu)/\mu=1/q$. At $q=100$ this is $1\%$. Treating the heavy body as fixed is thus a controlled approximation to internal motion, rather than an exact consequence of its being heavier.

= Two-particle relative coordinate
{parent=Reduced mass}
{title2=$\mathbf r=\mathbf r_2-\mathbf r_1$}

Choose <positions> in one <inertial frame> and let $M=m_1+m_2$. The <centre of mass> and <two-particle relative coordinate> are
$$
\mathbf R=\frac{m_1\mathbf r_1+m_2\mathbf r_2}{M},\qquad \mathbf r=\mathbf r_2-\mathbf r_1,\qquad
\mathbf r_1=\mathbf R-\frac{m_2}{M}\mathbf r,\quad \mathbf r_2=\mathbf R+\frac{m_1}{M}\mathbf r.
$$
The lighter body is farther from the <centre of mass>. Reconstructing both <positions> from these equations is part of solving the <two-body problem>; $\mathbf r$ alone describes their separation, not either body's laboratory <position>.<Newtonian Dynamics by Richard Fitzpatrick>[Reduced Mass, equations 324–326: centre and reconstruction of both particle positions]{ref}

\Image[reduced-mass-center-of-mass-relative-motion.png]
{id=image-reduced-mass-center-of-mass-relative-motion}
{title=<Centre of mass> translation and internal <two-particle relative coordinate> for a pair with mass ratio three}
{description=Original illustrative <free two-mass spring> model, with $m_2=3m_1$, equilibrium separation $\ell$, relative extension $s/\ell=0.3\cos\tau$, $\tau=\omega t$, and $R/\ell=0.08\tau$. The right plot subtracts both the translating <centre of mass> and each body's equilibrium offset. It shows internal <displacements>, not full distances from the <centre of mass>.}

Substitute the reconstructed <velocities> into the <kinetic energy>. The result is the exact two-particle instance of <kinetic-energy decomposition about the centre of mass>:
$$
T=\frac12m_1|\dot{\mathbf r}_1|^2+\frac12m_2|\dot{\mathbf r}_2|^2
=\frac12M|\dot{\mathbf R}|^2+\frac12\mu|\dot{\mathbf r}|^2.
$$
The mixed <dot product> has coefficient $-m_1m_2/M+m_2m_1/M=0$; the coefficient of $|\dot{\mathbf r}|^2$ is $(m_1m_2^2+m_2m_1^2)/M^2=\mu$. This algebra needs constant positive <masses> but no particular <force> law. In the stationary <centre of mass> frame, the fraction of internal <kinetic energy> carried by body 1 is $m_2/M$, and by body 2 is $m_1/M$: the lighter body carries more.

For a closed pair with no external <forces>, write $\mathbf F$ for the <force> on body 2 and $-\mathbf F$ for that on body 1, as required by <Newton's third law>. Adding and subtracting their <Newton's second law> equations yields
$$
M\ddot{\mathbf R}=\mathbf0,\qquad
\ddot{\mathbf r}=\left(\frac1{m_2}+\frac1{m_1}\right)\mathbf F,\qquad
\mu\ddot{\mathbf r}=\mathbf F.
$$
Thus the <centre of mass> moves uniformly while the <two-particle relative coordinate> behaves like one body of <reduced mass>. Equal-opposite internal <forces> suffice for these equations. For an internal <central force>, $\mathbf F=f(r)\widehat{\mathbf r}$ with $r=|\mathbf r|>0$, the internal <torque> vanishes and <conservation of angular momentum> gives constant $\mathbf L_{\rm rel}=\mu\mathbf r\times\dot{\mathbf r}$. If the <central force> comes from a time-independent <potential energy> $U(r)$, then $f(r)=-dU/dr$ and the internal <mechanical energy> $E_{\rm rel}=\mu|\dot{\mathbf r}|^2/2+U(r)$ is conserved. The <kinetic energy> split alone does not establish either conservation law.

= Free two-mass spring
{parent=Reduced mass}
{title2=$\omega=\sqrt{k/\mu}$}

Take two <point masses> joined by a massless <spring> of <spring constant> $k>0$ and equilibrium length $\ell>0$. Assume one-dimensional motion along the <spring>, no external <force>, no <damping>, and an extension $s=x_2-x_1-\ell$ satisfying <Hooke's law>; keep $x_2>x_1$. For a real <spring> this is a model within its elastic range. Its <potential energy> is $U=ks^2/2$, so
$$
\mu\ddot s=-ks,\qquad s(t)=A\cos(\omega t+\phi),\qquad
\omega=\sqrt{\frac{k}{\mu}}=\sqrt{k\left(\frac1{m_1}+\frac1{m_2}\right)},\qquad \nu=\frac\omega{2\pi}.
$$
Here $\omega$ is <angular frequency> and $\nu$ is <frequency>. Choose $|A|<\ell$ so the model bodies do not cross. The internal <normal mode> has <displacements> $u_1=-m_2s/M$ and $u_2=m_1s/M$ about equilibrium offsets from the <centre of mass>; its <amplitude> ratio is $|u_1|/|u_2|=m_2/m_1$. The other <normal mode> is collective translation with zero <frequency> and no <spring> extension. This free pair has different boundary conditions from <normal modes of two equal masses between three springs>.

For $k=10\,\mathrm{N/m}$ and two $1\,\mathrm{kg}$ <masses>, $\omega=\sqrt{20}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\approx4.47214\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and $\nu\approx0.711763\,\mathrm{Hz}$. Replacing the second <mass> by $100\,\mathrm{kg}$ gives $\omega=\sqrt{10.1}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\approx3.17805\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and $\nu\approx0.505802\,\mathrm{Hz}$. Fixing the second endpoint instead would give $\sqrt{10}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$: the free heavy partner still moves. The free pair's <angular frequency> exceeds that fixed-end limit by the factor $\sqrt{1+1/q}$, about $1.004988$ at $q=100$.

\Image[reduced-mass-ratio-spring-frequency.png]
{id=image-reduced-mass-ratio-spring-frequency}
{title=<Reduced mass> and <free two-mass spring> <angular frequency> as the partner becomes heavier}
{description=Original dimensionless model with $m_1$ and $k$ held fixed, $q=m_2/m_1>0$, $\mu/m_1=q/(1+q)$ and $\omega/\sqrt{k/m_1}=\sqrt{1+1/q}$. The marks at $q=1$ and $q=100$ correspond to the computed examples; curves are predictions, not measured data.}

= Isotope scaling of diatomic vibration
{parent=Reduced mass}
{title2=$\nu'/\nu\simeq\sqrt{\mu/\mu'}$}

Near a stable equilibrium <bond length> $r_e$, a <Taylor expansion> of a <diatomic molecule>'s <potential energy> gives $U(r)\simeq U(r_e)+k(r-r_e)^2/2$, with $k=U''(r_e)>0$. Treat the internal stretch as a <harmonic oscillator> of <reduced mass>, initially omitting <molecular rotation>. If replacing an <isotope> leaves this local <potential energy> curvature unchanged, then
$$
\frac{\nu'}\nu\simeq\sqrt{\frac\mu{\mu'}},\qquad
\widetilde\nu_{\rm harm}=\frac1{2\pi c}\sqrt{\frac{k}{\mu}}.
$$
Here $c$ is the <speed of light> and $\widetilde\nu_{\rm harm}$ a <spectroscopic wavenumber>; use consistent length units for $c$ and $\widetilde\nu_{\rm harm}$. The <quantum harmonic oscillator> gives adjacent <energy eigenvalues> separated by $h\nu$, with $h$ the <Planck constant>. Consequently a vibrational <photon absorption> can probe the same <frequency> scale, subject to <electric-dipole selection rules>.

For an explicitly approximate <isotope> example, assign the <atomic masses> of $^{1}\mathrm H$, $^{2}\mathrm H$, $^{35}\mathrm{Cl}$, and $^{37}\mathrm{Cl}$ the mass-number values $1u,2u,35u,37u$, respectively, with $u$ the <unified atomic mass unit>. These are stipulated approximations to individual <atomic masses>, not measured <masses> of <molecules> or natural-abundance averages. They give
$$
\mu_{\mathrm H^{35}\mathrm{Cl}}=\frac{35}{36}u,\quad
\mu_{\mathrm D^{35}\mathrm{Cl}}=\frac{70}{37}u,\quad
\frac{\nu_{\mathrm D^{35}\mathrm{Cl}}}{\nu_{\mathrm H^{35}\mathrm{Cl}}}\simeq\sqrt{\frac{37}{72}}\approx0.716860,
\qquad
\frac{\nu_{\mathrm H^{37}\mathrm{Cl}}}{\nu_{\mathrm H^{35}\mathrm{Cl}}}\simeq\sqrt{\frac{665}{666}}\approx0.999249.
$$
The symbol $\mathrm D$ means <deuterium>, $^{2}\mathrm H$. Doubling the light partner's approximate <atomic mass> therefore produces a large predicted shift, whereas changing the already heavy <chlorine> partner by two mass units produces a much smaller one. A quantitative comparison with <infrared spectroscopy> requires actual <isotope>-specific <atomic masses>, fitted band parameters, and uncertainties, rather than identifying any arbitrary peak with $\widetilde\nu_{\rm harm}$.

= HCl and DCl vibrational isotope experiment
{c}
{parent=Isotope scaling of diatomic vibration}

An actual <infrared spectroscopy> route is to compare gaseous <hydrogen chloride> containing ordinary <hydrogen> with its <deuterium> counterpart. A documented teaching experiment uses a stopcock-equipped gas cell with potassium bromide windows and a Nicolet Avatar instrument for <Fourier transform spectroscopy>. A nitrogen-purged flask and drying tube supply the mixture; the sample also contains sulfur dioxide. An empty sample-chamber background precedes the filled-cell scan. The <absorbance> trace and peak-picked <spectroscopic wavenumbers> are the readout. The analysis assigns and indexes $^{1}\mathrm H^{35}\mathrm{Cl}$ and $^{2}\mathrm H^{35}\mathrm{Cl}$ transitions separately and propagates transition uncertainties. The instructions distinguish resolvable <chlorine> <isotope> peaks from adequate precision for the intended parameter analysis. <Vibrational-Rotational Spectroscopy of HCl by James M. McCormick>[McCormick, Introduction; Experimental, Laboratory Component, Figures 1–2 and background/peak-picking paragraphs; Analysis and Discussion, first three paragraphs]{ref}

A separate published teaching dataset reports a Nicolet Nexus 670 instrument, a $10\,\mathrm{cm}$ gas cell at $20\,\mathrm{Torr}$, and <chlorine> <isotope> peaks resolved to baseline. These apparatus details describe that dataset, not the preceding experiment. <HCl and DCl IR absorption spectra by Jonathan Gutow>[Gutow, HCl and DCl IR absorption spectra, dataset description: instrument, path length, pressure and resolved chlorine isotope peaks]{ref}

The inference connects several models. Fit correctly assigned transitions to separate <molecular rotation> from <molecular vibration>, then compare the inferred vibrational scales using <isotope scaling of diatomic vibration>. Agreement supports the approximate separation between a nearly unchanged <chemical bond> stiffness and a changed nuclear <mass>. It does not make a real <molecule> an exact classical <spring>: <anharmonicity> shifts the fundamental transition away from the local <harmonic oscillator> scale, and coupling to <molecular rotation> shifts individual lines.

For an independent test, calculate the <reduced masses> from the specified <isotopes> and compare the inferred values $k=\mu(2\pi c\widetilde\nu_{\rm harm})^2$. Agreement within uncertainty tests the common-curvature assumption; choosing separate $k$ values just to fit each <frequency> would not test it. The documented background addresses instrument and ambient contributions, but does not by itself subtract window absorption, identify contaminating species, or calibrate the <spectroscopic wavenumber> scale. Those require separate controls. Finite spectral resolution, blended peaks, incorrect <isotope> assignments, and departure from the <harmonic approximation near a potential minimum> limit the comparison. No numerical measured line positions are assumed here.

= Uniform gravitational field
{parent=Classical mechanics}

In a uniform gravitational field, every freely falling center of mass has constant acceleration $-g$.

= Normal coordinate
{parent=Classical mechanics}

A normal coordinate diagonalizes the linearized kinetic and potential forms so that its motion decouples as a harmonic oscillator.

= Antisymmetric molecular vibration
{parent=Normal coordinate}

In an antisymmetric vibration of a symmetric linear triatomic molecule, the outer atoms move oppositely and the central atom does not couple to that mode.

= Virtual work
{parent=Classical mechanics}
{wiki}

= Principle of virtual work
{synonym}

Virtual work is the work of applied forces through an infinitesimal displacement compatible with the system's instantaneous constraints. Equilibrium or overdamped force balance requires the total virtual work to vanish for every admissible virtual displacement.
